An introduction to p p -adic L L -functions
Joaquín Rodrigues Jacinto, Chris Williams

Abstract
These expository notes introduce p p -adic L L -functions and the foundations of Iwasawa theory. We focus on Kubota–Leopoldt’s p p -adic analogue of the Riemann zeta function, which we describe in three different ways. We first present a measure-theoretic (analytic) p p -adic interpolation of special values of the Riemann zeta function. Next, we describe Coleman’s (arithmetic) construction via cyclotomic units. Finally, we examine Iwasawa’s (algebraic) construction via Galois modules over the Iwasawa algebra. The Iwasawa Main conjecture , now a theorem due to Mazur and Wiles, says that these constructions agree. We will state the conjecture precisely, and give a proof when p p is a Vandiver prime (which conjecturally covers every prime). Throughout, we discuss generalisations of these constructions and their connections to modern research directions in number theory.



\normalparindent
\normalparindent
\normalparindent
\normalparindent
\normalparindent
\normalparindent
\normalparindent

1
Introduction

The theory of
L
-functions, and their special values, has been central in number theory for 200 years. Their study spans from classical results, such as Gauss’s class number formula and the proof of Dirichlet’s theorem on primes in arithmetic progressions, to two major problems in mathematics: the Riemann hypothesis and Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture. They are also central in the Langlands program, a vast project connecting the fields of number theory, geometry and representation theory.

The Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture is one example of a huge network of conjectures on the special values of
L
-functions, including the Beilinson, Deligne, and Bloch–Kato conjectures. At their heart, these problems relate complex analytic information – such as the order of vanishing and special values of meromorphic functions – to arithmetic data, such as invariants attached to algebraic varieties and Galois representations. A fruitful approach to these problems has been the use of
p
-adic methods, for
p
a prime number. Naively, one might consider the complex world a ‘bad’ place to do arithmetic, as the integers are discrete in
{\mathbf{C}}
. This is not the case when one instead considers finite extensions of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
. The
p
-adic setting brings extra flexibility and methods with which to attack these open problems, including
p
-adic
L
-functions, Euler systems, and Hida and Coleman families of modular/automorphic forms.

The study of
p
-adic properties of special values of
L
-functions is generally known as
Iwasawa theory
. In these notes, we give an introduction to this subject, focusing on perhaps the most fundamental of all
L
-functions: the Riemann zeta function
\zeta(s)
. We describe what a
p
-adic
L
-function is, construct it in this setting, and then describe Iwasawa’s Main Conjecture
1
1

1

Iwasawa’s original conjecture was proved in full by Mazur and Wiles in
[
MW84
]
. However analogous conjectures, relating Selmer groups and
p
-adic
L
-functions, have been formulated in a large generality, for example for elliptic curves, modular forms, and beyond. These are also (somewhat confusingly) referred to as ‘Iwasawa Main Conjectures’, even in the special cases (such as the one we consider) where they have been proved. We discuss such generalisations in §
13.5
and Appendix
B
.
in this case. We try to anchor the theory in the context of current research activity, indicating how the various concepts we discuss have been generalised, and where the reader should turn next to learn more.

1.1
What do we cover in these notes?

We now summarise the main results we cover. In §
2
, we give a broad introduction to
p
-adic
L
-functions, with an emphasis on how one can naturally move from complex to
p
-adic
L
-functions. We make this precise in our case of interest by stating some of the main results of Part I.

Our focus for the rest of the notes is on the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function (or
p
-adic zeta function), which is the
p
-adic analogue of the Riemann zeta function. We will see three constructions of this object, each of a different flavour, and describe the connections between them.

Part I is devoted to the construction and study of an
analytic
version of the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function. This has the clearest connection to the classical complex Riemann zeta function; it is a pseudo-measure
\zeta_{p}^{\mathrm{an}}
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
that interpolates the (rational numbers)
\zeta(1-k)
for all positive integers
k

2
2

2

The precise meaning of this will be clear later. Here the word interpolation is as in Lagrange’s interpolation formula, i.e. a single object that hits certain specific values when evaluated at various points.
.

\bullet

In §
3
, we describe some basic tools and results from
p
-adic analysis needed to parse this statement, including measures/pseudo-measures, Iwasawa algebras, and their connections to power series.

\bullet

In §
4
we use the techniques developed in §
3
to prove Theorem
2.13
(see also Theorem
4.1
) on the existence of the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function
\zeta_{p}^{\mathrm{an}}
.

\bullet

In §
5
we prove that
\zeta_{p}^{\mathrm{an}}
also interpolates special values of
L
-functions of Dirichlet characters of
p
-power conductor, and construct analogues for arbitrary Dirichlet characters.

\bullet

In §
6
, we describe a result of Leopoldt, showing that the values of the
p
-adic
L
-function of a non-trivial Dirichlet character
1
are related to logarithms of cyclotomic units, establishing one of the first instances of the
p
-adic Beilinson conjectures. The (untwisted)
p
-adic zeta function has a simple pole at
s=1
; in §
7
, we prove an analogous result describing its residue.

\bullet

Finally in §
8
we discuss an approach to
p
-adic
L
-functions based on the theory of families of Eisenstein series.

In Part II, we give two more constructions of the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function:
arithmetic
and
algebraic
versions.

\bullet

In §
10
, we give an arithmetic construction. The
cyclotomic units
are special elements in cyclotomic fields. As one considers the tower
\mathbf{Q}_{p}(\mu_{p^{n}})
of cyclotomic extensions of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
, the cyclotomic units fit together into a norm-compatible tower/system. The
Coleman map
is a map that attaches a
p
-adic measure to any such tower of units. Via this process, we show that to the cyclotomic units, one can naturally attach a pseudo-measure
\zeta_{p}^{\mathrm{arith}}
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. One connection between arithmetic and analysis, fully explained in §
10
, is that
\zeta_{p}^{\mathrm{an}}=\zeta_{p}^{\mathrm{arith}}
.

\bullet

In §
11
and §
12
, we deepen the arithmetic picture, respectively stating and proving
Iwasawa’s theorem
describing the zeros of the
p
-adic zeta function via modules of cyclotomic units.

\bullet

In §
13
, we build on Iwasawa’s theorem to give an algebraic construction of the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function, as an ideal
\zeta_{p}^{\mathrm{alg}}
in the Iwasawa algebra. This ideal arises from the structure of a Galois module over the Iwasawa algebra. We also describe this module in terms of Selmer groups and discuss generalisations of the Main Conjecture.

The
Iwasawa Main Conjecture
says this ideal is generated by the analytic/arithmetic Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function, connecting the analytic, arithmetic and algebraic constructions, and ultimately connecting special complex
L
-values and Selmer groups. We state this precisely in §
13
, and prove it in the special case where
p
is a Vandiver prime.

The reader interested in taking a minimal path to the Iwasawa Main Conjecture can do so by reading the following sections: §
2.1
, §
2.3
–
2.4
, §
3.2
–
3.6
, §
4
, §
10.1
–
10.4
, §
11
, §
12
, §
13.1
–
13.4
.

In Appendix
B
, we conclude with remarks on how the analytic, arithmetic and algebraic constructions above have been generalised to other situations, each spawning a field of study in their own right. We illustrate this by giving a sketch in the case of modular forms.

Further reading

For more information and detail on Part I of these notes, the reader could consult
[
Lan90
]
. The construction of the
p
-adic zeta function we give here is based on Colmez’s beautiful lecture notes
[
Col
]
(in French).

Part II can serve as a prelude to a number of more advanced treatments, such as Rubin’s (complete) proof of the Main Conjecture using the theory of Euler systems. We must mention the book of Coates and Sujatha
[
CS06
]
, which inspired our original course, and whose aim was to present Rubin’s proof. A canonical book in the field is
[
Was97
]
, which introduces further topics in classical Iwasawa theory that there was not space to treat here. We give a flavour of such topics in Appendix
A
.

2
What is a
p
-adic
L
-function, and what should it do?

This introductory section aims to motivate the definition and study of
p
-adic
L
-functions. We start with a general discussion on complex
L
-functions and then lean slowly towards the
p
-adic world, focusing on the example of most importance to us in these lectures: the Riemann zeta function.

2.1
Classical
L
-functions

We first give some important examples of
L
-functions.

–

The
Riemann zeta function
, the most famous and fundamental of all
L
-functions, is defined by

\zeta(s)=\sum_{n\geq 1}n^{-s}=\prod_{p}(1-p^{-s})^{-1},

where the last product – an
Euler product
– runs over all prime numbers
p
and the second equality is a consequence of the unique prime factorisation of integers. The sum converges absolutely whenever
s
is a complex variable with real part greater than 1, making
\zeta
a well-defined holomorphic function in a right half-plane
\{s\in{\mathbf{C}}\,:\,\mathrm{Re}(s)>1\}
. It can be meromorphically continued to the whole complex plane, and satisfies a
functional equation
, a symmetry relating the values
\zeta(s)
and
\zeta(1-s)
.

–

Let
F
be a number field. The
zeta function of
F
is

\zeta_{F}(s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{0\neq I\subset\mathscr{O}_{F}}N(I)^{-s}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}(1-N(\mathfrak{p})^{-s})^{-1},

where the sum is over all non-zero ideals in the ring of integers, and the product is over all non-zero prime ideals of
K
. Again, this sum converges absolutely for
\mathrm{Re}(s)>1
, can be meromorphically continued to
{\mathbf{C}}
, and satisfies a functional equation relating
\zeta_{F}(s)
and
\zeta_{F}(1-s)
. The existence of the Euler product again follows from unique factorisation.

–

Let
\chi:({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}^{\times}
be a Dirichlet character. We extend
\chi
to a function
\chi:{\mathbf{Z}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
by setting
\chi(m)=\chi(m\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptN))
if
m
is prime to
N
, and
\chi(m)=0
otherwise. The
L
-function of
\chi
is

L(\chi,s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{n\geq 1}\chi(n)n^{-s}=\prod_{p}(1-\chi(p)p^{-s})^{-1}.

Yet again, the above sum defining
L(\chi,s)
converges for
\mathrm{Re}(s)>1
, admits meromorphic continuation to
{\mathbf{C}}
(analytic when
\chi
is non-trivial), and satisfies a functional equation relating the values at
s
and
1-s
.

–

Let
E/{\mathbf{Q}}
be an elliptic curve of conductor
N
. One can define an
L
-function

L(E,s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{n\geq 1}a_{n}(E)n^{-s}=\prod_{p\nmid N}(1-a_{p}(E)p^{-s}+p^{1-2s})^{-1}\prod_{p|N}L_{p}(s),

where
a_{p}(E)=p+1-\#E(\mathbf{F}_{p})
, and the
a_{n}(E)
are defined recursively from the
a_{p}(E)
. The factors
L_{p}(s)
at bad primes
p|N
are defined as
L_{p}(s)=1
(resp.
(1-p^{-s})^{-1}
, resp.
(1+p^{-s})^{-1}
) if
E
has bad additive (resp. split multiplicative, resp. non-split multiplicative) reduction at
p
. The above sum defining the function
L(E,s)
converges for
\mathrm{Re}(s)>3/2
, admits analytic continuation to
{\mathbf{C}}
, and satisfies a functional equation relating the values at
s
and
2-s
.

–

Let

f=\sum_{n\geq 1}a_{n}(f)q^{n}\in S_{k}(\Gamma_{0}(N),\omega_{f})

be a modular newform of weight
k
, level
N
and character
\omega_{f}
. The
L
-function associated to
f
is given by

L(f,s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{n\geq 1}a_{n}(f)n^{-s}=\prod_{p\nmid N}(1-a_{p}(f)p^{-s}+\omega_{f}(p)p^{k-1-2s})^{-1}\prod_{p|N}(1-a_{p}(f)p^{-s})^{-1}.

This sum converges for
\mathrm{Re}(s)>(k+1)/2
, admits analytic continuation to
{\mathbf{C}}
, and satisfies a functional equation relating the values at
s
and
k-s
. Such objects are introduced, and these results proved, in
[
DS05
, §5]
.

The above examples share common features. The ‘arithmetic’
L
-functions of most interest to us should have the following basic properties (which can, nevertheless, be extremely deep):

(1)

An Euler product converging absolutely in a right-half plane;

(2)

A meromorphic continuation to the whole complex plane;

(3)

A functional equation relating
s
and
k-s
for some
k\in{\mathbf{R}}
.

Remark 2.1
.

More generally, let
\mathscr{G}_{{\mathbf{Q}}}=\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}/{\mathbf{Q}})
denote the absolute Galois group of
{\mathbf{Q}}
and let
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{L}\,\mathscr{G}_{{\mathbf{Q}}}
be a
p
-adic Galois representation (i.e. a finite dimensional vector space over a finite extension
L
of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
equipped with a continuous linear action of
\mathscr{G}_{{\mathbf{Q}}}
). One defines the global
L
-function of
V
as a formal Euler product

L(V,s)=\prod_{\ell}L_{\ell}(V,s)

of local factors. For
\ell\neq p
a rational prime, the local factor at
\ell
is defined as

L_{\ell}(V,s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\det(\mathrm{Id}-\mathrm{Frob}_{\ell}^{-1}\ell^{-s}|V^{I_{\ell}})^{-1},

where
\mathrm{Frob}_{\ell}
denotes the arithmetic Frobenius at
\ell
, and
I_{\ell}
denotes the inertia group at
\ell
(all described in
[
Ser98
, §I]
). At
p
, defining the local factor is considerably more complicated, requiring
p
-adic Hodge theory, as described in
[
Ber02
,
Ben22
]
. In this case, one defines

L_{p}(V,s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\det(\mathrm{Id}-\varphi^{-1}p^{-s}|\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V))^{-1},

where
\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)
denotes the crystalline module of
V|_{\mathscr{G}_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}}
, equipped with a crystalline Frobenius denoted by
\varphi
.

When
V
is the representation attached to an arithmetic object
3
3

3

For example, a number field, a Dirichlet character, an elliptic curve, a modular form, or much more generally – in the spirit of the Langlands program – an automorphic representation of a reductive group.
, the
L
-function of the representation is typically equal to the
L
-function attached to that object; for example, taking
V=\mathbf{Q}_{p}(\chi)
(that is,
V
is 1-dimensional, with
\mathscr{G}_{{\mathbf{Q}}}
acting through the character
\chi
via class field theory), one recovers the Dirichlet
L
-functions described above. See
[
Bel09
]
for further introductions to these topics.

2.2
Motivating questions for Iwasawa theory

2.2.1
Special values and arithmetic data

There are deep results and conjectures relating special values of
L
-functions to important arithmetic information, of which a prototypical example is the following (cf., for example,
[
Neu07
, §5]
):

Theorem 2.2

(Class number formula)
.

Let
F
be a number field with
r_{1}
real embeddings,
r_{2}
pairs of complex embeddings,
w
roots of unity, discriminant
D
, and regulator
R
. The zeta function
\zeta_{F}
has a simple pole at
s=1
with residue

\mathrm{res}_{s=1}\zeta_{F}(s)=\frac{2^{r_{1}}(2\pi)^{r_{2}}R}{w\sqrt{|D|}}h_{F},

where
h_{F}
is the class number of
F
.

On the left-hand side, we have a special value of a complex
meromorphic function, from the world of analysis. On the right-hand side, we have invariants attached to a number field, from the world of arithmetic. The class number formula thus provides a deep connection between two different fields of mathematics.

A second famous example of such a connection comes in the form of the
Birch and Swinnerton–Dyer (BSD) conjecture
. Let
E/{\mathbf{Q}}
be an elliptic curve. The set of rational points
E({\mathbf{Q}})
forms a finitely generated abelian group and, based on computer computations, Birch and Swinnerton–Dyer predicted that

\mathrm{ord}_{s=1}L(E,s)=\mathrm{rank}_{{\mathbf{Z}}}E({\mathbf{Q}}).

They also predicted a closer analogue of the class number formula: that the leading term of the
L
-function can be described in terms of arithmetic invariants attached to
E
.

Again, the left-hand side is from the world of analysis, the right-hand side is from the world of arithmetic, and this prediction is inherently surprising. The worlds are so different that the analytic
L
-function defies easy study using arithmetic properties of the elliptic curve. For example, when the conjecture was formulated, the left-hand side was not even known to exist: nobody had proved that
L(E,s)
was defined at the value
s=1
. This relies on analytic continuation of the
L
-function; such a proof would not follow for several decades, and even now the only proof we have goes through another deep connection between arithmetic and analysis, namely Wiles’ modularity theorem.

2.2.2
Iwasawa Main Conjectures

One of the goals of Iwasawa theory is to seek and prove
p
-adic analogues of BSD and its generalisations, replacing complex analysis (which is poorly suited to arithmetic) with
p
-adic analysis (where arithmetic arises naturally). For each prime
p
, there is a
p
-adic
Iwasawa Main Conjecture (IMC)
for the elliptic curve
E
, relating a
p
-adic analytic
L
-function to certain
p
-adic arithmetic invariants of
E
:

\textstyle{\framebox{complex analytic $L$-function}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
BSD
\textstyle{\framebox{arithmetic invariants of $E$}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\framebox{$p$-adic analytic $L$-function}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
IMC
\textstyle{\framebox{$p$-adic invariants of $E$}}

One has many more tools available to attack the bottom row than the top, including Euler systems,
p
-adic families and eigenvarieties,
p
-adic Hodge theory and
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules, and more. As a result, the
p
-adic conjectures are much more tractable than their complex counterparts. Indeed, whilst BSD remains open beyond low rank special cases, the IMC for elliptic curves has been proved much more widely by Skinner–Urban (see
[
SU14
]
), following work of Kato (see
[
Kat04a
]
). See also
[
FW
]
for a recent summary of known results on the IMC for elliptic curves.

2.2.3
Applications of
p
-adic methods to classical BSD

Each new
p
-adic Iwasawa Main Conjecture that is proved brings the worlds of analysis and arithmetic a little closer together. They can also bring us closer to our original goal of, for example, BSD. Indeed, the current state-of-the-art results towards BSD have arisen as consequences of Iwasawa theory.

To elaborate, let us summarise some fundamental work (
p
-adic and otherwise) on BSD. There are two natural subquestions.

(a)

We could try to prove that
\mathrm{ord}_{s=1}L(E,s)\leq\mathrm{rank}_{{\mathbf{Z}}}E({\mathbf{Q}}).
A natural approach is to try to construct enough independent rational points on the elliptic curve.
The theory of
Heegner points
is based on such an idea. A Heegner point in
E({\mathbf{Q}})
has infinite order if and only if
\mathrm{ord}_{s=1}L(E,s)=1
. This yields the above inequality in this case.

(b)

Conversely, we could try and prove that
\mathrm{ord}_{s=1}L(E,s)\geq\mathrm{rank}\ _{{\mathbf{Z}}}E({\mathbf{Q}}).
In this case we want to bound the number of rational points. One method for trying to do this uses
Euler systems
attached to
p
-adic Galois representations (see
[
Rub00
]
for a comprehensive introduction). The main application of Euler systems is in bounding certain Galois cohomology groups, known as
Selmer groups
(cf. §
13.5
), which are defined using local behaviour and can be viewed as a cohomological interpretation of the group of rational points on
E
. Indeed,
let
\Sha(E/{\mathbf{Q}})
denote the
Tate–Shafarevich group
of
E
, a torsion abelian group that is conjecturally finite.
If the subgroup
\Sha(E/{\mathbf{Q}})[p^{\infty}]\leq\Sha(E/{\mathbf{Q}})
of elements with
p
-power order is finite, then the
{\mathbf{Z}}
-rank of
E({\mathbf{Q}})
is equal to the
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-corank
4
4

4

That is, the rank of the Pontryagin dual of the
p
-Selmer group; see
[
Ski18
, §2.1.4]
.
of the
p
-Selmer group. In this case, bounding the
p
-Selmer group is equivalent to bounding
E({\mathbf{Q}})
.

The ideas above have led to special cases of the conjecture; in particular, we now know it to be true (under some assumptions) when
\mathrm{ord}_{s=1}L(E,s)\leq 1
due to work of Kolyvagin, Gross–Zagier and Murty–Murty (see
[
Kol88
]
,
[
GZ86
]
and
[
MM91
]
). More recent Iwasawa-theoretic research building on the above has led to results towards the converse
[
Ski20
]
, as well as towards the leading term formula
[
JSW17
]
.

We emphasise that whilst these methods have yielded important progress towards BSD, to date such results have been fundamentally limited to elliptic curves defined over
{\mathbf{Q}}
and of rank
\leq 1
. It is natural to try to execute such strategies in more general settings. More recently, the
p
-adic theory of
Stark–Heegner points
, a
p
-adic analogue of (a) initiated in
[
Dar01
]
, has been used with some success for elliptic curves over totally real fields. Heegner and Stark–Heegner points are beautifully summarised in
[
Dar04
]
and
[
Dar06
]
. A more recent overview of work on Heegner and Stark–Heegner points, and the relationships between them, is given in
[
BDR+22
]
. There has also been encouraging (and fundamentally
p
-adic) work on analogous questions in rank 2 and beyond; for example, see
[
DR16
,
CH22
]
.

Remark 2.3
.

The study of
p
-adic
L
-functions is intrinsic to (b), and they also feature prominently in the
p
-adic analogues of (a). Mazur, Tate and Teitelbaum formulated a
p
-adic BSD conjecture (see
[
MTT86
]
and also
[
Col04
]
), which relates the order of vanishing of a
p
-adic
L
-function at
s=1
to the rank of the rational points of the elliptic curve, and expresses its principal coefficient in terms of arithmetic data in a manner analogous to the classical BSD conjecture (replacing the complex regulator by a
p
-adic regulator).

For an elliptic curve over
{\mathbf{Q}}
of analytic rank
0
, we know that the order of vanishing of its attached
p
-adic
L
-function is always 0 or 1. The possible extra zero, discussed in
[
MTT86
,
GS93
]
, is known as a
trivial zero
of the
p
-adic
L
-function and is well understood in terms of local data attached to
E
at
p
. If the Tate–Shafarevich group
\Sha(E/{\mathbf{Q}})
is finite, the
p
-adic and classical BSD conjectures are equivalent in this case.

Under the assumption of the non-degeneration of the
p
-adic height pairing, the
p
-adic IMC for elliptic curves implies the
p
-adic BSD conjecture (see
[
Sch85
]
).

2.2.4
The IMC for the Riemann zeta function

We mention the elliptic curve case only to motivate the study of
p
-adic
L
-functions and Iwasawa theory. In these notes, we will focus on a simpler example of the above picture, namely the Main Conjecture for the
p
-adic Riemann zeta function, as formulated by Iwasawa himself. Here the picture above is essentially complete; the full IMC is known for any prime
p
(thanks to
[
MW84
]
for
p
odd, and
[
Wil90
]
for
p=2
). We will (for odd
p
) construct the
p
-adic analogue of the zeta function on the way to stating the Main Conjecture, which we will prove for a special case.

2.3
The Riemann zeta function

Since the Riemann zeta function will be a central player in the rest of these notes, we take a brief detour to describe some of the classical theory surrounding it. We start with the following general result.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
f:{\mathbf{R}}_{\geq 0}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{R}}
be a rapidly decreasing
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}
-function (i.e. such that
f
and all of its derivatives
f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime},\ldots
decay exponentially at infinity). Let

(2.1)

\Gamma(s)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}t^{s-1}dt.

be the usual Gamma function. The function

L(f,s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}f(t)t^{s-1}dt,\hskip 12.0pts\in{\mathbf{C}},

which converges to a holomorphic function for Re
(s)>0
, has an analytic continuation to the whole complex plane, and

L(f,-n)=(-1)^{n}\frac{d^{n}}{dt^{n}}f(0).

We call
L(f,s)
the
Mellin transform
of
f
.

Proof.

To show analytic continuation, we claim that when Re
(s)>1
, we have

L(f,s)=-L(f^{\prime},s+1),

where
f^{\prime}=df/dt
. This is an exercise in integration by parts, using the identity
\Gamma(s)=(s-1)\Gamma(s-1)
, and gives the analytic continuation to all of
{\mathbf{C}}
by iteration. Finally, iterating the same identity
n+1
times shows that

\displaystyle L(f,-n)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}L(f^{(n+1)},1)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}\int_{0}^{\infty}f^{(n+1)}(t)dt=(-1)^{n}f^{(n)}(0)

from the fundamental theorem of calculus, giving the result.
∎

We would like to use the Mellin transform to recover the Riemann zeta function and its properties. For this, we pick a specific choice of
f
, namely, we let

f(t)=\frac{t}{e^{t}-1}=\sum_{n\geq 0}B_{n}\frac{t^{n}}{n!},

the generating function for the Bernoulli numbers
B_{n}
.

Remark 2.5
.

The Bernoulli numbers are highly combinatorial, and satisfy recurrence relations that ensure they are
rational
numbers; for example, the first few are

B_{0}=1,B_{1}=-\frac{1}{2},B_{2}=\frac{1}{6},B_{3}=0,B_{4}=-\frac{1}{30},...

For
k\geq 3
odd,
B_{k}=0
.

We want to plug this function into Theorem
2.4
, and for this, we require
5
5

5

We thank Keith Conrad for pointing out this proof.
:

Lemma 2.6
.

The function
f(t)
and all of its derivatives decay exponentially at infinity.

Proof.

For
t>0
, we may expand
f(t)
as a geometric series

\displaystyle f(t)

\displaystyle=t(e^{-t}+e^{-2t}+e^{-3t}+\cdots)=:tF(t).

Note that
f^{\prime}(t)=F(t)+tF^{\prime}(t)
, and
f^{\prime\prime}(t)=2F^{\prime}(t)+tF^{\prime\prime}(t)
; arguing inductively we see

\displaystyle f^{(n)}(t)

\displaystyle=nF^{(n-1)}(t)+tF^{(n)}(t)

\displaystyle=n(-1)^{n-1}(e^{-t}+2^{n-1}e^{-2t}+3^{n-1}e^{-3t}+\cdots)+(-1)^{n}t(e^{-t}+2^{n}e^{-2t}+3^{n}e^{-3t}+\cdots)

\displaystyle\sim(-1)^{n}te^{-t}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{ as }t\to\infty.

This decays exponentially.
∎

Lemma 2.7
.

For the choice of
f
as above, we have

(s-1)\zeta(s)=L(f,s-1).

Proof.

Substituting
t
for
nt
and rearranging in Equation (
2.1
) defining
\Gamma(s)
, we obtain

n^{-s}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-nt}t^{s-1}dt.

Now, when
\mathrm{Re}(s)
is sufficiently large, we can write

\zeta(s)=\sum_{n\geq 1}n^{-s}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\sum_{n\geq 1}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-nt}t^{s-1}dt=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sum_{n\geq 1}e^{-nt}\bigg)t\cdot t^{s-2}dt,

and the result now follows from the identity

\frac{1}{e^{t}-1}=\sum_{n\geq 1}e^{-nt}.\qed

From the theorem above, we immediately obtain:

Corollary 2.8
.

For
n\geq 0
, we have

\zeta(-n)=-\frac{B_{n+1}}{n+1}.

In particular,
\zeta(-n)\in{\mathbf{Q}}
for
n\geq 0
, and
\zeta(-n)=0
if
n\geq 2
is even.

2.4
p
-adic
L
-functions

As explained in the introduction,
p
-adic
L
-functions are excellent tools to study special values of
L
-functions.
In this section, we explain what a
p
-adic
L
-function is and the properties it should satisfy.

2.4.1
p
-adic
L
-functions, a first idea

The complex
\zeta
-function is a complex analytic function

\zeta:{\mathbf{C}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{C}}

which is rational at negative integers. Since
{\mathbf{Z}}
is a common subset of both
{\mathbf{C}}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{p}\subseteq\mathbf{C}_{p}
, it is natural to ask if there exists a function

\zeta_{p}:\mathbf{Z}_{p}\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}_{p}

that is ‘
p
-adic analytic’ (in some sense to be defined) and which agrees with the complex
L
-function at negative integers in the sense that

(2.2)

\zeta_{p}(1-n)=(*)\cdot\zeta(1-n),

for some explicit factor
(*)
. We would say that such a function ‘
p
-adically interpolates the special values of
\zeta(s)
’. Ideally, one would like these properties to uniquely characterise
\zeta_{p}
.

2.4.2
Ideles, measures and Tate’s thesis

In practice, there is no
single
analytic function on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
that interpolates all of the special values
6
6

6

Rather, there are
p-1
different analytic functions
\zeta_{p,1},\hskip 1.0pt...,\hskip 1.0pt\zeta_{p,p-1}
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, and
\zeta_{p,i}
interpolates only the values
\zeta(1-k)
for which
k\equiv i\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp-1)
.
, as we shall explain in Section
5.3
. Instead, a better way of thinking about
L
-functions is to use a viewpoint initiated by Tate in his thesis
[
Tat50
]
(and later independently by Iwasawa; see
[
Iwa52
]
). This viewpoint sees
L
-functions as
measures on ideles
, and allows one to package together
all
Dirichlet
L
-functions, including the Riemann zeta function, into a single object. We will give a brief account of the classical theory here, but for fuller accounts, one should consult the references above.

We begin with some observations on characters.

Proposition 2.9
.

The following assertions hold.

(i)

There is an identification between Dirichlet characters
\chi
and continuous characters

\chi:\prod_{\ell\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{prime}}{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{C}}^{\times},

where the source is equipped with the product of the
\ell
-adic topologies.

(ii)

There is an identification of
{\mathbf{C}}
with the space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}({\mathbf{R}}_{>0},{\mathbf{C}}^{\times})
of continuous multiplicative characters by sending
s
to
x\mapsto x^{s}
.

In particular, each pair
(\chi,s)
, where
\chi
is a Dirichlet character and
s\in{\mathbf{C}}
, corresponds to a (unique) continuous character

\displaystyle\kappa_{\chi,s}:{\mathbf{R}}_{>0}\times\prod_{\ell\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{prime}}{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathbf{C}}^{\times}

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\longmapsto x^{s}\chi(y),

where we equip the source with the product topology, and all continuous characters on this group are of this form.

Proof.

First, observe that any Dirichlet character
\chi:({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{C}}^{\times}
induces naturally a character

\chi:\prod_{\ell\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{prime}}{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{C}}^{\times}.

Indeed, suppose first that
N=\ell^{n}
is a power of some prime
\ell
, with
n\geq 1
. As
({\mathbf{Z}}/\ell^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}\cong{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times}/(1+\ell^{n}{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell})
, we can lift
\chi
from
({\mathbf{Z}}/\ell^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}
to a function on
{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times}
.
The case for general
N
follows from the Chinese remainder theorem. Conversely, any continuous character
\chi:\prod_{\ell\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{prime}}{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\times}
must factor through a finite quotient
({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}
of
\prod_{\ell\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{prime}}{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times}
for some large enough
N
. Indeed, the image of a sufficiently small open neighbourhood

U_{N}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Big\{x\in\prod_{\ell}{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times}\;:\;x\equiv 1\text{ (mod $N$)}\Big\}

of
1
is contained in
\{z\in{\mathbf{C}}:|z-1|<1\}
. This image is a compact subgroup, but the only compact subgroup of the latter set is
\{1\}
. Hence
\chi
is trivial on
U_{N}
, and factors through
(\prod_{\ell}{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times})/U_{N}=({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}
, inducing a Dirichlet character. These two procedures are inverse to each other, showing the first point.

We now prove the second point. For
s\in{\mathbf{C}}
, the function
x\mapsto x^{s}
is visibly a continuous character on
{\mathbf{R}}_{>0}
. We want to show that all such characters are of this form. After taking a logarithm, this is equivalent to showing that all continuous homomorphisms (of additive groups)
g:{\mathbf{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
are of the form
g(x)=xg(1)
, which is shown by directly computing the values of
g
on
{\mathbf{Q}}
and extending by continuity.
∎

The product space appearing in Proposition
2.9
is more usually written using ideles.

Definition 2.10
.

Define the
ideles

\mathbf{A}^{\times}
of
{\mathbf{Q}}
to be

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{\times}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}_{{\mathbf{Q}}}^{\times}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}={\mathbf{R}}^{\times}\times\sideset{}{{}^{\prime}}{\prod}_{\ell\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{prime}}{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times}

\displaystyle=\big\{(x_{{\mathbf{R}}},x_{2},x_{3},x_{5},...):x_{\ell}\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times}\text{ for all but finitely many }\ell\big\}.

(The prime on the product denotes
restricted product
, which indicates the almost everywhere integral property in the definition.) The ideles form a topological ring equipped with the restricted product topology, namely the topology with a basis of open neighbourhoods given by subsets of the form
U\times\prod_{\ell}U_{\ell}
, with
U\subseteq{\mathbf{R}}^{\times}
,
U_{\ell}\subseteq{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times}
open subsets such that
U_{\ell}={\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times}
for almost all primes
\ell
. The units
{\mathbf{Q}}^{\times}
embed diagonally in
\mathbf{A}^{\times}
(that is, via
x\mapsto(x,x,x,...)
) and we have:

Proposition 2.11

(Strong approximation)
.

There is a topological isomorphism

{\mathbf{Q}}^{\times}\backslash\mathbf{A}^{\times}\cong{\mathbf{R}}_{>0}\times\prod_{\ell\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{prime}}{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times}.

Hence all continuous characters

{\mathbf{Q}}^{\times}\backslash\mathbf{A}^{\times}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{C}}^{\times}

are of the form
\kappa_{\chi,s}
as above, where
\chi
is a Dirichlet character and
s\in{\mathbf{C}}
.

Proof.

See
[
GH11
, Proposition I.4.6]
.
∎

Remark 2.12
.

The space
{\mathbf{Q}}^{\times}\backslash\mathbf{A}^{\times}
is the
idele class group
of
{\mathbf{Q}}
, and features prominently in the idelic formulation of Class Field Theory.

By the identification of
{\mathbf{C}}
with
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}({\mathbf{R}}_{>0},{\mathbf{C}}^{\times})
one can view
\zeta
as a function

\displaystyle\zeta:\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}({\mathbf{R}}_{>0},{\mathbf{C}}^{\times})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathbf{C}}

\displaystyle\big[x\mapsto x^{s}\big]

\displaystyle\longmapsto\zeta(s).

But now we can consider
all
complex Dirichlet
L
-functions
at once
via the function

(2.3)

\displaystyle L:\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}({\mathbf{Q}}^{\times}\backslash\mathbf{A}^{\times},{\mathbf{C}}^{\times})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathbf{C}}

\displaystyle\kappa_{\chi,s}

\displaystyle\longmapsto L(\chi,s).

In the framework of Tate, this function
L
can be viewed as integrating
\kappa_{\chi,s}
against the
Haar measure
on
{\mathbf{Q}}^{\times}\backslash\mathbf{A}^{\times}
. In Tate’s thesis, he showed properties such as the analytic continuation and functional equations of Dirichlet
L
-functions using harmonic analysis on measures. Indeed, the idelic formulation gives a beautiful conceptual explanation for the appearance of the
\Gamma
-functions and powers of
2\pi i
in the functional equation of the zeta function; these factors form the ‘Euler factor at the archimedean place’. The measure-theoretic perspective has proven to be a powerful method of defining and studying automorphic
L
-functions in wide generality.

2.4.3
p
-adic
L
-functions via measures

To obtain a
p
-adic version of this picture, by analogy with (
2.3
), a natural thing to do is to consider
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}({\mathbf{Q}}^{\times}\backslash\mathbf{A}^{\times},\mathbf{C}_{p}^{\times})
(that is, replacing
{\mathbf{C}}
with
\mathbf{C}_{p}
). Again, by strong approximation, an element of this space corresponds to a
\mathbf{C}_{p}
-valued character on
{\mathbf{R}}_{>0}\times\prod{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times}
. Since
{\mathbf{R}}_{>0}
is connected and
\mathbf{C}_{p}
is totally disconnected, the restriction of any such character to
{\mathbf{R}}_{>0}
is trivial. Also using topological arguments we find that the restriction to
\prod_{\ell\neq p}{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times}
factors through a finite quotient, so gives rise to some Dirichlet character of conductor prime to
p
. This leaves the restriction to
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, i.e.
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times},\mathbf{C}_{p}^{\times})
, which is by far the most interesting part.

In particular, in the spirit of (
2.2
), we look for a ‘
p
-adic analytic’ function

\zeta_{p}:\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times},\mathbf{C}_{p}^{\times})\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}_{p}

which ‘sees’ the special values of
\zeta(s)
in the sense that that

\zeta_{p}(x\mapsto x^{k})=(*)\cdot\zeta(1-k),\hskip 12.0ptk\geq 1

for an explicit factor
(*)
.

In §
3
, we will develop the appropriate notion of ‘
p
-adic analytic’ object in this setting:
p
-adic measures
7
7

7

It is not immediately obvious why we describe these as analytic, but in the background such objects can be described in terms of
rigid analysis
, a
p
-adic analogue of complex analysis. Whilst we will not explicitly use rigid analysis, the connection is described precisely in Remark
3.47
.
In this language, measures correspond to analytic functions, and pseudo-measures to meromorphic functions with at worst simple poles.
(and pseudo-measures) on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. Then in §
4
we will prove:

Theorem 2.13

(Kubota-Leopoldt, Iwasawa)
.

There exists a unique pseudo-measure
\zeta_{p}
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
such that, for all
k>0
,

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k}\cdot\zeta_{p}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\zeta_{p}(x\mapsto x^{k})=\big(1-p^{k-1}\big)\zeta(1-k).

Remark 2.14
.

Note that the factor
(1-p^{k-1})
is the inverse of the factor at the prime
p
of the product formula
\zeta(s)=\prod_{\ell}(1-\ell^{-s})^{-1}
, evaluated at
s=1-k
. So, even though the Euler product does not converge at
s=1-k
, Theorem
2.13
morally says that, removing the factor at
p
from the Euler product formula, one can
p
-adically interpolate the Riemann zeta function. This is a general phenomenon appearing in the theory of
p
-adic
L
-functions.

From the pseudo-measure
\zeta_{p}
, we can build
p-1
(meromorphic) functions on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, each satisfying a partial version of (
2.2
). If we stick with the measure-theoretic approach, however, we have much more. The following result illustrates this power. In constructing
\zeta_{p}
, we use only values of
\zeta(s)
, without referring to Dirichlet characters at all. However, we also have:

Theorem 2.15
.

Let
\chi
be a Dirichlet character of conductor
p^{n}
,
n\geq 0
, viewed as a locally constant character on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}

8
8

8

That is, a character on
{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}
factoring through
{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}/(1+p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}}_{p})
for some large enough
n
.
. Then, for all
k>0
,

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi(x)x^{k}\cdot\zeta_{p}=\big(1-\chi(p)p^{k-1}\big)L(\chi,1-k).

Thus
\zeta_{p}
also interpolates all the negative integer values
L(\chi,-k)
for
all
Dirichlet
L
-functions of
p
-power conductor. This is very surprising, since a priori one constructs
\zeta_{p}
using only information about the untwisted special
L
-values.

To complete the picture given in §
2.4.2
, one also considers Dirichlet characters of conductor prime to
p
. Similar ideas can also be used to show:

Theorem 2.16
.

Let
D>1
be any integer coprime to
p
, and let
\eta
denote a (primitive) Dirichlet character of conductor
D
. There exists a unique measure
\zeta_{\eta}
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
with the following interpolation property: for all primitive Dirichlet characters
\chi
with conductor
p^{n}
for some
n\geq 0
, we have, for all
k>0
,

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi(x)x^{k}\cdot\zeta_{\eta}=\big(1-\chi\eta(p)p^{k-1}\big)L(\chi\eta,1-k).

Remark 2.17
.

Let
({\mathbf{Z}}/D{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times\wedge}
denote the space of characters on
({\mathbf{Z}}/D{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}
. The measures given by Theorem
2.16
can be seen as functions on
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}({{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}},\mathbf{C}_{p}^{\times})\times({\mathbf{Z}}/D{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times\wedge}
and they are compatible with respect to the natural maps
({\mathbf{Z}}/E{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times\wedge}\to({\mathbf{Z}}/D{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times\wedge}
for
E|D
. This shows that they define a function on

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}({{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}},\mathbf{C}_{p}^{\times})\times\varinjlim_{(D,p)=1}\big({\mathbf{Z}}/D{\mathbf{Z}}\big)^{\times\wedge}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}({{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}},\mathbf{C}_{p}^{\times})\times\big(\prod_{\ell\neq p}{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times}\big)^{\wedge}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}({\mathbf{Q}}^{\times}\backslash\mathbf{A}^{\times},\mathbf{C}_{p}^{\times}).

In other words, they give a measure on the idele class group of
{\mathbf{Q}}
.

Remark 2.18
.

Note that if
k\equiv\ell\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp^{m-1}(p-1))
, then
x^{k}\equiv x^{\ell}\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp^{m})
for any
x\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. In particular, for any Dirichlet character
\eta
of conductor prime to
p
, these theorems tell us that the special values of
L
-functions satisfy congruences

(1-\eta(p)p^{k-1})L(\eta,1-k)\equiv(1-\eta(p)p^{\ell-1})L(\eta,1-\ell)\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp^{m}).

For the Riemann zeta function, these are the
(generalised) Kummer congruences
, which played a role in his classification of irregular primes, and which provided significant motivation for Theorem
2.13
. This gives an alternative way of viewing
p
-adic
L
-functions: as
p
-adic analytic objects that package together systematic congruences between
L
-values.

Part I: The Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function

In this part, we give constructions of the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function and the
p
-adic
L
-functions of Dirichlet characters. In Section
3
, we introduce the necessary formalism of
p
-adic (pseudo-)measures and Iwasawa algebras, and – via Mahler transforms – their relationship with spaces of power series. This section sets up language and correspondences we will use throughout the rest of these notes.

In Section
4
, we construct a pseudo-measure on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
that interpolates the values of the Riemann zeta function at negative integers. In Section
5
, we show moreover that this pseudo-measure interpolates the values
L(\chi,-k)
for
k>0
and
\chi
any Dirichlet character of
p
-power conductor. Further, if
\eta
is a Dirichlet character of conductor prime to
p
, we construct a measure on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
that interpolates the values
L(\chi\eta,-k)
for the same range of
k
and
\chi
. In Section
5.3
we rephrase the construction in terms of analytic functions on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
via the Mellin transform. In §
6
and §
7
we describe the behaviour at
s=1
of these analytic functions, a point outside the region of interpolation. Finally, in §
8
we discuss how these results can be used to construct the
p
-adic family of Eisenstein series, a prototype for Hida and Coleman families.

3
Measures and Iwasawa algebras

In this section, we formally develop the theory of
p
-adic analysis that we will be using in the sequel. Whilst some of the results may appear a little dry in isolation, fluency in the measure-theoretic language will greatly help us simplify later calculations that would otherwise be very technical.

We start in a general setting, letting
G
be a profinite abelian group, and introducing
p
-adic measures on
G
. We then show that the space of
p
-adic measures is isomorphic to the Iwasawa algebra of
G
. Additionally, in the special case where
G=\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, we give a third perspective, showing that the Iwasawa algebra is also isomorphic to a space of power series via the Mahler transform. After developing a measure-theoretical toolkit for later use, we introduce pseudo-measures. We then conclude by discussing generalisations, including locally analytic distributions and rigid analytic functions.

Throughout, we fix a finite extension
L
of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
, with the
p
-adic valuation normalised so that
v_{p}(p)=1
; this will be the coefficient field. We write
\mathscr{O}_{L}
for its ring of integers.

3.1
Preliminaries on
p
-adic Banach spaces

We first collect some technical general definitions to anchor our discussions. This is intended only to make precise some of the notions we subsequently use, and the reader comfortable with
p
-adic Banach spaces and orthonormal bases may skip to §
3.2
. For more details, see
[
Col10a
, §I.1]
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
B
be an
L
-vector space. A
valuation
on
B
is a function
v:B\to{\mathbf{R}}\cup\{+\infty\}
such that:

(i)

v(x)=+\infty
if and only if
x=0
;

(ii)

v(x+y)\geq\mathrm{min}(v(x),\ v(y))
for all
x,y\in B
;

(iii)

and
v(\lambda x)=v_{p}(\lambda)+v(x)
for all
\lambda\in L,x\in B
.

Such a valuation induces a norm (hence a topology) on
B
.

Definition 3.2
.

An
L
-Banach space
is a complete topological
L
-vector space
B
whose topology is induced from a valuation
v
.

Definition 3.3
.

(1)

Let
I
be a set, and
\ell^{0}_{\infty}(I,L)
the set of sequences
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
in
L
that tend to 0 in the sense that for all
\varepsilon>0
, we have
|a_{i}|_{L}<\varepsilon
for all but finitely many
i
. This is naturally an
L
-Banach space with valuation
v((a_{i})_{i})=\mathrm{inf}_{i\in I}v_{p}(a_{i}).

(2)

If
B
is an
L
-Banach space, an
orthonormal basis
of
B
is a collection
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
, for some set
I
, such that we have an isometry

\displaystyle\ell^{0}_{\infty}(I,L)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow B,

\displaystyle(a_{i})_{i\in I}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}e_{i},

Remark 3.4
.

By
[
Col10a
, Prop. I.1.5]
, if
B
is an
L
-Banach space with valuation
v_{B}
, and
v_{B}(B)=v_{p}(L)
, then
B
admits an orthonormal basis.

We shall also be concerned with dual spaces. If
B
is a topological
L
-vector space, let

B^{*}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(B,L)

be its continuous linear dual. If
B
is an
L
-Banach space, there are two natural topologies on
B^{*}
.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
B
be an
L
-Banach space and
B^{*}
its continuous dual.

–

The
strong topology
is the topology induced by the natural dual valuation
v^{*}
, where

v^{*}(\mu)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{inf}_{x\in B}\left(v_{p}(\mu(x))-v(x)\right).

This is the coarsest topology such that a sequence
(\mu_{j})_{j}\subset B^{*}
converges if and only if it converges uniformly (in the usual sense of continuous functions on
B
).

–

The
weak topology
is induced by the family of semivaluations
9
9

9

That is, functions
v
that satisfy (ii) and (iii) of Definition
3.1
, but not necessarily (i).

\{v_{x}:x\in B\}
, where

v_{x}(\mu)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=v_{p}(\mu(x)).

This is the topology of pointwise convergence, the coarsest such that a sequence
(\mu_{j})_{j}\subset B^{*}
converges if and only if
\mu_{j}(x)
converges for all
x\in B
.

Remark 3.6
.

The dual
B^{*}
is complete with both of these topologies. However generally it is an
L
-Banach space only for the strong topology, whilst
B
is reflexive (canonically isomorphic to its double continuous dual) only when
B^{*}
is equipped with the weak topology.

3.2
p
-adic measures

We now return to our specific setting, and introduce the
p
-adic measures fundamental to our story. Let
G
be a profinite abelian group; the examples
G=\mathbf{Z}_{p}
or
G=\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
are of most interest to us.

Definition 3.7
.

We denote by
\mathscr{C}(G,L)
the space of continuous functions
\phi:G\to L
. We equip this space with a valuation

v_{\mathscr{C}}(\phi)=\inf_{x\in G}v_{p}(\phi(x)),\hskip 20.00003pt\phi\in\mathscr{C}(G,L),

noting this is well-defined as
G
is compact (hence
\phi
is bounded).

This valuation induces the sup norm on
\mathscr{C}(G,L)
, and endows it with the structure of an
L
-Banach space, in the sense of Definition
3.2
.

Definition 3.8
.

We define the space
\mathscr{M}(G,L)
of
L
-valued measures on
G
as the continuous linear dual
\mathscr{C}(G,L)^{*}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\rm cts}(\mathscr{C}(G,L),L)
. If
\phi\in\mathscr{C}(G,L)
and
\mu\in\mathscr{M}(G,L)
, the evaluation of
\mu
at
\phi
will be denoted by

\int_{G}\phi(x)\cdot\mu(x),

or by
\int_{G}\phi\cdot\mu
if the variable of integration is clear from the context.

We say that an element
\mu\in\mathscr{M}(G,L)
is an
\mathscr{O}_{L}
-valued measure
, and write
\mu\in\mathscr{M}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})
, if
\int_{G}\phi\cdot\mu\in\mathscr{O}_{L}
for every
\mathscr{O}_{L}
-valued function
\phi
. Since measures are continuous (or equivalently, bounded), we have
\mathscr{M}(G,L)=\mathscr{M}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{L}}L
. We will be mainly concerned with
\mathscr{O}_{L}
-valued functions and measures.

Remark 3.9
.

All parts of Definitions
3.7
and
3.8
apply identically if we replace
G
with any subset
X\subset G
equipped with the subspace topology (noting that
X
no longer need be a group).

The following simple example will be crucial in later chapters.

Example 3.10
.

For any
g\in G
, the
Dirac measure

\delta_{g}\in\mathscr{M}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})
is the linear functional ‘evaluation at
g
’, that is, the measure defined by

\displaystyle\delta_{g}:\mathscr{C}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathscr{O}_{L}

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\longmapsto\phi(g).

We will give a number of alternative descriptions of
p
-adic measures. Firstly, we make the following simplifications.

Remark 3.11
.

Let
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{lc}}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})
denote the space of locally constant functions
G\to\mathscr{O}_{L}
; this is a dense subspace of the continuous functions
\mathscr{C}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})
. Indeed, any continuous function
\phi\in\mathscr{C}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})
can be
p
-adically approximated by its locally constant truncations
\phi_{n}(x)=\sum_{a\in({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})}\phi(a)\mathbf{1}_{a+p^{n}\mathbf{Z}_{p}}(x)
, where
\mathbf{1}_{a+p^{n}\mathbf{Z}_{p}}(x)
denotes the characteristic function of
a+p^{n}\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. Let

\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{lc}}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{lc}}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})^{*}

be the continuous dual, the space of ‘locally constant measures on
G
’. We claim restriction from
\mathscr{C}
to
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{lc}}
defines a canonical isomorphism

(3.1)

\mathscr{M}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})\xrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{lc}}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L}).

To see this, we write down an inverse. Suppose
\mu^{\mathrm{lc}}\in\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{lc}}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})
, and let
\phi\in\mathscr{C}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})
. Using density, choose a sequence
\phi_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{lc}}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})
with
\phi_{n}\to\phi
and define

\int_{G}\phi\cdot\mu\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{G}\phi_{n}\cdot\mu^{\mathrm{lc}}.

By continuity, the limit is well-defined and independent of the choice of
\phi_{n}
, and hence we obtain a well defined measure
\mu\in\mathscr{M}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})
. This gives a map

\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{lc}}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})\longrightarrow\mathscr{M}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})

visibly inverse to (
3.1
).

Henceforth we drop the notation
\mu^{\mathrm{lc}}
and just write
\mu
.

Remark 3.12
.

We have an identification of
\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{lc}}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})
with the space of additive functions

(3.2)

\mu:\{\text{open compact subsets of $G$}\}\longrightarrow\mathscr{O}_{L}.

Indeed, if
\mu\in\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{lc}}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})
and
U\subset G
is an open compact set, one defines
\mu(U)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{G}\mathbf{1}_{U}(x)\cdot\mu(x)
, where
\mathbf{1}_{U}(x)
denotes the characteristic function of
U
.

Conversely, let
\mu
be such a function and let
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{lc}}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})
. We will see how to integrate
\phi
against
\mu
. As
\phi
is locally constant, there is some open subgroup
H
of
G
such that
\phi
can be viewed as a function on
G/H
. We define the integral of
\phi
against
\mu
to be

\int_{G}\phi\cdot\mu\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{[a]\in G/H}\phi(a)\mu(aH).

Combining Remarks
3.11
and
3.12
, we have an identification of
\mathscr{M}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})
with the space of additive functions on the open compact subsets of
G
.

Remark 3.13
.

On
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, we have a (real-valued) Haar measure defined so that open compact subsets of the form
a+p^{n}\mathbf{Z}_{p}
have measure
p^{-n}
. Whilst this is probably the most natural measure one might consider on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, observe that this is
not
a
p
-adic measure, as it is not
p
-adically bounded!

Example 3.14
.

For
g\in G
, the Dirac measure
\delta_{g}
from Example
3.10
corresponds to the function
\widetilde{\delta}_{g}
on open compact subsets given by

\widetilde{\delta}_{g}(X)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\text{if }g\in X\\
0&\text{if }g\notin X,\end{array}\right.

as can be seen directly from the identification above.

3.3
The Iwasawa algebra

We will now express measures in terms of algebra. As a prototype, we recall a useful fact from representation theory. If
G
is a finite abelian group, let
\mathscr{C}(G,{\mathbf{Z}})
be the space of functions
G\to{\mathbf{Z}}
, and
\mathscr{M}(G,{\mathbf{Z}})
its dual, the space of ‘continuous measures’ on
G
(when we equip
G
with the discrete topology). For any
g\in G
we have the Dirac measure
\delta_{g}\in\mathscr{M}(G,{\mathbf{Z}})
given by
\delta_{g}(\phi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\phi(g)
, as in Example
3.10
. Then recall the following classical result.

Proposition 3.15
.

If
G
is a finite abelian group, the map
[g]\mapsto\delta_{g}
induces an isomorphism between the group algebra
{\mathbf{Z}}[G]
and
\mathscr{M}(G,{\mathbf{Z}})
.

When
G
is profinite abelian, we have an analogous
p
-adic result after replacing the group algebra with its profinite completion, the Iwasawa algebra.

Proposition 3.16
.

We have an natural isomorphism

\mathscr{M}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})\cong\varprojlim_{H}\mathscr{O}_{L}[G/H],

where the limit is over all open subgroups of
G
.

Proof.

By Remark
3.11
, we have a canonical isomorphism

\mathscr{M}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})\cong\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{lc}}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{lc}}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L}),\mathscr{O}_{L}).

As any locally constant function factors through
G/H
for some open compact subgroup
H\leq G
, we also have a natural isomorphism

\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{lc}}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})\cong\varinjlim_{H}\mathscr{C}(G/H,\mathscr{O}_{L}).

We thus have

(3.3)

\mathscr{M}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\rm cts}(\varinjlim_{H}\mathscr{C}(G/H,\mathscr{O}_{L}),\mathscr{O}_{L})\cong\varprojlim_{H}\mathscr{M}(G/H,\mathscr{O}_{L}),

the final isomorphism following from compatibility of duals with limits.
As
G/H
is a finite group, by Proposition
3.15
we have that
\mathscr{M}(G/H,\mathscr{O}_{L})\cong\mathscr{O}_{L}[G/H]
, giving the result.
∎

We explicitly describe both maps in this isomorphism.

Let
\mu
be a measure, considered as an additive function as in (
3.2
), and let
H
be an open subgroup of
G
. We define an element
\lambda_{H}
of
\mathscr{O}_{L}[G/H]
by setting

\lambda_{H}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{[a]\in G/H}\mu(aH)[a].

By the additivity property of
\mu
, we see that
(\lambda_{H})_{H}\in\varprojlim\mathscr{O}_{L}[G/H]
, and this gives the map from measures to the inverse limit.

Conversely, given such an element
\lambda
of the inverse limit, write
\lambda_{H}
for its image in
\mathscr{O}_{L}[G/H]
under the natural projection. Then

\lambda_{H}=\sum_{[a]\in G/H}c_{a}[a].

We define

\mu(aH)=c_{a}.

Since the
\lambda_{H}
are compatible under projection maps, this defines an additive function on the open compact subsets of
G
, i.e. an element
\mu\in\mathscr{M}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})
.

Definition 3.17
.

We define the
Iwasawa algebra of
G
to be the profinite completion of the group algebra
\mathscr{O}_{L}[G]
, i.e.,

\Lambda(G)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\longleftarrow\\
H\end{subarray}}\mathscr{O}_{L}[G/H].

(Note that we suppress
L
from the notation.)

Remark 3.18
.

The Iwasawa algebra
\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p})
has a natural
\mathscr{O}_{L}
-algebra structure, and hence by transport of structure we obtain such a structure on
\mathscr{M}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},\mathscr{O}_{L}).
As with the classical situation for finite group rings, the algebra structure on the space of measures can be described directly via
convolution of measures
. For a general profinite abelian group
G
, given two measures
\mu,\lambda\in\mathscr{M}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})
, one defines their convolution
\mu*\lambda
to be

\int_{G}\phi\cdot(\mu*\lambda)=\int_{G}\left(\int_{G}\phi(x+y)\cdot\lambda(y)\right)\cdot\mu(x).

One checks that this does give an algebra structure and that the isomorphism above is an isomorphism of
\mathscr{O}_{L}
-algebras.

Example 3.19
.

Let
a\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, and let
\delta_{a}
be the Dirac measure on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
from Example
3.10
. Recall this corresponds to the function
\widetilde{\delta}_{a}
on open compact subsets given by
\widetilde{\delta}_{a}(a)=1
(if
a\in X
) and
\widetilde{\delta}_{a}(a)=0
(if
a\notin X
). Under the isomorphism of Proposition
3.16
,
\delta_{a}
corresponds to the projective system

([a+p^{n}\mathbf{Z}_{p}])_{n\in{\mathbf{N}}}\in\varprojlim_{n\in{\mathbf{N}}}\mathscr{O}_{L}[\mathbf{Z}_{p}/p^{n}\mathbf{Z}_{p}].

In the inverse limit this yields an element of the Iwasawa algebra that we denote
[a]
.

3.4
p
-adic analysis and Mahler transforms

So far we have given three equivalent descriptions of
p
-adic measures on a profinite abelian group
G
:

(1)

as linear functionals on
\mathscr{C}(G,L)
,

(2)

as additive functions on open compact subsets of
G
, and

(3)

as elements of the Iwasawa algebra of
G
.

In this section we specialise to
G=\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, and in this case give yet another equivalent description, via the power series ring
\mathscr{O}_{L}[\![T]\!]
.

Definition 3.20
.

For
x\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, let

\begin{pmatrix}x\\
n\end{pmatrix}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{x(x-1)\cdots(x-n+1)}{n!}\text{ for }n\geq 1,\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and }\begin{pmatrix}x\\
0\end{pmatrix}=1.

One easily checks that
x\mapsto{x\choose n}
defines an element in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},\mathbf{Z}_{p})
of valuation
v_{\mathscr{C}}\big({x\choose n}\big)=0
. The following theorem is fundamental in all that follows. It says that the functions
{x\choose n}
form an orthonormal basis for the
L
-Banach space
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
(in the sense of Definition
3.3
).

Theorem 3.21

(Mahler)
.

Let
\phi:\mathbf{Z}_{p}\rightarrow L
be a continuous function. There exists a unique expansion

\phi(x)=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n}(\phi)\begin{pmatrix}x\\
n\end{pmatrix},

where
a_{n}(\phi)\in L
and
a_{n}(\phi)\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Moreover,
v_{\mathscr{C}}(\phi)=\inf_{n\in{\mathbf{N}}}v_{p}(a_{n}(\phi))
.

Proof.

See
[
Col10a
, Théorème 1.2.3.]
.
∎

Remark 3.22
.

The coefficients
a_{n}(\phi)
are called the
Mahler coefficients
of
\phi
.
One can write down the Mahler coefficients of
\phi
very simply: we define the
discrete derivatives
of
\phi
by

\phi^{[0]}=\phi,\hskip 12.0pt\phi^{[k+1]}(x)=\phi^{[k]}(x+1)-\phi^{[k]}(x),

and then
a_{n}(\phi)=\phi^{[n]}(0).

It is natural to study a measure by looking at its values on the elements of the (orthonormal) Mahler basis. We encode these values in the following power series.

Definition 3.23
.

Let
\mu\in\mathscr{M}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},\mathscr{O}_{L})
be a
p
-adic measure on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. Define the
Mahler transform
(or
Amice transform
) of
\mu
to be

\mathscr{A}_{\mu}(T)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}(1+T)^{x}\cdot\mu(x)=\sum_{n\geq 0}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\begin{pmatrix}x\\
n\end{pmatrix}\cdot\mu\right)T^{n}\in\mathscr{O}_{L}[\![T]\!].

Example 3.24
.

Let
a\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, and recall the Dirac measure
\delta_{a}
. By definition, its Mahler transform is

\mathscr{A}_{\delta_{a}}(T)=\sum_{n\geq 0}\begin{pmatrix}a\\
n\end{pmatrix}T^{n}=(1+T)^{a}.

Before stating the main theorem concerning the Mahler transform, let us consider how it interacts with the isomorphism
\mathscr{M}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},\mathscr{O}_{L})\xrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p})
of Proposition
3.16
. As
1
is a topological generator of (the additive group)
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, and likewise
1+T
is a topological generator of
\mathscr{O}_{L}[\![T]\!]
, one may check that
[1]\mapsto(1+T)
induces an isomorphism
\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p})\xrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}\mathscr{O}_{L}[\![T]\!]
, fitting into a commutative diagram

(3.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{M}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},\mathscr{O}_{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{O}_{L}[\![T]\!]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p})}

Indeed, by continuity it suffices to check on Dirac measures. As
\delta_{a}\mapsto(1+T)^{a}
under the top arrow, and
\delta_{a}\mapsto[a]\mapsto(1+T)^{a}
under the bottom arrows, we are done.

Given the bottom two arrows in (
3.4
) are isomorphisms, the following theorem should not be surprising.

Theorem 3.25
.

The Mahler transform gives an
\mathscr{O}_{L}
-algebra isomorphism

\mathscr{M}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},\mathscr{O}_{L})\xrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}\mathscr{O}_{L}[\![T]\!].

Proof.

This is almost a tautology from the definition of orthonormal basis. By continuity and linearity, any measure
\mu\in\mathscr{M}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},\mathscr{O}_{L})
is uniquely determined by the values
\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}{x\choose n}\cdot\mu
. Indeed, let
\phi\in\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},\mathscr{O}_{L})
. By Mahler’s theorem, we can write
\phi(x)=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n}(\phi){x\choose n}
for some unique
a_{n}(\phi)\in\mathscr{O}_{L}
such that
a_{n}(\phi)\to 0
as
n\to\infty
; and then we have

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\phi\cdot\mu=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n}(\phi)\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}{x\choose n}\cdot\mu.

Conversely, given any collection of values
c_{n}\in\mathscr{O}_{L}
, defining an element
g=\sum_{n\geq 0}c_{n}T^{n}\in\mathscr{O}_{L}[\![T]\!]
, there is a unique measure
\mu_{g}
with
\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}{x\choose n}\cdot\mu_{g}=c_{n}
. Concretely, for any
\phi=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n}(\phi){x\choose n}\in\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},\mathscr{O}_{L})
as above, we define

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\phi\cdot\mu_{g}=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n}(\phi)c_{n},

which converges to an element in
\mathscr{O}_{L}
. Visibly we have
\mathscr{A}_{\mu_{g}}=g
, so this defines an inverse to the Mahler transform.
∎

Remark 3.26
.

Each space in the diagram (
3.4
) has a description as an inverse limit. For measures, this is (
3.3
); for the Iwasawa algebra, this is by definition; and for power series, we have

\mathscr{O}_{L}[\![T]\!]\cong\varprojlim_{n}\mathscr{O}_{L}[T]\big/\big((1+T)^{p^{n}}-1\big).

The appearance of the expression
(1+T)^{p^{n}}-1
will become clearer in §
3.5.5
below (and its importance further recognised in Appendix
A
).

We invite the reader to spell out maps between the ‘level
n
’ terms of the inverse limits, analogous to (
3.4
) and such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{M}(\mathbf{Z}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}},\mathscr{O}_{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{O}_{L}[T]\big/\big((1+T)^{p^{n}}-1\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{O}_{L}[{\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}}]}

Definition 3.27
.

If
g\in\mathscr{O}_{L}[\![T]\!]
, we continue to write
\mu_{g}\in\mathscr{M}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},\mathscr{O}_{L})
for the corresponding (
\mathscr{O}_{L}
-valued) measure on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
(so that
\mathscr{A}_{\mu_{g}}=g)
.

Remark 3.28
.

(1)

Mahler’s isomorphism induces an isomorphism

\mathscr{M}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)\cong\mathscr{M}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},\mathscr{O}_{L})\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{L}}L\xrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}\mathscr{O}[\![T]\!]\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{L}}L\cong\mathscr{O}[\![T]\!][1/p].

(2)

Let
g\in\mathscr{O}_{L}[\![T]\!]
with associated measure
\mu_{g}
. From the definitions, it is easily seen that

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mu_{g}=g(0),\;\;\;\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}x\cdot\mu_{g}=g^{\prime}(0),\;\;\;\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}x^{2}\cdot\mu_{g}=g^{\prime\prime}(0)+g^{\prime}(0),

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}x^{3}\cdot\mu_{g}=g^{\prime\prime\prime}(0)+3g^{\prime\prime}(0)+g^{\prime}(0),\ \ \ \ldots,

that is, for every
n
, the value
\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}x^{n}\cdot\mu_{g}
can be written as an integer combination of
g^{(r)}(0)
for
0\leq r\leq n
. We simplify this in Corollary
3.30
below.

(3)

Recall (from Definition
3.5
) that there are two natural topologies on
\mathscr{M}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},\mathscr{O}_{L})
. One can check that under the isomorphism of Theorem
3.25
, the strong topology corresponds to the
p
-adic topology on
\mathscr{O}_{L}[\![T]\!]
, whilst the weak topology corresponds to the
(p,T)
-adic topology. Analogously to Remark
3.6
, the
p
-adic topology on
\mathscr{O}_{L}[\![T]\!]
is that of uniform convergence in the power series coefficients, whilst the
(p,T)
-adic topology is that of pointwise (term-by-term) convergence. For example, consider the sequence
1,T,T^{2},T^{3},\dots
in
\mathscr{O}_{L}[\![T]\!]
. This converges to 0 pointwise, and in the
(p,T)
-adic (weak) topology; but it does not converge uniformly, or in the
p
-adic (strong) topology.

3.5
A measure-theoretic toolbox

There are natural operations one might consider on measures, and via the Mahler transform these give rise to operators on power series. The following operations can be considered as a ‘toolbox’ for working with measures and power series; as we shall see in the sequel, the ability to manipulate measures in this way has important consequences. For further details (and more operations), see
[
Col10a
]
.

Notation
.

In light of the isomorphism between the space
\mathscr{M}(G,\mathscr{O}_{L})
of measures and the Iwasawa algebra
\Lambda(G)
, we will frequently conflate the two. In particular, it is convenient – and indeed standard – to write
\mu\in\Lambda(G)
for measures on
G
, typically suppressing the coefficient field
L
, which is fixed throughout.

3.5.1
Multiplication by
x

Given a measure
\mu
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, we naturally wish to compute
\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}x^{k}\cdot\mu
for
k
a positive integer. To allow us to do that, we let
x\mu
be the measure defined by

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}f(x)\cdot x\mu=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}xf(x)\cdot\mu.

We can ask what the operation
\mu\mapsto x\mu
does on Mahler transforms; we find:

Lemma 3.29
.

We have

\mathscr{A}_{x\mu}=\partial\mathscr{A}_{\mu},

where
\partial
denotes the differential operator
(1+T)\frac{d}{dT}
.

Proof.

The result follows directly from computing

x\begin{pmatrix}x\\
n\end{pmatrix}=(x-n)\begin{pmatrix}x\\
n\end{pmatrix}+n\begin{pmatrix}x\\
n\end{pmatrix}=(n+1)\begin{pmatrix}x\\
n+1\end{pmatrix}+n\begin{pmatrix}x\\
n\end{pmatrix}.\qed

From the above lemma, we immediately obtain the following expressions, completing the examples seen in Remark
3.28
.

Corollary 3.30
.

For
\mu\in\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p})
, we have

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}x^{k}\cdot\mu=\big(\partial^{k}\mathscr{A}_{\mu}\big)(0).

3.5.2
Multiplication by
z^{x}

Totally analogously to above, if
g\in\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
and
\mu
is a measure on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, then we can define a measure
g(x)\mu
by

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}f(x)\cdot g(x)\mu\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}f(x)g(x)\cdot\mu.

Of particular interest is the measure
z^{x}\mu
, for
z\in\mathscr{O}_{L}
such that
|z-1|<1
. We claim the Mahler transform of
z^{x}\mu
is

\mathscr{A}_{z^{x}\mu}(T)=\mathscr{A}_{\mu}\big((1+T)z-1\big).

Indeed, from the definition of the Mahler transform, we see that

\mathscr{A}_{\mu}\big((1+T)z-1\big)=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\big((1+T)z\big)^{x}\cdot\mu,

and this is precisely the Mahler transform of
z^{x}\mu
(one has to be slightly careful about convergence issues).

3.5.3
Restriction to open compact subsets

Consider an open compact subset
X\subset\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, and write
\mathbf{1}_{X}
for the characteristic function of
X
. The ‘restriction of
\mu
to
X
’ is the measure
\mathrm{Res}_{X}(\mu)
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
defined by

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}f\cdot\mathrm{Res}_{X}(\mu)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}f\mathbf{1}_{X}\cdot\mu.

It is also standard (and intuitive) to denote this quantity as

\int_{X}f\cdot\mu.

We say a measure
\mu
is
supported on
X
if
\mu=\mathrm{Res}_{X}(\mu)
.

Remark 3.31
.

Note that we may view this restriction as a measure on
X
as follows. For any continuous function
g:X\to L
, let
\widetilde{g}:\mathbf{Z}_{p}\to L
denote its extension by 0 outside
X
. The map

\mathscr{C}(X,L)\longrightarrow L,\hskip 20.00003ptg\longmapsto\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\widetilde{g}\cdot\mu

defines a measure on
X
. Abusing notation, we also denote this measure by
\mathrm{Res}_{X}(\mu)
, noting it is intuitively compatible with its cousin defined on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. Indeed we will often blur the distinction between considering
\mathrm{Res}_{X}(\mu)
as a measure on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
or on
X
.

In the case where
X=b+p^{n}\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, we can write the characteristic function explicitly as

\mathbf{1}_{b+p^{n}\mathbf{Z}_{p}}(x)=\frac{1}{p^{n}}\sum_{\xi\in\mu_{p^{n}}}\xi^{x-b},

and then using the above, we calculate the Mahler transform of
\mathrm{Res}_{b+p^{n}\mathbf{Z}_{p}}(\mu)
to be

(3.5)

\mathscr{A}_{\mathrm{Res}_{b+p^{n}\mathbf{Z}_{p}}(\mu)}(T)=\frac{1}{p^{n}}\sum_{\xi\in\mu_{p^{n}}}\xi^{-b}\mathscr{A}_{\mu}\big((1+T)\xi-1\big).

3.5.4
Restriction to
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}

From the above applied to
b=0
and
n=1
, it is immediate that

(3.6)

\mathscr{A}_{\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}(\mu)}(T)=\mathscr{A}_{\mu}(T)-\frac{1}{p}\sum_{\xi\in\mu_{p}}\mathscr{A}_{\mu}\big((1+T)\xi-1\big).

In order to calculate a formula for the restriction to an arbitrary open compact subset
X\subseteq\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, we can write
X
(or its complement, as we did with
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
) as a disjoint union of sets of the form
b+p^{n}\mathbf{Z}_{p}
and apply the formulas obtained before.

3.5.5
The action of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
,
\varphi
and
\psi

We introduce an action of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
that serves as a precursor to a Galois action later on. Let
a\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. We can define a measure
\sigma_{a}(\mu)
by

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}f(x)\cdot\sigma_{a}(\mu)=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}f(ax)\cdot\mu.

This has Mahler transform

\mathscr{A}_{\sigma_{a}(\mu)}=\mathscr{A}_{\mu}\big((1+T)^{a}-1\big).

In a similar manner, we can define an operator
\varphi
acting as ‘
\sigma_{p}
’ by

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}f(x)\cdot\varphi(\mu)=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}f(px)\cdot\mu,

and this corresponds to

(3.7)

\mathscr{A}_{\varphi(\mu)}=\varphi(\mathscr{A}_{\mu})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathscr{A}_{\mu}\big((1+T)^{p}-1\big).

Finally, we also define the analogous operator for
p^{-1}
; we define a measure
\psi(\mu)
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
by defining

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}f(x)\cdot\psi(\mu)=\int_{p\mathbf{Z}_{p}}f(p^{-1}x)\cdot\mu.

Note that
\psi\circ\varphi=\mathrm{id}
, whilst
\varphi\circ\psi(\mu)=\mathrm{Res}_{p\mathbf{Z}_{p}}(\mu)
. Indeed, we have

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}f(x)\cdot\psi\circ\varphi(\mu)=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathbf{1}_{p\mathbf{Z}_{p}}(x)f(p^{-1}x)\cdot\varphi(\mu)=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathbf{1}_{p\mathbf{Z}_{p}}(px)f(x)\cdot\varphi(\mu)=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}f(x)\cdot\mu,

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}f(x)\cdot\varphi\circ\psi(\mu)=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}f(px)\cdot\psi(\mu)=\int_{p\mathbf{Z}_{p}}f(x)\cdot\mu=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}f(x)\cdot\mathrm{Res}_{p\mathbf{Z}_{p}}(\mu).

In particular, we have

(3.8)

\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}(\mu)=(1-\varphi\circ\psi)(\mu).

The operator
\psi
also gives an operator on any
F(T)\in\mathscr{O}_{L}[\![T]\!]
under the Mahler transform, and using the restriction formula above, we see that it is the unique operator satisfying

(3.9)

\varphi\circ\psi(F)(T)=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{\xi\in\mu_{p}}F\big((1+T)\xi-1\big).

The following result will be useful in Part II.

Corollary 3.32
.

A measure
\mu\in\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p})
is supported on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
if and only if
\psi(\mathscr{A}_{\mu})=0
.

Proof.

Let
\mu\in\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p})
. Then
\mu
is supported on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
if and only if
\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}(\mu)=\mu
, or equivalently if and only if
\mathscr{A}_{\mu}=\mathscr{A}_{\mu}-\varphi\circ\psi(\mathscr{A}_{\mu})
, which happens if and only if
\psi(\mathscr{A}_{\mu})=0
, since the operator
\varphi
is injective.
∎

Remark 3.33
.

We have an injection
\iota:\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})\hookrightarrow\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p})
given by

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\phi\cdot\iota(\mu)=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\phi\big|_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\cdot\mu,

and as
\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\circ\iota
is the identity on
\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
, we can identify
\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
with its image as a subset of
\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p})
. By Corollary
3.32
, a measure
\mu\in\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p})
lies in
\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
if and only if
\psi(\mu)=0
. Whilst we identify
\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
with a subset of
\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p})
, it is important to remark that it is
not
a subalgebra. Indeed, convolution of measures on a group
G
uses the group structure of
G
; for
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
this is multiplicative, and for
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
this is additive (cf. Remark
3.18
). If
\lambda
and
\mu
are two measures on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, writing
\mu*_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\lambda
for the convolution over
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, we have

(3.10)

\int_{{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}}f(x)\cdot(\mu*_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\lambda)=\int_{{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}}\bigg(\int_{{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}}f(xy)\cdot\mu(x)\bigg)\cdot\lambda(y)

3.6
Pseudo-measures

The Mahler transform gives a correspondence between
p
-adic measures and
p
-adic analytic functions on the open unit ball (explained in Remark
3.39
below). The Riemann zeta function, however, is not analytic everywhere, as it has a simple pole at
s=1
. To reflect this, we also need to be able to handle simple poles on the
p
-adic side. We do this via the theory of pseudo-measures.

Definition 3.34
.

Let
G
be a profinite abelian group, and let
Q(G)
denote the ring of fractions of the Iwasawa algebra
\Lambda(G)
. A
pseudo-measure
on
G
is an element
\lambda\in Q(G)
such that

([g]-[1])\lambda\in\Lambda(G)

for all
g\in G
. (This is using the natural product on the Iwasawa algebra
\Lambda(G)
, which we recall corresponds to convolution (
3.10
) of measures).

We first explain how to integrate certain functions against pseudo-measures. Let
\chi:G\to{\mathbf{C}}_{p}^{\times}
be a non-trivial character, and let
\lambda\in Q(G)
be a pseudo-measure. Then one can define

(3.11)

\int_{G}\chi\cdot\lambda\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\chi(g)-1)^{-1}\int_{G}\chi\cdot([g]-[1])\lambda,

where
g\in G
is any element such that
\chi(g)\neq 1
. This definition does not depend on the choice of
g\in G
. Indeed one has

(\chi(h)-1)\int_{G}\chi\cdot([g]-[1])\lambda=\int_{G}\chi\cdot([g]-1)([h]-1)\lambda=(\chi(g)-1)\int_{G}\chi\cdot([h]-1)\mu,

where in the first and last equalities we used the convolution product and that
G
is abelian.

Remark 3.35
.

To motivate this definition, note that if
\chi:G\to\mathbf{C}_{p}^{\times}
is a non-trivial character, then from the definition of convolution product, the function

\displaystyle\Lambda(G)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}_{p},

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle\longmapsto\int_{G}\chi\cdot\mu

is a ring homomorphism. This induces a unique homomorphism
Q(G)\to\mathbf{C}_{p}
, which – when applied to a pseudo-measure – yields the expression (
3.11
) above.

We will be most interested in pseudo-measures on
G=\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. The following lemma shows that a pseudo-measure
\mu
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
is uniquely determined by the values
\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k}\cdot\mu
for
k>0
.

Lemma 3.36
.

(i)

Let
\mu\in\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
be such that

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k}\cdot\mu=0

for all
k>0
. Then
\mu=0
.

(ii)

Let
\mu\in\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
be such that

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k}\cdot\mu\neq 0

for all
k>0
. Then
\mu
is not a zero divisor in
\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}).

(iii)

Part (i) holds if, more generally,
\mu
is a pseudo-measure.

Proof.

(i) Note that the vanishing condition forces the Mahler transform
\mathscr{A}_{\mu}(T)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}{x\choose k}\cdot\mu\right)T^{k}
of
\mu
to be constant, since each non-trivial binomial polynomial is a linear combination of strictly positive powers of
x
. As
\mu
is a measure on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, we also have
\psi(\mathscr{A}_{\mu})(T)=0
by (
3.8
). Since
\psi
is the identity on constants (using, e.g., (
3.9
)), we deduce that
\mathscr{A}_{\mu}(T)=0
, so
\mu=0
.

(ii) Suppose there exists a measure
\lambda
such that
\mu*_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\lambda=0
, where the product is the convolution product on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
(cf. Remark
3.33
). Then

0=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k}\cdot(\mu*_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\lambda)=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}(xy)^{k}\cdot\mu(x)\bigg)\cdot\lambda(y)=\bigg(\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k}\cdot\mu\bigg)\bigg(\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k}\cdot\lambda\bigg),

which forces
\lambda=0
by part (i). In particular
\mu
is not a zero divisor.

(iii) Let
\mu
be a pseudo-measure satisfying the vanishing condition. Let
a\neq 1
be an integer prime to
p
; then
\lambda=([a]-[1])\mu\in\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
is a measure by the definition of pseudo-measure, and by (
3.11
) we have

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k}\cdot\lambda=(a^{k}-1)\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k}\cdot\mu=0

for all
k>0
. By part (i), we have
\lambda=0
. But
[a]-[1]
satisfies the condition of part (ii), so it is not a zero-divisor, and this forces
\mu=0
, as required.
∎

Finally, we give a simpler process for writing down pseudo-measures on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
.

Definition 3.37
.

The
augmentation ideal

I((\mathbf{Z}_{p}/p^{n})^{\times})\subset\mathscr{O}_{L}[(\mathbf{Z}_{p}/p^{n})^{\times}]
is the kernel of the natural ‘degree’ map

\mathscr{O}_{L}[({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}]\to\mathscr{O}_{L},\hskip 20.00003pt\sum_{a}c_{a}[a]\mapsto\sum_{a}c_{a}.

These fit together into a degree map
\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})\to\mathscr{O}_{L}
; we call its kernel the augmentation ideal
I(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})\subset\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
. One may check directly there is an isomorphism

I(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})\cong\varprojlim I((\mathbf{Z}_{p}/p^{n})^{\times}).

Lemma 3.38
.

Let
a
be any topological generator of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
(for example, take
a
to be a primitive root modulo
p
such that
a^{p-1}\not\equiv 1\;(mod\;p^{2})
), and
\mu\in\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
a measure. Then
