\mu^{\prime}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\mu}{[a]-[1]}\in Q(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})

is a pseudo-measure.

Proof.

As
p
is odd,
(\mathbf{Z}_{p}/p^{n})^{\times}
is cyclic, generated by
\overline{a}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=a\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp^{n})
, and we have

I((\mathbf{Z}_{p}/p^{n})^{\times})=([\overline{a}]-[\overline{1}])\mathscr{O}_{L}[(\mathbf{Z}_{p}/p^{n})^{\times}].

In the inverse limit we see that

I(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})=([a]-[1])\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}).

Thus if
g\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, we have
[g]-[1]\in I(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
, and we must have

[g]-[1]=\nu([a]-[1])

for some
\nu\in\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
. Then

([g]-[1])\mu^{\prime}=\nu([a]-[1])\mu^{\prime}=\nu\cdot\mu\in\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}),

that is,
\mu^{\prime}
is a pseudo-measure.
∎

Note moreover that
all
pseudo-measures have this shape. Indeed, let
\mu^{\prime}
be a pseudo-measure, and
a\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
a topological generator; then
\mu=([a]-[1])\mu^{\prime}
is a measure, and
\mu^{\prime}=\mu/([a]-[1])
as above.

3.7
Locally analytic functions and distributions

We finally introduce another important space of functions and its dual, namely
locally analytic functions
and
locally analytic distributions
. This subsection may be safely skipped on a first reading, and indeed we make only peripheral use of its content in these notes (in §
6.2
and §
7
, to study values of the
p
-adic zeta function). The locally analytic theory is nonetheless of fundamental importance in more general settings, so we include a sketch here, and indicate how it dovetails beautifully with the theory of measures we have already studied. All of this – and other related theories in
p
-adic functional analysis – are described in detail in
[
Col10a
]
.

As motivation, we first note the following.

Remark 3.39
.

The space
\mathscr{M}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
of measures has an interpretation via rigid analysis. To explain this, consider the
p
-adic open unit ball in
\mathbf{C}_{p}
, i.e. the space

B(0,1)=\{z\in\mathbf{C}_{p}\,|\,|z|<1\}\subset\mathbf{C}_{p}.

This is the set of
\mathbf{C}_{p}
-points of a rigid analytic space in the sense of
[
BGR84
]
. An
L
-valued function on
B(0,1)
is
rigid analytic
if it can be written as a power series
\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n}T^{n}\in L[\![T]\!]
that is everywhere convergent on
B(0,1)
(i.e.
|a_{n}|r^{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
for all
r<1
); write
\mathscr{R}^{+}\subset L[\![T]\!]
for the space of such functions. A rigid analytic function is
bounded
if the
|a_{i}|
are bounded.

Note that the space of bounded
L
-valued rigid analytic functions is
\mathscr{O}_{L}[\![T]\!]\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{L}}L
, which, via the Mahler transform (Remark
3.28
), is isomorphic to
\mathscr{M}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
. Hence
p
-adic measures on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
can be viewed as bounded rigid analytic functions on
B(0,1)
.

It is natural to ask if the Mahler correspondence, as studied in Theorem
3.25
, can be extended from
\mathscr{O}_{L}[\![T]\!]
to all of
\mathscr{R}^{+}
. Such an extension is given by locally analytic distributions, in the sense described in Theorem
3.43
and equation (
3.12
) below.

Definition 3.40
.

Let
L/\mathbf{Q}_{p}
be a finite extension, and let
f:\mathbf{Z}_{p}\to L

be a function.

(1)

For
z\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, we say
f
is
locally analytic at
z
if
f
can be described locally around
z
by a convergent power series. Precisely, this means there exists some integer
n_{z}\geq 0
and numbers
\{a_{k}(z)\in L:k\geq 0\}
, such that

\sum_{k\geq 0}a_{k}(z)\cdot(x-z)^{k}

converges to
f(x)
for all
x\in U_{z}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=z+p^{n_{z}}\mathbf{Z}_{p}
.

(2)

We say
f
is
locally analytic
if it is locally analytic at all
z\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}
.

(3)

We write
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{la}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
for the
L
-vector space of all locally analytic functions
\mathbf{Z}_{p}\to L
.

Recall that
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
can be equipped with a valuation that makes it into an
L
-Banach space, and that the space of measures
\mathscr{M}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
was defined as its continuous dual. Analogously, the space
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{la}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
has a natural topology, described as follows (see
[
Col10a
, §I.4]
for more details). For each
n\in{\mathbf{N}}
, we say a function
f:\mathbf{Z}_{p}\to L
is
locally analytic of radius
p^{-n}
if it is locally analytic and moreover we can take
n_{z}=n
for all
z\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}
; in other words, it is analytic (described by a single power series) on each open set of form
z+p^{n}\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. Denote by
\mathscr{C}^{n-\mathrm{an}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
the subspace of such functions. Then one can check that
\mathscr{C}^{n-\mathrm{an}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
is an
L
-Banach space with valuation given by

v_{n}(f)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\inf_{z\in(\mathbf{Z}_{p}/p^{n}\mathbf{Z}_{p})}\inf_{k\in{\mathbf{N}}}\Big(nk+v_{p}(a_{k}(z))\Big),

or equivalently norm given by

||f||_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sup_{z\in(\mathbf{Z}_{p}/p^{n}\mathbf{Z}_{p})}\sup_{k\in{\mathbf{N}}}|a_{k}(z)|p^{-nk}.

Moreover, almost by definition, we have that
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{la}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)=\varinjlim_{n\in{\mathbf{N}}}\mathscr{C}^{n-\mathrm{an}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
, and so it inherits a natural topology given by the direct limit topology.

Remark 3.41
.

Locally analytic functions are continuous, so
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{la}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)\subset\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
. Note, however, that the topology we just defined on
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{la}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
is
not
the one induced from
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
. Nonetheless the image of this inclusion is dense, as, for example, locally constant functions are locally analytic functions and are dense in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
.

Analogously to Definition
3.8
, we make the following definition.

Definition 3.42
.

We define the space
\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{la}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
of locally analytic distributions on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
to be the continuous dual
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{la}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L),L)
.

If
\mu
is a locally analytic distribution on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, and
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{la}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
, we continue to write

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\phi(x)\cdot\mu(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mu(\phi).

The binomial polynomials
{x\choose n}
are visibly locally analytic, so we may also extend the Mahler transform to this generality; namely, if
\mu\in\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{la}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
, define

\mathscr{A}_{\mu}(T)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}(1+T)^{x}\cdot\mu(x)=\sum_{n\geq 0}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}{x\choose n}\cdot\mu\right)T^{n}\in L[\![T]\!].

The following crucial result provides the desired extension of the Mahler transform beyond bounded measures/power series.

Theorem 3.43
.

The Mahler transform induces a bijection

\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{la}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)\longrightarrow\mathscr{R}^{+}\subset L[\![T]\!].

Proof.

This is
[
Col10a
, Thm. II.2.2]
.
∎

As with Theorem
3.25
, the theorem says more: the bijection respects natural topologies on both sides. Here the topologies are as follows:

–

\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{la}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
is the inverse limit of the continuous duals
\mathscr{D}^{n-\mathrm{an}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
; each of these is a Banach space with the natural dual (strong) topology (see Remark
3.6
). This endows
\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{la}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
with the corresponding inverse limit topology.

–

As the open unit disc is the union of the closed discs
B(0,r)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{z\in\mathbf{C}_{p}:|z|\leq r\}
of radius
r<1
, we can write
\mathscr{R}^{+}
as the inverse limit over
r<1
of the Banach spaces
\mathcal{O}(B(0,r))
of analytic functions on
B(0,r)
. Again we get an inverse limit topology on
\mathscr{R}^{+}
.

A topology induced from an inverse limit of Banach spaces is called a
Fréchet topology
. The Mahler transform is then an isomorphism of Fréchet spaces.

Remark 3.44
.

If
\mu\in\mathscr{M}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
is any measure, then we obtain a locally analytic distribution
\widetilde{\mu}\in\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{la}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
by restricting to
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{la}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)\subset\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)
. As locally analytic functions are dense inside continuous functions, the association
\mu\mapsto\widetilde{\mu}
is injective, and hence this identification allows us to consider
\mathscr{M}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)\subset\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{la}}
as a subset. The combination of Theorems
3.25
and
3.43
says that this inclusion is compatible with the natural inclusion of bounded power series inside power series converging in the open unit disc, that is, the following diagram commutes:

(3.12)

\textstyle{\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{la}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\supset}
\scriptstyle{\mu\longmapsto\mathscr{A}_{\mu}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{R}^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\supset}
\textstyle{\mathscr{M}(\mathbf{Z}_{p},L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu\longmapsto\mathscr{A}_{\mu}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{O}_{L}[\![T]\!]\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{L}}L.}

Remark 3.45
.

Observe that every part of our ‘measure-theoretic toolbox’ from §
3.5
, including corresponding operations on Mahler transforms, carries over identically to the setting of locally analytic distributions.

Remark 3.46
.

Locally analytic
p
-adic analysis is fundamentally important in the study of
p
-adic
L
-functions (and in many other areas, such as in the study of
p
-adic automorphic forms, in the
p
-adic Langlands correspondence, in
p
-adic Hodge theory, etc.). Indeed, more general
p
-adic
L
-functions – for example, those attached to elliptic curves and modular forms – are frequently not measures or pseudo-measures, but rather locally analytic distributions with prescribed growth, in the sense of
[
AV75
]
. We discuss this further in Appendix
B
.

3.8
Further remarks

The following remarks will not be seriously used in the sequel, but are included for completeness, and to illustrate some other ways that the objects studied in this chapter appear in the literature.

Remark 3.47
.

We have an analogue of Remark
3.39
for measures on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, using instead the multiplicative structure. The key object here is the
weight space

\mathcal{W}(\mathbf{C}_{p})=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times},\mathbf{C}_{p}^{\times}).

Since
{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}=\mu_{p-1}\times(1+p\mathbf{Z}_{p})
, we have

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times},\mathbf{C}_{p}^{\times})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(\mu_{p-1},\mathbf{C}_{p}^{\times})\times\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(1+p\mathbf{Z}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{p}^{\times}).

Moreover, evaluation at a topological generator of
1+p\mathbf{Z}_{p}
identifies
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(1+p\mathbf{Z}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{p}^{\times})
with
B(0,1)
from above. Hence we may identify
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times},\mathbf{C}_{p}^{\times})
with
p-1
copies of
B(0,1)
. Summarising,

\mathcal{W}(\mathbf{C}_{p})=\bigsqcup_{\nu\in(\mu_{p-1})^{\vee}}U_{\nu},

where:

–

\nu
ranges over the
p-1
different characters of
\mu_{p-1}
.

–

U_{\nu}\subset\mathcal{W}(\mathbf{C}_{p})
is the subset of characters
\chi
of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
with
\chi|_{\mu_{p-1}}=\nu
.

–

Each
U_{\nu}
may be identified with
B(0,1)
.

This space can also be given more structure; there is a rigid analytic space
\mathcal{W}
such that the elements of
\mathcal{W}(\mathbf{C}_{p})
are the
\mathbf{C}_{p}
-points of
\mathcal{W}
. Analogously to above, a theorem of Amice says that giving a measure
\mu
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
is equivalent to giving a bounded rigid analytic function
F_{\mu}
on
\mathcal{W}
. This equivalence is given as follows: if
\mu
is a measure on
{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}
and
\chi:{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}\to{\mathbf{C}}_{p}^{\times}
is a character (seen as a point on
\mathcal{W}(\mathbf{C}_{p})
), then one defines
F_{\mu}(\chi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}}\chi\cdot\mu
. Observe that the multiplicative convolution product corresponds to pointwise multiplication of rigid functions.

Finally, if
\lambda\in Q({{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}})
is a pseudo-measure, then it is of the form
\frac{\mu}{([a]-[1])}
for some topological generator
a\in{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}
and some measure
\mu\in\Lambda({{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}})
. Note
\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi\cdot([a]-[1])=0
if and only if
\chi(a)=1
, which – as
a
is a topological generator of
{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}
– implies
\chi
is the trivial character. Hence, as a function on the weight space,
\lambda
might have a simple pole at the trivial character. So pseudo-measures can be seen as rigid analytic functions on the weight space that possibly have a simple pole at the trivial character.

Remark 3.48
.

Power series rings have been generalised to what now are called Fontaine rings. It turns out that Galois representations are connected to certain modules over these rings called
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules. The operations described above generalise to fundamental operations on
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules, and their interpretation via
p
-adic analysis inspired the proof of the
p
-adic Langlands correspondence for
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{p})
(see
[
Col10b
]
).

4
The Kubota-Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function

In this section, we prove the following:

Theorem 4.1
.

There is a unique pseudo-measure
\zeta_{p}
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
such that, for all
k>0
, we have

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k}\cdot\zeta_{p}=(1-p^{k-1})\zeta(1-k).

This pseudo-measure, denoted
\zeta_{p}^{\mathrm{an}}
in §
1
, is the
Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function
.

4.1
The measure
\mu_{a}

Recall from Lemma
2.7
that we can write the Riemann zeta function in the form

(s-1)\zeta(s)=L(f,s-1)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s-1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}f(t)t^{s-2}dt,

where
f(t)=t/(e^{t}-1)
, and that
\zeta(-k)=(d^{k}f/dt^{k})(0)=(-1)^{k}B_{k+1}/(k+1).
We want to remove the smoothing factor at
s=1
. For this, let
a
be an integer coprime to
p
and consider the related function

f_{a}(t)=\frac{1}{e^{t}-1}-\frac{a}{e^{at}-1}.

This is also
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}
and rapidly decreasing, so we can apply Theorem
2.4
and consider the function
L(f_{a},s)
. The presence of
a
removes the factor of
s-1
, at the cost of introducing a different smoothing factor.

Lemma 4.2
.

We have

L(f_{a},s)=(1-a^{1-s})\zeta(s),

which has an analytic continuation to
{\mathbf{C}}
, and

f_{a}^{(k)}(0)=(-1)^{k}(1-a^{1+k})\zeta(-k).

Proof.

This follows from calculations similar to those in the proof of Lemma
2.7
.
∎

We now introduce the
p
-adic tools we have developed into the picture. We will start with the function
f_{a}(t)
, and slowly manipulate it until we construct a (pseudo-)measure with the desired interpolation properties. Note first the following very simple observation.

Lemma 4.3
.

Under the substitution
e^{t}=T+1
, the derivative
d/dt
becomes the operator
\partial=(1+T)\frac{d}{dT}
. In particular, if we define

F_{a}(T)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{T}-\frac{a}{(1+T)^{a}-1},

we have

(4.1)

f_{a}^{(k)}(0)=\big(\partial^{k}F_{a}\big)(0).

The left-hand side of (
4.1
) computes the
L
-value
\zeta(-k)
by Lemma
4.2
. The right-hand side is similar to Corollary
3.30
, which expressed the integral
\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}x^{k}\cdot\mu
in terms of the Mahler transform
\mathscr{A}_{\mu}
. This motivates us to seek a measure
\mu_{a}
with
\mathscr{A}_{\mu_{a}}=F_{a}
. This is possible by:

Proposition 4.4
.

The function
F_{a}(T)
is an element of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
.

Proof.

We can expand

(1+T)^{a}-1=\sum_{n\geq 1}{a\choose n}T^{n}=aT\big[1+Tg(T)\big],

where
g(T)=\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{1}{a}{a\choose n}T^{n-2}
has coefficients in
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
since we have chosen
a
coprime to
p
. Hence, expanding the geometric series, we find

\frac{1}{T}-\frac{a}{(1+T)^{a}-1}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{n\geq 1}(-T)^{n}g(T)^{n},

which is visibly an element of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
.
∎

Definition 4.5
.

Let
\mu_{a}
be the measure on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
whose Mahler transform is
F_{a}(T)
.

Proposition 4.6
.

For
k\geq 0
, we have

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}x^{k}\cdot\mu_{a}=(-1)^{k}(1-a^{k+1})\zeta(-k).

Proof.

By Corollary
3.30
, the left-hand side is
(\partial^{k}\mathscr{A}_{\mu_{a}})(0)
. By definition of
\mu_{a}
and Lemma
4.3
this is
(\partial^{k}F_{a})(0)=f_{a}^{(k)}(0)
. This equals the right-hand side by Lemma
4.2
.
∎

4.2
Restriction to
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}

Recall from the introduction that we want the
p
-adic analogue of the Riemann zeta function to be a measure on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, not all of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. We have already defined a restriction operator in equation (
3.8
), which on Mahler transforms acts as
1-\varphi\circ\psi
. We begin with a short but important property of the measure
\mu_{a}
.

Lemma 4.7
.

We have
\psi(\mu_{a})=\mu_{a}
.

Proof.

We show the result by considering the action on power series. We wish to show
\psi(F_{a})=F_{a}
. First note that
F_{a}(T)=\frac{1}{T}-a\cdot\sigma_{a}(\frac{1}{T})
, for
\sigma_{a}
as in §
3.5.5
. As
\psi
commutes with
\sigma_{a}
, we have
\psi(F_{a})=\psi(\frac{1}{T})-a\cdot\sigma_{a}\psi(\frac{1}{T})
, so it suffices to show
\psi(\frac{1}{T})=\frac{1}{T}
.

By definition (cf. equation (
3.9
)) we have

\displaystyle(\varphi\circ\psi)\left(\frac{1}{T}\right)

\displaystyle=p^{-1}\sum_{\xi\in\mu_{p}}\frac{1}{(1+T)\xi-1}=\frac{1}{(1+T)^{p}-1}=\varphi\left(\frac{1}{T}\right),

as can be seen by calculating the partial fraction expansion. By injectivity of
\varphi
, we deduce that
\psi(\frac{1}{T})=\frac{1}{T}
, and conclude.
∎

Proposition 4.8
.

We have

(4.2)

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k}\cdot\mu_{a}=(-1)^{k}(1-p^{k})(1-a^{k+1})\zeta(-k).

(In other words, restricting to
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
removes the Euler factor at
p
).

Proof.

Since
\mathrm{Res}_{{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}}=1-\varphi\circ\psi
, we deduce that

\int_{{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}}x^{k}\cdot\mu_{a}=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}x^{k}\cdot(1-\varphi\circ\psi)\mu_{a}=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}x^{k}\cdot(1-\varphi)\mu_{a}=(1-p^{k})\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}x^{k}\cdot\mu_{a},

where for the second equality we have used Lemma
4.7
. This finishes the proof.
∎

4.3
Rescaling and removing dependence on
a

Finally we remove the dependence on
a
. Thus far, the presence of
a
has acted as a ‘smoothing factor’ which removes the pole of the Riemann zeta function; so to remove it, we must be able to handle such poles on the
p
-adic side. We use the notion of pseudo-measures from §
3.6
.

Definition 4.9
.

Let
a
be an integer that is prime to
p
, and let
\theta_{a}
denote the element of
\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
corresponding to
[a]-[1]
. Note that, by definition, we have

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k}\cdot\theta_{a}=a^{k}-1

However, in (
4.2
) it is
a^{k+1}-1
that appears. To bridge this gap, note that on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, we have a well-defined operation ‘multiplication by
x^{-1}
’ given by

(4.3)

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}f(x)\cdot x^{-1}\mu\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{-1}f(x)\cdot\mu,

and that

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k}\cdot x^{-1}\mu_{a}=(-1)^{k}(a^{k}-1)(1-p^{k-1})\zeta(1-k).

We comment further on this multiplication by
x^{-1}
in Remark
12.7
.

Definition 4.10
.

Let
a
be a topological generator of
{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}
. The
p
-adic zeta function
is

\zeta_{p}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{x^{-1}\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\mu_{a}}{\theta_{a}}\in Q(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}).

Proposition 4.11
.

The element
\zeta_{p}
is a well-defined pseudo-measure satisfying

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k}\cdot\zeta_{p}=(1-p^{k-1})\zeta(1-k)\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all }k>0.

Proof.

We see
\zeta_{p}
is a pseudo-measure by Lemma
3.38
. It is independent of the choice of
a
by Lemma
3.36
(iii).

Using Equation (
3.11
) (to integrate the pseudo-measure) and Proposition
4.8
, we obtain the interpolation property

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k}\cdot\zeta_{p}=(-1)^{k}(1-p^{k-1})\zeta(1-k).

To get the result, we may remove the
(-1)^{k}
as
\zeta(1-k)\neq 0
if and only if
k
is even.
∎

We finally prove Theorem
4.1
. Existence of the pseudo-measure is Proposition
4.11
. To conclude the proof we need only show uniqueness; but this follows from Lemma
3.36
(iii). ∎

5
Interpolation at Dirichlet characters

In our study of the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function, the entire construction was essentially built to interpolate special values of the Riemann zeta function, so this property should not have come as a surprise. Now, however, some real magic happens. Since the introduction, we’ve not mentioned Dirichlet
L
-functions once – but, miraculously, the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function also interpolates Dirichlet
L
-values as well.

5.1
Characters of
p
-power conductor

We start studying the interpolation properties when twisting by a Dirichlet character of conductor a power of
p
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\chi
be a (primitive) Dirichlet character of conductor
p^{n}
for some integer
n\geq 1
(seen as a locally constant character of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, cf. §
2.4.2
). Then, for
k>0
, we have

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi(x)x^{k}\cdot\zeta_{p}=L(\chi,1-k).

The rest of this subsection will contain the proof of this result. The proof is somewhat calculation-heavy, but – given familiarity with the dictionary between measures and power series – is not conceptually difficult.

In particular: the Riemann zeta function was the complex Mellin transform of a real analytic function, which – via Theorem
2.4
– gave us a formula for its special values. Under the transformation
e^{t}=T+1
, we obtained a
p
-adic power series; and under the measures–power series correspondence given by the Mahler transform, this gave us a measure on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, from which we constructed
\zeta_{p}
. To obtain interpolation at Dirichlet characters, we pursue this in reverse, as summarised in the following diagram:

\textstyle{(1-a^{1-s})\zeta(s)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Mellin
\textstyle{(1-\chi(a)a^{1-s})L(\chi,s)}
\textstyle{f_{a}(t)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e^{t}=T+1}
\textstyle{F_{a}(T)\in\mathcal{O}_{L}[\![T]\!]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Mahler
\textstyle{f_{a,\chi}(t)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e^{t}=T+1}
Mellin
\textstyle{F_{a,\chi}(T)\in\mathcal{O}_{L}[\![T]\!]}
\textstyle{\mu_{a}\in\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
“twist by
\chi
”
\textstyle{\mu_{a,\chi}\in\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Mahler
\textstyle{\zeta_{p}}

Firstly, we introduce a twisting operation on measures. If
\mu
is a measure on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, we define a measure
\mu_{\chi}
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
by

(5.1)

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}f(x)\cdot\mu_{\chi}=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\chi(x)f(x)\cdot\mu.

Observe that, as
\chi
is supported on
{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}
, the twisted measure
\mu_{\chi}
is automatically supported on
{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}
as well. In particular, under this we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi(x)x^{k}\cdot\zeta_{p}=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k}\cdot(\zeta_{p})_{\chi}=\big(\partial^{k}\mathscr{A}_{(\zeta_{p})_{\chi}}\big)(0),

where the last equality follows from Corollary
3.30
. Thus we want to determine the Mahler transform of
\mu_{\chi}
in terms of
\mathscr{A}_{\mu}
, for which we use our measure-theoretic toolkit. This requires a classical definition.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
\chi
be a primitive Dirichlet character of conductor
p^{n}
,
n\geq 1
. Define the
Gauss sum of
\chi
as

G(\chi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\chi(c)\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{c},

where
(\varepsilon_{p^{n}})_{n\in{\mathbf{N}}}
denotes a system of primitive
p
-power roots of unity in
\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}
such that
\varepsilon_{p^{n+1}}^{p}=\varepsilon_{p^{n}}
for all
n\geq 0
(if we fix an isomorphism
\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}\cong{\mathbf{C}}
, then one can take
\varepsilon_{p^{n}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=e^{2\pi i/p^{n}}
).

Remark 5.3
.

We note the following basic properties of Gauss sums (see
[
DS05
, §4.3]
):

(i)

G(\chi)G(\chi^{-1})=\chi(-1)p^{n}.

(ii)

G(\chi)=\chi(a)\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\chi(c)\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{ac}
for any
a\in{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

The Mahler transform of
\mu_{\chi}
is

\mathscr{A}_{\mu_{\chi}}(T)=\frac{1}{G(\chi^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\chi(c)^{-1}\mathscr{A}_{\mu}\big((1+T)\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{c}-1\big).

Proof.

Since
\chi
is constant modulo
p^{n}
, the measure
\mu_{\chi}
is simply

\mu_{\chi}=\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\chi(c)\mathrm{Res}_{c+p^{n}\mathbf{Z}_{p}}(\mu).

Using this expression and the formula for the Mahler transform of the restriction of a measure to
c+p^{n}\mathbf{Z}_{p}
given in (
3.5
), we find that

\mathscr{A}_{\mu_{\chi}}(T)=\frac{1}{p^{n}}\sum_{b\in({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\chi(b)\sum_{\xi\in\mu_{p^{n}}}\xi^{-b}\mathscr{A}_{\mu}\big((1+T)\xi-1\big).

Writing
\mu_{p^{n}}=\{\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{c}:c=0,...,p^{n}-1\},
and rearranging the sums, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{\mu_{\chi}}(T)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{n}}\sum_{c\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp^{n})}\sum_{b\in({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\chi(b)\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{-bc}\mathscr{A}_{\mu}\big((1+T)\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{c}-1\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{n}}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}G(\chi)\chi(-c)^{-1}\mathscr{A}_{\mu}\big((1+T)\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{c}-1\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{G(\chi^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\chi(c)^{-1}\mathscr{A}_{\mu}\big((1+T)\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{c}-1\big),

where the second equality follows from Remark
5.3
(ii) and the last one from Remark
5.3
(i). This finishes the proof.
∎

We now consider the case where
\mu=\mu_{a}
from Definition
4.5
, the measure from which we built the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function, and which has Mahler transform

\mathscr{A}_{\mu_{a}}(T)=\frac{1}{T}-\frac{a}{(1+T)^{a}-1}.

Applying the above transformation, we obtain a measure
\mu_{\chi,a}
with Mahler transform

F_{\chi,a}(T)=\frac{1}{G(\chi^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\chi(c)^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{(1+T)\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{c}-1}-\frac{a}{(1+T)^{a}\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{ac}-1}\right].

Via the standard substitution
e^{t}=T+1
, this motivates the study of the function

f_{\chi,a}(t)=\frac{1}{G(\chi^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\chi(c)^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{e^{t}\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{c}-1}-\frac{a}{e^{at}\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{ac}-1}\right],

by analogy with the case of the Riemann zeta function.

Lemma 5.5
.

We have

L(f_{\chi,a},s)=\chi(-1)\big(1-\chi(a)a^{1-s}\big)L(\chi,s),

where
L(f_{\chi,a},s)
is as defined in Theorem
2.4
. Moreover, for
k\geq 0
, we have

f_{\chi,a}^{(k)}(0)=\begin{cases}-\big(1-\chi(a)a^{k+1}\big)L(\chi,-k)&\quad\text{if }\chi(-1)(-1)^{k}=-1\\
0&\quad\text{if }\chi(-1)(-1)^{k}=1.\end{cases}

Proof.

We follow a similar strategy to that used for the Riemann zeta function (in Lemma
2.7
). In particular, expanding as a geometric series, we obtain

\frac{1}{e^{t}\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{c}-1}=\sum_{k\geq 1}e^{-kt}\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{-kc}.

Then we have

L(f_{\chi,a},s)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)G(\chi^{-1})}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\chi(c)^{-1}\sum_{k\geq 1}\bigg(e^{-kt}\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{-kc}-e^{-akt}\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{-akc}\bigg)t^{s-1}dt.

Note that

\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\chi(c)^{-1}\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{-akc}=\chi(-ak)G(\chi^{-1}),

and similarly for the first term, so that the expression collapses to

L(f_{\chi,a},s)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sum_{k\geq 1}\chi(-k)\big(e^{-kt}-\chi(a)e^{-akt}\big)t^{s-1}dt.

For Re
(s)\gg 0
, we can rearrange the sum and the integral, and then we can evaluate the
k
-th term of the sum easily to
(1-\chi(a)a^{1-s})k^{-s}
, giving

L(f_{\chi,a},s)=\chi(-1)\big(1-\chi(a)a^{1-s}\big)\sum_{k\geq 1}\chi(-k)k^{-s}=\chi(-1)\big(1-\chi(a)a^{1-s}\big)L(\chi,s),

showing the equality of
L
-functions.

To see the final statement about special values, we use Theorem
2.4
, which immediately says

(5.2)

f_{\chi,a}^{(k)}(0)=(-1)^{k}\chi(-1)(1-\chi(a)a^{k+1})L(\chi,-k).

To get the claimed statement, we note that

\frac{1}{e^{-t}\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{c}-1}=-1-\frac{1}{e^{t}\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{-c}-1},

and using this twice, we find

f_{\chi,a}(-t)=-\frac{1}{G(\chi^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\chi(c)^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{e^{t}\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{-c}-1}-\frac{a}{e^{at}\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{-ac}-1}\right].

Changing
c
for
-c
yields
f_{\chi,a}(-t)=-\chi(-1)f_{\chi,a}(t)
, whence
(-1)^{k}f^{(k)}_{\chi,a}(0)=-\chi(-1)f^{(k)}_{\chi,a}(0)
. This implies that
f_{\chi,a}^{(k)}(0)=0
unless
\chi(-1)(-1)^{k}=-1
, concluding the proof.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

By (
5.2
) and the above proof, we recover the well-known fact that
L(\chi,-k)=0
if
\chi(-1)(-1)^{k}=1
.

We can now prove Theorem
5.1
.

Proof.

(Theorem
5.1
).

Since
\chi
is 0 on
p\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, we have

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi(x)x^{k}\cdot\mu_{a}=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\chi(x)x^{k}\cdot\mu_{a}=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}x^{k}\cdot\mu_{\chi,a},

where
\mu_{\chi,a}
is the twist of
\mu_{a}
by
\chi
. We know this integral to be

\big(\partial^{k}F_{\chi,a}\big)(0)=f_{\chi,a}^{(k)}(0),

under the standard transform
e^{t}=T+1
. Hence, by Lemma
5.5
, we find

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi(x)x^{k}\cdot\mu_{a}=-(1-\chi(a)a^{k+1})L(\chi,-k),

so that

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi(x)x^{k}\cdot x^{-1}\mu_{a}=-(1-\chi(a)a^{k})L(\chi,1-k).

By definition, we have

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi(x)x^{k}\cdot\theta_{a}=-(1-\chi(a)a^{k}),

and hence we find

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi(x)x^{k}\cdot\zeta_{p}=L(\chi,1-k),

as was to be proved.
∎

5.2
Non-trivial tame conductors

We can go even further. The theorem above deals with the case of ‘tame conductor 1’, in that we have constructed a
p
-adic measure that interpolates all of the
L
-values
L(\chi,1-k)
for
k>0
and cond
(\chi)=p^{n}
with
n\geq 0
(where trivial conductor corresponds to the Riemann zeta function). More generally, we have the following result.

Theorem 5.7
.

Let
D>1
be any integer coprime to
p
, and let
\eta
denote a (primitive) Dirichlet character of conductor
D
. There exists a unique measure
\zeta_{\eta}\in\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
such that, for all primitive Dirichlet characters
\chi
with conductor
p^{n}
,
n\geq 0
, and for all
k>0
, we have

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi(x)x^{k}\cdot\zeta_{\eta}=\big(1-\chi\eta(p)p^{k-1}\big)L(\chi\eta,1-k).

Remark 5.8
.

(1)

In this case, we obtain a genuine measure rather than a pseudo-measure. As
L
-functions of non-trivial Dirichlet characters are everywhere holomorphic, there is no need for the smoothing factor involving
a
.

(2)

Implicit in this theorem is the fact that the relevant Iwasawa algebra is defined over a (fixed) finite extension
L/\mathbf{Q}_{p}
containing the values of
\eta
.

Proof.

Since many of the ideas involved in proving the above theorem are present in the case of trivial tame conductor, the proof of Theorem
5.7
is a good exercise. As such, we give only the main ideas involved in the proof.

For
\chi
of
p
-power conductor, note that the calculation relating
L(f_{\chi,a},s)
to
L(\chi,s)
above was entirely classical, in the sense that
p
did not appear anywhere. We can thus perform a similar calculation for general conductors. As there is no need for the smoothing factor
a
, we consider the function

f_{\eta}(t)=\frac{-1}{G(\eta^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/D{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\frac{\eta(c)^{-1}}{e^{t}\varepsilon_{D}^{c}-1}.

(This scaling by
-1
also appears in the trivial tame conductor situation, but it is incorporated into
\theta_{a}
). In the above definition, the Gauss sum
G(\eta^{-1})
of a Dirichlet character
\eta
of conductor
D
is defined as in Definition
5.2
, replacing the power of
p
by
D
. Define
F_{\eta}(T)
by substituting
T+1
for
e^{t}
, i.e.

(5.3)

F_{\eta}(T)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{-1}{G(\eta^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/D{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\frac{\eta(c)^{-1}}{(1+T)\varepsilon_{D}^{c}-1}.

Expanding the geometric series, we find

F_{\eta}(T)=\frac{-1}{G(\eta^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/D{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\eta(c)^{-1}\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{\varepsilon_{D}^{kc}}{(\varepsilon_{D}^{c}-1)^{k+1}}T^{k}.

This is an element of
\mathscr{O}_{L}[\![T]\!]
for some sufficiently large finite extension
L
of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
, since the Gauss sum is a
p
-adic unit (indeed, we have
G(\eta)G(\eta^{-1})=\eta(-1)D
and
D
is coprime to
p
) and
\varepsilon_{D}^{c}-1\in\mathscr{O}_{L}^{\times}
(since it has norm dividing
D
). There is therefore a measure
\mu_{\eta}\in\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p})
, the Iwasawa algebra over
\mathscr{O}_{L}
, corresponding to
F_{\eta}
under the Mahler transform.

Lemma 5.9
.

We have
L(f_{\eta},s)=-\eta(-1)L(\eta,s).
Hence for
k\geq 0
we have

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}x^{k}\cdot\mu_{\eta}=L(\eta,-k).

Proof.

The first statement is proved in a similar manner to Lemma
5.5
. The second is proved by equating
\partial
with
d/dt
and using the general theory described in Theorem
2.4
.
∎

The following is the analogue of Lemma
4.7
.

Lemma 5.10
.

We have
\psi(F_{\eta})=\eta(p)F_{\eta}
.

Proof.

We show first that

(5.4)

\frac{1}{p}\sum_{\xi\in\mu_{p}}\frac{1}{(1+T)\xi\varepsilon_{D}^{c}-1}=\frac{1}{(1+T)^{p}\varepsilon_{D}^{pc}-1}.

Expanding each summand as a geometric series, the left hand side is

\frac{-1}{p}\sum_{\xi\in\mu_{p}}\sum_{n\geq 0}(1+T)^{n}\varepsilon_{D}^{nc}\xi^{n}=-\sum_{n\geq 0}(1+T)^{pn}\varepsilon_{D}^{pcn},

and summing the geometric series now gives the right hand side of (
5.4
). It follows that

\displaystyle(\varphi\circ\psi)(F_{\eta})

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{pG(\eta)^{-1}}\sum_{\xi\in\mu_{p}}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/D{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\frac{\eta(c)^{-1}}{(1+T)\xi\varepsilon_{D}^{c}-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{G(\eta^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/D{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\frac{\eta(c)^{-1}}{(1+T)^{p}\varepsilon_{D}^{pc}-1}

\displaystyle=\eta(p)\varphi(F_{\eta}).

The result now follows from the injectivity of
\varphi
.
∎

We can now show the interpolation property at powers of
x
.

Lemma 5.11
.

We have

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k}\cdot\mu_{\eta}=\big(1-\eta(p)p^{k}\big)L(\eta,-k).

Proof.

By Lemma
5.10
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}(\mu_{\eta})

\displaystyle=(1-\varphi\circ\psi)(\mu_{\eta})=\mu_{\eta}-\eta(p)\varphi(\mu_{\eta}),

and

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}x^{k}\cdot\varphi(\mu_{\eta})=p^{k}\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}x^{k}\cdot\mu_{\eta}.

The result now follows from Lemma
5.9
.
∎

Now let
\chi
be a Dirichlet character of conductor
p^{n}
for some
n\geq 0
, and let
\theta\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\chi\eta
the product (a Dirichlet character of conductor
Dp^{n}
). For such
\theta=\chi\eta
, we define

(5.5)

\mu_{\theta}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\mu_{\eta})_{\chi}.

Using Lemma
5.4
, we find easily that:

Lemma 5.12
.

The Mahler transform of
\mu_{\theta}
is

F_{\theta}(T)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathscr{A}_{\mu_{\theta}}(T)=\frac{-1}{G(\theta^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/Dp^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\frac{\theta(c)^{-1}}{(1+T)\varepsilon_{Dp^{n}}^{c}-1}.

Via a calculation essentially identical to the cases already seen, we can prove

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\chi(x)x^{k}\cdot\mu_{\eta}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}x^{k}\cdot\mu_{\theta}=L(\theta,-k),

and that

(5.6)

\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}(\mu_{\theta})=\big(1-\theta(p)\varphi\big)\mu_{\theta}.

(Here, note that if
\chi
is non-trivial, then
\mu_{\theta}
is already supported on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
; but this is consistent, as
\theta(p)=0
in this case).
Combining the above we find

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi(x)x^{k}\cdot\mu_{\eta}=\big(1-\theta(p)p^{k}\big)L(\theta,-k).

Finally, to complete the proof of Theorem
5.7
and to ensure compatibility with the construction of
\zeta_{p}
, we introduce a shift by 1. The following is directly analogous to the construction of
\zeta_{p}
; note again that
\zeta_{\eta}
is truly a measure, not a pseudo-measure.

Definition 5.13
.

Define
\zeta_{\eta}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=x^{-1}\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}(\mu_{\eta}).

We see that

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi(x)x^{k}\cdot\zeta_{\eta}=\big(1-\theta(p)p^{k-1}\big)L(\theta,1-k).

which completes the proof of Theorem
5.7
.
∎

5.3
Analytic functions on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
via the Mellin transform

The reader should hopefully now be convinced that measures are a natural language with which to discuss
p
-adic
L
-functions. In this subsection, we use this (more powerful) language to answer the question we originally posed in the introduction: namely, we define analytic functions on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
interpolating the values
\zeta(1-k)
for
k>0
. In passing from measures to analytic functions on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, we lose the clean interpolation statements. In particular, there is no
single
analytic function on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
interpolating the values
\zeta(1-k)
for all
k>0
, but rather
p-1
different ‘branches’ of the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, each interpolating a different range.

The reason we cannot define a single
p
-adic
L
-function on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
is down to the following technicality. We’d
like
to be able to define “
\zeta_{p}(s)=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{-s}\cdot\zeta_{p}
” for
s\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. The natural way to define the exponential
x\mapsto x^{s}
is as

x^{s}=\mathrm{exp}(s\cdot\mathrm{log}(x)),

but unfortunately in the
p
-adic world the exponential map does not converge on all of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, so this is not well-defined for general
x\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. Instead:

Lemma 5.14
.

The
p
-adic exponential map converges on
p\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. Hence, for any
s\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, the function
1+p\mathbf{Z}_{p}\rightarrow\mathbf{Z}_{p}
given by
x\mapsto x^{s}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{\exp}(s\cdot\mathrm{log}(x))
is well-defined.

Proof.

This is a standard result in the theory of local fields; see e.g.
[
Cas86
, §12]
.
∎

Definition 5.15
.

Recall that we assume
p
to be odd and that we have a decomposition
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}\cong\mu_{p-1}\times(1+p\mathbf{Z}_{p})
. Let

\displaystyle\omega:\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mu_{p-1},

\displaystyle\langle\cdot\rangle:\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 1+p\mathbf{Z}_{p},

where
\omega(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=
Teichmüller lift of the reduction modulo
p
of
x
and
\langle x\rangle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\omega^{-1}(x)x
denote the projections to the first and second factors respectively. If
x\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, then we can write
x=\omega(x)\langle x\rangle
.

By Lemma
5.14
, the function
x\mapsto\langle x\rangle^{s}
is well-defined for any
s\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. For each
i=1,..,p-1
we can define an injection

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}_{p}

\displaystyle\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times},\mathbf{C}_{p}^{\times})

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\longmapsto\big[x\mapsto\omega(x)^{i}\langle x\rangle^{s}\big],

and hence we can define a meromorphic function as follows.

Definition 5.16
.

We define the
i
-th branch of the
p
-adic zeta function as

\displaystyle\zeta_{p,i}:\mathbf{Z}_{p}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}_{p}

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\longmapsto\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\omega(x)^{i}\langle x\rangle^{1-s}\cdot\zeta_{p}.

This function
does not
interpolate as wide a range of values as the measure
\zeta_{p}
, since the character
x^{k}
can be written in the form
\omega(x)^{i}\langle x\rangle^{k}
if and only if
k\equiv i\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp-1)
, and in this case
x^{k}
is the value of
\omega(x)^{i}\langle x\rangle^{1-s}
at the value
s=1-k
. Then we have the following result.

Theorem 5.17
.

For all
k\geq 1
with
k\equiv i\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp-1)
, we have

\zeta_{p,i}(1-k)=(1-p^{k-1})\zeta(1-k).

Note that the above theorem implies that
\zeta_{p,i}
is identically zero whenever
i
is odd (as by Corollary
2.8
the value
\zeta(1-k)
is zero for every odd positive integer
k\geq 1
). More generally, we can twist by Dirichlet characters as we have done before.

Definition 5.18
.

Let
\theta=\chi\eta
be a Dirichlet character, where
\eta
has conductor
D
prime to
p
and
\chi
has conductor
p^{n}
for
n\geq 0
. Define

L_{p}(\theta,s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi(x)\langle x\rangle^{1-s}\cdot\zeta_{\eta},\hskip 12.0pts\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}.

Remark 5.19
.

An equivalent definition is

(5.7)

L_{p}(\theta,s)=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi\omega^{-1}(x)\langle x\rangle^{-s}\cdot\mu_{\eta}=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi\omega^{s-1}(x)x^{-s}\cdot\mu_{\eta}.

Note here we use the measure
\mu_{\eta}
, rather than the (shifted-by-1) analogue
\zeta_{\eta}
.
In
[
Was97
]
, the analytic functions
L_{p}(\theta,s)
are constructed directly without using measures, and the more direct approach differs from the one obtained using our measure-theoretic approach by precisely this factor of
\omega
. This twist by 1 also appears naturally when we study the Iwasawa Main Conjecture.

Theorem 5.20
.

For all
k\geq 1
, we have

L_{p}(\theta,1-k)=\big(1-\theta\omega^{-k}(p)p^{k-1}\big)L(\theta\omega^{-k},1-k).

Proof.

We use the description of (
5.7
). From the definitions, we have

\chi\omega^{-1}(x)\langle x\rangle^{k-1}=\chi\omega^{-k}(x)\cdot\omega^{k-1}(x)\langle x\rangle^{k-1}=\chi\omega^{-k}(x)x^{k-1},

so that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi(x)\langle x\rangle^{k-1}\cdot\mu_{\eta}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi\omega^{-k}(x)x^{k-1}\cdot\mu_{\eta}

\displaystyle=\big(1-\theta\omega^{-k}(p)p^{k-1}\big)L(\theta\omega^{-k},1-k),

as required. The last equality here is Lemma
5.11
.
∎

Remark 5.21
.

Directly from the definitions, we have
\zeta_{p,i}(s)=L_{p}(\omega^{i},s)
. Hence for arbitrary
k>0
, Theorem
5.20
gives

\zeta_{p,i}(1-k)=(1-\omega^{i-k}(p)p^{k-1})L(\omega^{i-k},1-k).

Of course,
\omega^{i-k}
is just the trivial character when
i\equiv k\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp-1)
, so we recover Theorem
5.17
from Theorem
5.20
.

In general, for any measure
\mu
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
one can define

\mathrm{Mel}_{\mu,i}(s)=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\omega(x)^{i}\langle x\rangle^{s}\cdot\mu,

the
Mellin transform
of
\mu
at
i
. We have then
\zeta_{p,i}(s)=\mathrm{Mel}_{\zeta_{p},i}(1-s)
. This transform gives a way to pass from
p
-adic measures on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
to analytic functions on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
.

Remark 5.22
.

The results of this section are simply a more concrete version of Remark

3.47
. There we described how a measure (resp. pseudo-measure)
\mu
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
gives rise to a rigid analytic (resp. rigid meromorphic) function
F_{\mu}
on weight space
\mathcal{W}(\mathbf{C}_{p})
. The function
\zeta_{p,i}
above corresponds to the restriction of
F_{\mu}
to the open ball
U_{\omega^{i}}\subset\mathcal{W}(\mathbf{Z}_{p})
(again explaining why we need
p-1
such functions, corresponding to the
p-1
disjoint open balls).

6
The values at
s=1

In the following we give an example of further remarkable links between the classical and
p
-adic zeta functions. Let
\theta
be a non-trivial Dirichlet character, which as usual we write in the form
\chi\eta
, where
\chi
has conductor
p^{n}
and
\eta
has conductor
D
prime to
p
. By Theorem
5.7
, for any integer
k>0
, we have

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi(x)x^{k}\cdot\zeta_{\eta}=L(\theta,1-k).

It’s natural to ask what happens outside the range of interpolation
k>0
. In particular, what happens when we take
k=0
? Since this is
outside
the range of interpolation, this value may
a priori
have nothing to do with classical
L
-values. Indeed, the classical value
L(\theta,1)
is transcendental
10
10

10

This follows from Baker’s theorem and Theorem
6.1
, part (i).
, so one cannot see it as a
p
-adic number in a natural way. However, even though we cannot directly equate the two values, it turns out that there is a formula for the
p
-adic
L
-function at
s=1
which is strikingly similar to its classical analogue.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
\theta
be a non-trivial Dirichlet character of conductor
N
, and let
\varepsilon_{N}
denote a primitive
N
th root of unity. Then:

(i)

(Classical value at
s=1
). We have

L(\theta,1)=-\frac{1}{G(\theta^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\theta^{-1}(c)\log\big(1-\varepsilon_{N}^{c}\big).

(ii)

(
p
-adic value at
s=1
). We have

L_{p}(\theta,1)=-\big(1-\theta(p)p^{-1}\big)\frac{1}{G(\theta^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\theta^{-1}(c)\log_{p}\big(1-\varepsilon_{N}^{c}).

Remark 6.2
.

Part
(ii)
of Theorem
6.1
is due to Leopoldt. Values in the range of interpolation, where the link to classical
L
-values is explicit, are often called
critical
values. Values outside this range, such as those studied in Theorem
6.1
, are called
non-critical
values. The above result is an instance of the
p
-adic Beilinson or Perrin-Riou conjectures, which give arithmetic descriptions of non-critical special values of
p
-adic
L
-functions. We refer the interested reader to
[
PR95a
]
(or Remark
6.8
below) for more details on this.

If
\theta
is an odd character, both sides of the
p
-adic formula vanish. In any case, the formulae are identical up to replacing
\log
with its
p
-adic avatar and, as usual, deleting the Euler factor at
p
. This result can be used to prove a
p
-adic analogue of the class number formula.

6.1
The complex value at
s=1

For completeness, we prove the complex case of Theorem
6.1
, following
[
Was97
, Thm. 4.9]
.

Proof.

(Theorem
6.1
(i)).

Write

L(\theta,s)=\sum_{a\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\theta(a)\sum_{n\equiv a\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptD)}n^{-s}.

Using the fact that

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})}\varepsilon_{N}^{(a-n)c}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c l}0&:\text{ if $n\not\equiv a$ mod $N$}\\
1&:\text{ if $n\equiv a$ mod $N$,}\end{array}\right.

we show that the above formula equals

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\theta(a)\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})}\varepsilon_{N}^{(a-n)c}n^{-s}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})}\left(\sum_{a\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\theta(a)\varepsilon_{N}^{ac}\right)\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{\varepsilon_{N}^{-nc}}{n^{s}}

\displaystyle=\frac{G(\theta)}{N}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})}\theta^{-1}(c)\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{\varepsilon_{N}^{-nc}}{n^{s}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta(-1)}{G(\theta^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\theta^{-1}(c)\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{\varepsilon_{N}^{-nc}}{n^{s}}

(6.1)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{G(\theta^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\theta^{-1}(c)\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{\varepsilon_{N}^{nc}}{n^{s}}.

Here the penultimate equality uses the standard identity
G(\theta)G(\theta^{-1})=\theta(-1)\mathrm{cond}(\theta)
of Gauss sums (cf. Remark
5.3
(i)) and that
\theta^{-1}(c)=0
if
(c,N)\neq 1
, and the last equality follows from the change of variables
c\mapsto-c
.

Finally we evaluate this expression at
s=1
. As
\theta
is not trivial we have
N>1
, so
\varepsilon_{N}^{c}\neq 1
for any
c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}
. Thus we may consider the Taylor series expansion

-\log(1-\varepsilon_{N}^{c})=\sum_{n\geq 1}\varepsilon_{N}^{nc}n^{-1}.

Substituting this into (
6.1
), we see the series converges at
s=1
to the required result.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

We can further refine this expression depending on the parity of
\theta
. If
\theta
is even then
\theta^{-1}(c)\log(1-\varepsilon_{N}^{c})+\theta^{-1}(-c)\log(1-\varepsilon_{N}^{-c})=2\theta^{-1}(c)\log|1-\varepsilon_{N}^{c}|
, so, rearranging

L(\theta,1)=-\frac{1}{G(\theta^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\theta^{-1}(c)\log|1-\varepsilon^{c}|.

If
\theta
is odd, we can use the functional equation to obtain

L(\theta,1)=-i\pi\frac{1}{G(\theta^{-1})}B_{1,\theta^{-1}},

where
B_{k,\theta^{-1}}
denotes the
k
-th twisted Bernoulli number (see
[
Was97
, Chapter 4]
).

6.2
The
p
-adic value at
s=1

We now compute

(6.2)

\displaystyle L_{p}(\theta,1)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}}\chi(x)x^{-1}\cdot\mu_{\eta}=\int_{{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}}x^{-1}\cdot\mu_{\theta}.

A tempting argument to study this goes as follows. Suppose
k
is divisible by
p-1
. We know from Equation (
5.7
) that

L_{p}(\theta,1-k)=\int_{{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}}\chi(x)x^{k-1}\cdot\mu_{\eta},

noting that
\omega^{(1-k)-1}=\omega^{-k}=1
as
\omega
has order
p-1
. Now if
k>0
, we showed above that

\int_{{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}}\chi(x)x^{k-1}\cdot\mu_{\eta}=\mathscr{A}_{\mathrm{Res}_{{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}}(x^{k-1}\mu_{\theta})}(0)=(1-\theta(p)p^{k-1})\big(\partial^{k-1}F_{\theta}\big)(0).

We want to compute this for
k=0
. Identically we could try to argue that

(6.3)

L_{p}(\theta,1)=\mathscr{A}_{\mathrm{Res}_{{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}}(x^{-1}\mu_{\theta})}(0)=(1-\theta(p)p^{-1})\big(\partial^{-1}F_{\theta}\big)(0).

Then, recalling that by Lemma
5.12
we have

F_{\theta}(T)=-\frac{1}{G(\theta^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\frac{\theta(c)^{-1}}{(1+T)\varepsilon_{N}^{c}-1},

we observe that, if we define

\widetilde{F}_{\theta}(T)=-\frac{1}{G(\theta^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\theta^{-1}(c)\log\big((1+T)\varepsilon_{N}^{c}-1\big),

then formally
\partial\widetilde{F}_{\theta}=F_{\theta}
; we will show this in the proof of Lemma
6.5
below. In particular,
\widetilde{F}_{\theta}
is a good candidate for
\partial^{-1}F_{\theta}
. Plugging in
T=0
and combining with (
6.3
) would give the claimed value of
L_{p}(\theta,1)
.

In order to make this reasoning rigorous, one needs to deal with the fact that
x^{-1}
is not a well-defined operation on measures on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, rendering
x^{-1}\mu_{\theta}
ill-defined. On power series, this is captured by the indeterminacy in defining
\partial^{-1}
. In particular,
\widetilde{F}_{\theta}(T)
is
not
necessarily a bounded power series, so under the Mahler correspondence, does not correspond to a
p
-adic measure. However we do have the following. Recall that
\mathscr{R}^{+}
(from Remark
3.39
) denotes the space of power series
\sum a_{n}T^{n}
such that
|a_{n}|r^{n}\to 0
for any
0\leq r<1
.

Lemma 6.4
.

The power series
\widetilde{F}_{\theta}(T)
is an element of
\mathscr{R}^{+}
.

Proof.

We can write

\displaystyle\log((1+T)\varepsilon_{N}^{c}-1)

\displaystyle=\log_{p}(\varepsilon_{N}^{c}-1)+\log\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon_{N}^{c}T}{\varepsilon_{N}^{c}-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log_{p}(\varepsilon_{N}^{c}-1)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n}\cdot\frac{\varepsilon_{N}^{cn}}{(\varepsilon_{N}^{c}-1)^{n}}T^{n}.

We now consider two cases. If
(N,p)=1
, we know that
(\varepsilon_{N}^{c}-1)
is a
p
-adic unit; then the coefficient of
T^{n}
has
p
-adic valuation bounded below by
-v_{p}(n)
. This means the coefficients in
\widetilde{F}_{\theta}(T)
have logarithmic growth, and in particular
\widetilde{F}_{\theta}(T)\in\mathscr{R}^{+}
. More generally, suppose
N=Dp^{n}
with
(D,p)=1
. We write
\theta=\eta\chi
, with
\eta
and
\chi
characters of conductors
D
and
p^{n}
respectively. Then, as in Lemma
5.4
or Lemma
5.12
, we have

\widetilde{F}_{\theta}(T)=(\widetilde{F}_{\eta})_{\chi}(T)=-\frac{1}{G(\chi^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\chi^{-1}(c)\widetilde{F}_{\eta}((1+T)\varepsilon_{p^{n}}^{c}-1).

As
\widetilde{F}_{\eta}(T)\in\mathscr{R}^{+}
, the same holds for
\widetilde{F}_{\theta}(T)
.
∎

By Theorem
3.43
,
\widetilde{F}_{\theta}(T)
is the Mahler transform of a locally analytic distribution
\widetilde{\mu}_{\theta}
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. We now relate this distribution to
x^{-1}\mathrm{Res}_{{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}}(\mu_{\theta})
, as appeared in (
6.2
).

Lemma 6.5
.

We have

x\widetilde{\mu}_{\theta}=\mu_{\theta}.

In particular,

\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}(\widetilde{\mu}_{\theta})=x^{-1}\mathrm{Res}_{{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}}(\mu_{\theta}).

Proof.

The first equality can be checked on Mahler transforms. By Lemma
3.29
, this means showing

(6.4)

\partial\widetilde{F}_{\theta}(T)=(1+T)\frac{d}{dT}\widetilde{F}_{\theta}(T)=\mathscr{A}_{\mu_{\theta}}(T).

By Lemma
5.12
, we have

\mathscr{A}_{\mu_{\theta}}(T)=F_{\theta}(T)=\frac{-1}{G(\theta^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\frac{\theta(c)^{-1}}{(1+T)\varepsilon_{N}^{c}-1}.

Then (
6.4
) follows immediately from the formula

\partial\log\big((1+T)\varepsilon_{D}^{c}-1\big)=\frac{(1+T)\varepsilon_{D}^{c}}{(1+T)\varepsilon_{D}^{c}-1}=1+\frac{1}{(1+T)\varepsilon_{D}^{c}-1}

and the fact that
\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/D{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\theta^{-1}(c)=0
.

To see the second equality, note that as measures on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, we visibly have
x\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}(\widetilde{\mu}_{\theta})=\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}(\mu_{\theta})
. It follows that
\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}(\widetilde{\mu}_{\theta})=x^{-1}\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}(\mu_{\theta})
as ‘multiplication by
x
’ is an invertible operator on measures/distributions on
{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}
.
∎

From the above, we now know that

(6.5)

L_{p}(\theta,1)=\mathscr{A}_{x^{-1}\mathrm{Res}_{{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}}(\mu_{\theta})}(0)=\mathscr{A}_{\mathrm{Res}_{{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}}(\widetilde{\mu}_{\theta})}(0)=\Big((1-\varphi\circ\psi)\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\Big)(0),

where the last equality follows from the formula for the restriction of a distribution to
{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}
(cf. Equation (
3.8
), and Remark
3.45
). We are now ready to prove Theorem
6.1
(ii).

Proof.

(Theorem
6.1
(ii))
.
We compute the right-hand side of (
6.5
). Recall from §
3.5.5
that
\varphi\circ\psi(\widetilde{F}_{\theta})
is the Mahler transform of
\mathrm{Res}_{p\mathbf{Z}_{p}}(\widetilde{\mu}_{\theta})
. Recall that
N=Dp^{n}
, and
\theta=\eta\chi
, where
\eta
has prime-to-
p
conductor
D
, and
\chi
has conductor
p^{n}
. To compute this we break into two cases.

(1)

First assume that
n>1
, so that
\chi\neq 1
; then, as
\chi|_{p\mathbf{Z}_{p}}=0
, we see
\widetilde{\mu}_{\theta}=(\widetilde{\mu}_{\eta})_{\chi}
is automatically supported on
{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}
by (
5.1
). In particular,
\mathrm{Res}_{p\mathbf{Z}_{p}}(\widetilde{\mu}_{\theta})=0
, and thus
\varphi\circ\psi(\widetilde{F}_{\theta})=0
. In particular, in this case

L_{p}(\theta,1)=\widetilde{F}_{\theta}(0).

It is convenient to write this in the form
L_{p}(\theta,1)=(1-\theta(p)p^{-1})\widetilde{F}_{\theta}(0)
using that
\theta(p)=0
.

(2)

Now assume
n=0
, so
N=D
is coprime to
p
and hence
\theta=\eta
. By (
3.9
), we know

\displaystyle\varphi\circ\psi(\widetilde{F}_{\theta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{\xi\in\mu_{p}}\widetilde{F}_{\theta}((1+T)\xi-1)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{G(\theta^{-1})}\cdot\frac{1}{p}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\theta^{-1}(c)\sum_{\xi\in\mu_{p}}\log_{p}((1+T)\xi\varepsilon_{N}^{c}-1).

Evaluating at
T=0
, we get

\displaystyle\varphi\circ\psi(\widetilde{F}_{\theta})(0)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{G(\theta^{-1})}\cdot\frac{1}{p}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\theta^{-1}(c)\sum_{\xi\in\mu_{p}}\log_{p}(\xi\varepsilon_{N}^{c}-1)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{G(\theta^{-1})}\cdot\frac{1}{p}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\theta^{-1}(c)\log_{p}(\varepsilon_{N}^{pc}-1)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{G(\theta^{-1})}\cdot\frac{\theta(p)}{p}\sum_{c^{\prime}\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\theta^{-1}(c^{\prime})\log_{p}(\varepsilon_{N}^{c^{\prime}}-1)

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta(p)}{p}\widetilde{F}_{\theta}(0).

Here, we used that, since
p\nmid N
, the assignment
c\mapsto c^{\prime}=pc
defines an automorphism of
({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}
. Hence in this case we also find that

\displaystyle L_{p}(\theta,1)

\displaystyle=\Big((1-\varphi\circ\psi)\widetilde{F}_{\theta}\Big)(0)=\Big(1-\theta(p)p^{-1}\Big)\widetilde{F}_{\theta}(0).

To complete the proof, we simply evaluate the expression
\widetilde{F}_{\theta}(0)
(and use that
\log_{p}(x)=\log_{p}(-x)
for
x\in\mathbf{C}_{p}^{\times}
) to find, for all
N
, that

\displaystyle L_{p}(\theta,1)

\displaystyle=-\Big(1-\theta(p)p^{-1}\Big)\frac{1}{G(\theta^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\theta^{-1}(c)\log_{p}\big(1-\varepsilon_{N}^{c}),

as claimed.
∎

Remark 6.6
.

Theorem
6.1
has been generalised by Coleman in
[
Col82
]
for every positive integer value
s=k\geq 1
. More precisely, for
s,z\in{\mathbf{C}}
, let
\mathrm{Li}_{s}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{z^{n}}{n^{s}}
be the polylogarithm fuction; recall that it admits a unique analytic continuation to
{\mathbf{C}}\backslash\{z\in{\mathbf{R}}:z\geq 1\}
. In particular, one sees that
\mathrm{Li}_{s}(1)=\zeta(s)
, and
\mathrm{Li}_{1}(z)=-\log(1-z)
. Coleman constructed
p
-adic analogues
\mathrm{Li}_{k,p}(z)
, which are locally analytic functions on
{\mathbf{C}}_{p}\backslash\{1\}
, and showed:

Theorem 6.7

(
[
Col82
]
)
.

Let
\theta
be a non-trivial Dirichlet character of conductor
N
, let
k\geq 1
be an integer, and let
\varepsilon_{N}
denote a primitive
N
th root of unity. Then:

(i)

(Classical value at
s=k
). We have

L(\theta,k)=\frac{1}{G(\theta^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\theta^{-1}(c)\mathrm{Li}_{k}(\varepsilon_{N}^{c}).

(ii)

(
p
-adic value at
s=k
). We have

L_{p}(\theta,k)=\big(1-\theta(p)p^{-k}\big)\frac{1}{G(\theta^{-1})}\sum_{c\in({\mathbf{Z}}/N{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}}\theta^{-1}(c)\mathrm{Li}_{k,p}(\varepsilon_{N}^{c}).

Remark 6.8
.

We highlighted in Remark
6.2
that Theorem
6.1
(ii) is an instance of Perrin-Riou’s
p
-adic Beilinson conjectures. More precisely, her conjectures describe special, non-critical values of
p
-adic
L
-functions of motives in terms of arithmetic data. Specialised to the case of the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function (see
[
PR95b
, §4.3.3]
), this gives formulas for the values of
L_{p}(\theta,k)
in terms of
p
-adic regulators of the cyclotomic units, special elements that we will see later; the right-hand sides of Theorems
6.1
(ii) and
6.7
(ii) can be reinterpreted in such terms, justifying Remark
6.2
. We refer to
[
PR95a
]
for more details, and
[
Col00
]
for an excellent survey on all of this.

We also mention the pioneering work of Gross
[
Gro90
]
, based on Coleman’s work, as well as
[
KNQD98
]
, for another approach expressing values of Dirichlet
p
-adic
L
-functions at odd positive integers in terms of syntomic regulators and
K
-theory.

7
The residue of
\zeta_{p}
at
s=1

In the previous section, we described the value of the Dirichlet
p
-adic
L
-functions
L_{p}(\theta,s)
at
s=1
for a non-trivial Dirichlet character
\theta
. We now turn to the value at
s=1
of the
untwisted

p
-adic zeta function, that is, the analogue when
\theta
is trivial. Recall the Riemann zeta function has a simple pole at
s=1
with residue
1
and that, in the
p
-adic world, we have defined the
p
-adic zeta function as a pseudo-measure (rather than a measure) which implies, as explained in Remark
3.47
, that there might be a potential pole at the trivial character. We now show that there is indeed a simple pole here, and calculate its residue.

As with
L_{p}(\theta,s)
, it is convenient to use the language of analytic functions
\zeta_{p,i}
from Definition
5.16
. The behaviour of
\zeta_{p}
at the trivial character is captured by the behaviour of
\zeta_{p,p-1}(s)
at
s=1
. The main result of this section is the following.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,p-1\}
. The following assertions hold:

(i)

If
i\neq p-1
, then
\zeta_{p,i}
is analytic at
s=1
.

(ii)

The function
\zeta_{p,p-1}
has a simple pole at
s=1
with residue
(1-p^{-1})
.

The proof will occupy the rest of this section.

For any
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,p-1\}
, by Definition
5.16
we have

\displaystyle\zeta_{p,i}(s)=\int_{Z_{p}^{\times}}\omega(x)^{i}\langle x\rangle^{1-s}\cdot\zeta_{p}.

Now, from Definition
4.10
, we have

\zeta_{p}=\frac{x^{-1}\mathrm{Res}_{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}(\mu_{a})}{\theta_{a}}=\frac{x^{-1}\mathrm{Res}_{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}(\mu_{a})}{[a]-[1]},

where
a
is any topological generator of
{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}
. Thus by the expression (
3.11
) for evaluating pseudo-measures, we find

\displaystyle\zeta_{p,i}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}\omega(x)^{i}\langle x\rangle^{1-s}x^{-1}\cdot\mu_{a}}{\omega(a)^{i}\langle a\rangle^{1-s}-1}

(7.1)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}\omega(x)^{s+i-1}x^{-s}\cdot\mu_{a}}{\omega(a)^{i}\langle a\rangle^{1-s}-1},

where we have used
\langle x\rangle=\omega^{-1}(x)x
in the second equality. Let

g_{a,i}(s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\omega(a)^{i}\langle a\rangle^{1-s}-1

be the denominator of (
7.1
).

Lemma 7.2
.

The following assertions hold.

(i)

If
i\neq p-1
, then
g_{a,i}(1)\neq 0
. In particular, Theorem
7.1
(i) holds.

(ii)

We have
g_{a,p-1}(1)=0
, and

\lim_{s\to 1}(s-1)^{-1}g_{a,p-1}(s)=-\log_{p}(a).

Proof.

Since
a
is a topological generator of
{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}
, we see
\omega(a)
is a primitive
(p-1)
-th root of unity. Hence the denominator
\omega(a)^{i}\langle a\rangle^{1-s}-1
of equation (
7.1
) vanishes at
s=1
if and only
i=p-1
. This already implies Theorem
7.1
(i), as the expression (
7.1
) does not have a pole at
s=1
.

If
i=p-1
, we know
\omega(a)^{i}=1
, so
g_{a,p-1}(1)=0
. Moreover

\displaystyle g_{a,p-1}(s)=\omega(a)^{p-1}\langle a\rangle^{1-s}-1

\displaystyle=\langle a\rangle^{1-s}-1

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq 1}{{1-s}\choose n}(\langle a\rangle-1)^{n}

(7.2)

\displaystyle=(1-s)\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n}{{-s}\choose{n-1}}(\langle a\rangle-1)^{n},

where in the last equality we have used the identity
{{1-s}\choose n}=\frac{1-s}{n}{{-s}\choose{n-1}}
for
n\geq 1
. The sum in (
7.2
) evaluates at
s=1
to

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n}{{-1}\choose{n-1}}(\langle a\rangle-1)^{n}=\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n}(\langle a\rangle-1)^{n}

\displaystyle=\log_{p}(\langle a\rangle)=\log_{p}(a),

where we have used
{{-1}\choose{n-1}}=(-1)^{n-1}
(direct from Definition
3.20
).
We deduce that

\lim_{s\to 1}[(s-1)^{-1}g_{a,s-1}(s)]=-\log_{p}(a),

as claimed.
∎

Combining Lemma
7.2
(ii) with (
7.1
), we deduce

(7.3)

\lim_{s\to 1}(s-1)\zeta_{p,p-1}(s)=-\frac{\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{-1}\cdot\mu_{a}}{\log_{p}(a)}.

We calculate the numerator in this expression via similar methods to those used in §
6.2
. Recall that
F_{a}(T)=\frac{1}{T}-\frac{a}{(1+T)^{a}-1}
is the Mahler transform of
\mu_{a}
; we find a power series
\widetilde{F}_{a}(T)
such that
\partial\widetilde{F}_{a}(T)=F_{a}(T)
, where
\partial=(1+T)\frac{d}{dT}
. Then, via Lemma
6.5
and directly analogously to (
6.5
), we find

(7.4)

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{-1}\mu_{a}=\Big((1-\varphi\circ\psi)\widetilde{F}_{a}\Big)(0).

To this end, let

\widetilde{F}_{a}(T)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\log\left(\frac{T}{1+T}\cdot\frac{(1+T)^{a}}{(1+T)^{a}-1}\right).

Lemma 7.3
.

Formally, we have

\partial\widetilde{F}_{a}(T)=F_{a}(T).

Proof.

We let the reader check that

\partial\log\left(\frac{(1+T)^{a}-1}{(1+T)^{a}}\right)=\frac{a}{(1+T)^{a}-1}.

In particular, taking
a=1
, we also get

\partial\log\left(\frac{T}{1+T}\right)=\frac{1}{T}.

We conclude as

\displaystyle\partial\widetilde{F}_{a}(T)

\displaystyle=\partial\log\left(\frac{T}{1+T}\right)-\partial\log\left(\frac{(1+T)^{a}-1}{(1+T)^{a}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}-\frac{a}{(1+T)^{a}-1}=F_{a}(T).\qed

As in Lemma
6.4
, we must also check
\widetilde{F}_{a}(T)\in\mathscr{R}^{+}
to use the Mahler correspondence.

Lemma 7.4
.

We have
\widetilde{F}_{a}(T)\in\mathscr{R}^{+}
.

Proof.

It is convenient to note

(7.5)

\widetilde{F}_{a}(T)=\log\left(\frac{T}{(1+T)^{a}-1}\cdot(1+T)^{a-1}\right).

As in Proposition
4.4
, write

(1+T)^{a}-1=aT(1+Tg(T)),\hskip 20.00003ptg(T)=\sum_{n\geq 2}a^{-1}{a\choose n}T^{n-2}.

Recall from the proof of that proposition that we have

(7.6)

\frac{1}{(1+T)^{a}-1}=\frac{1}{aT}(1+Th(T)),

with

h(T)=\sum_{n\geq 1}(-1)^{n}T^{n-1}g(T)^{n}\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!].

We thus see that

\frac{T}{(1+T)^{a}-1}=a^{-1}(1+Th(T)),

whose logarithm is given by

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{T}{(1+T)^{a}-1}\right)

\displaystyle=-\log_{p}(a)+\log(1+Th(T))

(7.7)

\displaystyle=-\log_{p}(a)+\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{(-1)^{n+1}}{n}T^{n}h(T)^{n}.

As in Lemma
6.4
, the coefficients here have logarithmic growth in
n
, so this lies in
\mathscr{R}^{+}
. Identically,
(1+T)^{a-1}=1+T\sum_{n\geq 1}{{a-1}\choose n}T^{n-1}
also has well defined logarithm in
\mathscr{R}^{+}
. Adding these two elements of
\mathscr{R}^{+}
yields
\widetilde{F}_{a}(T)
and completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

We have
\big((1-\varphi\circ\psi)\widetilde{F}_{a}\big)(0)=-(1-p^{-1})\log_{p}(a)
.

Proof.

First, by (
7.5
) we know that

(7.8)

\widetilde{F}_{a}(0)=\log\left(\frac{T}{(1+T)^{a}-1}\right)\Big|_{T=0}+\log\left((1+T)^{a-1}\right)|_{T=0}=-\log_{p}(a)+0,

where we use (
7.7
) to evaluate the first summand. Secondly, we have

\displaystyle\varphi\circ\psi(\widetilde{F}_{a})(T)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{\xi\in\mu_{p}}\widetilde{F}_{a}((1+T)\xi-1)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{\xi\in\mu_{p}}\log\left(\frac{(1+T)\xi-1}{(1+T)\xi}\cdot\frac{(1+T)^{a}\xi^{a}}{(1+T)^{a}\xi^{a}-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{\xi\in\mu_{p}}\log\left(\frac{(1+T)\xi-1}{(1+T)^{a}\xi^{a}-1}\cdot(1+T)^{a-1}\xi^{a-1}\right).

This rearranges to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\log\left(\prod_{\xi\in\mu_{p}}\frac{(1+T)\xi-1}{(1+T)^{a}\xi^{a}-1}\cdot(1+T)^{a-1}\xi^{a-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\log\left(\frac{(1+T)^{p}-1}{(1+T)^{ap}-1}\cdot(1+T)^{(a-1)p}\right).

Here we simplify both terms of the fraction using
\prod_{\xi\in\mu_{p}}(X\xi-1)=X^{p}-1
, in the denominator noting that as
a
is a topological generator of
{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}
, we have
\{\xi^{a}:\xi\in\mu_{p}\}=\mu_{p}
. In the final term we use that
\prod_{\xi\in\mu_{p}}\xi^{a-1}=(\prod_{\xi\in\mu_{p}}\xi)^{a-1}=1
.

Writing

(1+T)^{p}-1=pT(1+Tj(T)),\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{1}{(1+T)^{ap}-1}=\frac{1}{apT}(1+Tk(T))

analogously to (
7.6
), we find ultimately that

\varphi\circ\psi(\widetilde{F}_{a})(T)=\frac{1}{p}\log\left(\frac{1}{a}(1+Tj(T))(1+Tk(T))\cdot(1+T)^{(a-1)p}\right),

and the right-hand side at
T=0
collapses to
-p^{-1}\log_{p}(a)
. Combining with (
7.8
),

\Big((1-\varphi\circ\psi)\widetilde{F}_{a}\Big)(0)=\widetilde{F}_{a}(0)-(\varphi\circ\psi)\widetilde{F}_{a}(0)=-\log_{p}(a)-p^{-1}\log_{p}(a)=-(1-p^{-1})\log_{p}(a),

as required.
∎

Combining Equations (
7.3
) and (
7.4
) with Lemma
7.5
, we deduce that

\lim_{s\to 1}(s-1)\zeta_{p,p-1}(s)=1-p^{-1},

completing the proof of Theorem
7.1
(ii). ∎

8
The
p
-adic family of Eisenstein series

We finally take a brief detour to illustrate another example of
p
-adic variation in number theory, namely the
p
-adic variation of modular forms. In constructing the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function, we have seen many of the key ideas that go into the simplest example of this, namely the
p
-adic family of Eisenstein series, which we will illustrate below.

Let
k\geq 4
be an even integer. The
Eisenstein series of level
k
, defined as

G_{k}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}c,d\in{\mathbf{Z}}\\
(c,d)\neq(0,0)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{(cz+d)^{k}},\hskip 12.0ptz\in\mathcal{H}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{z\in{\mathbf{C}}:\mathrm{Im}(z)>0\}

can be viewed as a two-dimensional analogue of the zeta value
\zeta(k)
. It is an example of a
modular form of weight
k
. In the classical theory of modular forms, one computes the normalised Fourier expansion of such an object to be

\displaystyle E_{k}(z)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{G_{k}(z)(k-1)!}{2\cdot(2\pi i)^{k}}=\frac{\zeta(1-k)}{2}+\sum_{n\geq 1}\sigma_{k-1}(n)q^{n},

where
\sigma_{k-1}(n)=\sum_{0<d|n}d^{k-1}
and
q=e^{2i\pi z}
. In particular, it is a power series with rational coefficients. (This is a standard exercise; see
[
DS05
, Chapter 1.1]
for details).

From the definition, we see the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function as a pseudo-measure that, when evaluated at
x^{k}
with
k\geq 4
even, gives (up to an Euler factor) the constant coefficient of the Eisenstein series of weight
k
. The idea now is to find measures giving similar interpolations of the other coefficients.
Fortunately, these are much easier to deal with: we only need interpolations of the functions
k\mapsto d^{k}
, where
k
is varying
p
-adically. When
d
is coprime to
p
, we do this by viewing
d
as an element of
{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}
and considering the Dirac measure
\delta_{d}
at
d
(that is, evaluation at
d
). Indeed,
\int_{{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}}x^{k}\cdot\delta_{d}=d^{k}
for any
k\in{\mathbf{Z}}
.

When
d
is divisible by
p
, however, we run into an immutable obstacle. There is no Dirac measure on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
corresponding to evaluation at
p
, since
p\notin\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. Moreover, the function
k\mapsto p^{k}
can
never
be interpolated continuously
p
-adically; it simply behaves too badly for this to be possible. Suppose there was indeed a measure
\theta_{p}
with

\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k}\cdot\theta_{p}=p^{k},

and then suppose
k_{n}
is a strictly increasing sequence of integers
p
-adically tending to
k
. Then

p^{k_{n}}=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k_{n}}\cdot\theta_{p}\longrightarrow\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k}\cdot\theta_{p}=p^{k},

which is clearly impossible since
p^{k_{n}}
tends to 0.

We get around this issue by taking
p
-stabilisations to kill the coefficients at
p
.

Definition 8.1
.

We define the
p
-stabilisation of
E_{k}
to be

E_{k}^{(p)}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=E_{k}(z)-p^{k-1}E_{k}(pz).

An easy check shows that

E_{k}^{(p)}=\frac{(1-p^{k-1})\zeta(1-k)}{2}+\sum_{n\geq 1}\sigma_{k-1}^{p}(n)q^{n},

where

\sigma_{k-1}^{p}(n)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<d|n\\
p\nmid d\end{subarray}}d^{k-1}.

Note
E_{k}^{(p)}
is a modular form of weight
k
and level
\Gamma_{0}(p)=\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}({\mathbf{Z}}):p|c\}
.

We’ve done all the work in proving the following result.

Theorem 8.2
.

There exists a power series

\ \mathbf{E}(z)=\sum_{n\geq 0}A_{n}q^{n}\in Q(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})[\![q]\!]

such that:

(a)

A_{0}
is a pseudo-measure, and
A_{n}\in\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
for all
n\geq 1
;

(b)

For all even
k\geq 4
, we have

\int_{{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}}x^{k-1}\cdot\mathbf{E}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{n\geq 0}\bigg(\int_{{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}}x^{k-1}\cdot A_{n}\bigg)q^{n}=E_{k}^{(p)}(z).

Proof.

The pseudo-measure
A_{0}
is simply
x\zeta_{p}/2
(shifting by 1 again, but in the opposite direction to before). We then define

A_{n}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<d|n\\
p\nmid d\end{subarray}}\delta_{d}\in\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}).

By the interpolation property of the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function,
A_{0}
interpolates the constant term of the Eisenstein series. We also have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k-1}\cdot A_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<d|n\\
p\nmid d\end{subarray}}\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}x^{k-1}\cdot\delta_{d}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<d|n\\
p\nmid d\end{subarray}}d^{k-1}=\sigma_{k-1}^{p}(n),

so we get the required interpolation property.
∎

Remark 8.3
.

(1)

The power series
\mathbf{E}(z)
is an example of a
\Lambda
-adic modular form
. In particular, it can be colloquially described as the statement:

“Eisenstein series vary
p
-adically continuously as you change the weight; if
k
and
k^{\prime}
are close
p
-adically, then the Fourier expansions of
E_{k}
and
E_{k^{\prime}}
are close
p
-adically.”

The theory of
p
-adic modular forms, and in particular the construction and study of
p
-adic families of Eisenstein series, was introduced by Serre
[
Ser73
]
to give a new construction of the
p
-adic zeta function of a totally real number field. Indeed, the main idea of Serre’s paper (cf.
[
Ser73
, Corollaire 2]
) was to show that if one can interpolate all of the non-constant coefficients – which, as we saw above, is quite simple – then this automatically gives an interpolation of the constant term, namely the
p
-adic zeta function, which is much more difficult to interpolate directly.

(2)

These results are often presented instead using the weight space
\mathcal{W}
from Remark
3.47
. The integers are naturally a subset of
\mathcal{W}(\mathbf{C}_{p})
via the maps
\kappa_{k}:x\mapsto x^{k}
, and two integers
k,k^{\prime}
lie in the same unit ball if and only if
k\equiv k^{\prime}\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp-1)
. Let
\mathscr{O}^{+}(\mathcal{W})
be the space of bounded rigid analytic functions on
\mathcal{W}
(corresponding to measures on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
), and
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{W})
the space of rigid meromorphic functions on
\mathcal{W}
with a possible (simple) pole only at the trivial character (corresponding to pseudo-measures on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
). Then we can view
\mathbf{E}
as a power series
\mathbf{E}(q)=\sum_{n\geq 0}B_{n}q^{n}\in\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{W})[\![q]\!]
, with
B_{n}\in\mathscr{O}^{+}(\mathcal{W})
for all
n>0
, such that for all
k\geq 4
, we have
\mathbf{E}(q)(\kappa_{k})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{n\geq 0}B_{n}(\kappa_{k})q^{n}=E_{k}^{(p)}(z)
. Hence we see
\mathbf{E}
as a
p
-adic interpolation of the Eisenstein series over the weight space.

(3)

These two remarks go hand in hand. Indeed, pioneering work of Hida went much further on the study of
p
-adic weight families of modular forms, showing that similar families (known as
Hida families
) exist for far more general modular forms. His work has been vastly generalised to the theory of Coleman families and eigenvarieties, parametrising the
p
-adic variation of modular/automorphic forms over appropriate weight spaces. Such families have important applications to the construction and study of
p
-adic
L
-functions; notable constructions in this direction are given in
[
PS13
,
Bel12
]
. For a flavour of the theory of Hida and Coleman families of modular forms, see the books
[
Hid93
]
or
[
Bel21
]
.

Part II: Iwasawa’s Main Conjecture

The second part of this work is devoted to the motivation, formulation and study of Iwasawa’s Main Conjecture. We will start by studying the Coleman map, a map between towers of local units and
p
-adic measures. This gives a connection between the tower of cyclotomic units – historically important for their connection to class numbers – and the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function
\zeta_{p}
from Part I, and hence a new arithmetic construction of
\zeta_{p}
(Theorem
10.15
). This construction can be seen as an arithmetic manifestation of the Euler product expression of the zeta function, and this point of view has led to beautiful generalisations now known as the theory of Euler Systems. We then prove a theorem of Iwasawa (Theorem
12.23
), relating the zeros of the
p
-adic
L
-function to arithmetic information in terms of units. Using these two results and class field theory, we will naturally arrive at the formulation and proof of (a special case of) the Main Conjecture (Theorem
13.8
).

9
Notation

Our study of the Iwasawa Main Conjecture requires a certain amount of notation, which we introduce straight away for convenience. The following should be used as an index of the key notation, and the reader is urged to consult the definition of new objects as they appear in the text.

Let
p
be an odd prime. Throughout this section, we work with coefficient field
L=\mathbf{Q}_{p}
. For
n\in{\mathbf{N}}
, write

F_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}={\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p^{n}}),\;\;\;F_{n}^{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}={\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p^{n}})^{+};

\mathscr{V}_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathscr{O}_{F_{n}}^{\times},\;\;\;\mathscr{V}_{n}^{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathscr{O}_{F_{n}^{+}}^{\times};

K_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{Q}_{p}(\mu_{p^{n}}),\;\;\;K_{n}^{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{Q}_{p}(\mu_{p^{n}})^{+};

\mathscr{U}_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathscr{O}_{K_{n}}^{\times},\;\;\;\mathscr{U}_{n}^{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathscr{O}_{K_{n}^{+}}^{\times},

where
(-)^{+}
denotes the maximal totally real subfield (i.e. the fixed points under complex conjugation). The extensions
F_{n}/{\mathbf{Q}}
,
K_{n}/\mathbf{Q}_{p}
,
F^{+}_{n}/{\mathbf{Q}}
and
K_{n}^{+}/\mathbf{Q}_{p}
are Galois and totally ramified at
p
(the first two of degree
(p-1)p^{n-1}
and the last two of degree
\frac{p-1}{2}p^{n-1}
) and we denote by
\mathfrak{p}_{n}
(resp.
\mathfrak{p}_{n}^{+}
) the unique prime ideal of
F_{n}
(resp.
F_{n}^{+}
) above the rational prime
p
. We let

F_{\infty}={\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}F_{n},\;\;\;F_{\infty}^{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(F_{\infty})^{+}=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}F_{n}^{+},

K_{\infty}=\mathbf{Q}_{p}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}K_{n},\;\;\;K_{\infty}^{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(K_{\infty})^{+}=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}K_{n}^{+},

and denote by
\mathfrak{p}
(resp.
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
) the unique prime of
F_{\infty}
(resp.
F^{+}_{\infty}
) above
p
.

Write
\Gamma\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{Gal}(F_{\infty}/{\mathbf{Q}})
,
\Gamma^{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{Gal}(F_{\infty}^{+}/{\mathbf{Q}})=\Gamma/\langle c\rangle
, where
c
denotes the complex conjugation. Since
\mathrm{Gal}(F_{n}/{\mathbf{Q}})
sends a primitive
p^{n}
th root of unity to a primitive
p^{n}
th root of unity, one deduces an isomorphism

\chi_{n}:\mathrm{Gal}(F_{n}/{\mathbf{Q}})\xrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}

determined by the identity

\sigma(\xi)=\xi^{\chi_{n}(\sigma)},

for
\sigma\in\mathrm{Gal}(F_{n}/{\mathbf{Q}})
and
\xi\in\mu_{p^{n}}
any primitive
