p^{n}
th root of unity. By infinite Galois theory,

(9.1)

\Gamma=\mathrm{Gal}(F_{\infty}/{\mathbf{Q}})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\varprojlim_{n}\mathrm{Gal}(F_{n}/{\mathbf{Q}})\xrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}\varprojlim_{n}({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}\cong\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times},

via the
cyclotomic character

\chi\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\varprojlim\chi_{n}
. Note
\chi
induces an isomorphism
\Gamma^{+}\cong{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}/\{\pm 1\}
.

We also define

(9.2)

\mathscr{U}_{n,1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{u\in\mathscr{U}_{n}\;:\;u\equiv 1\text{ (mod }\mathfrak{p}_{n})\},\;\;\;\mathscr{U}_{n,1}^{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathscr{U}_{n,1}\cap\mathscr{U}_{n}^{+}.

The subsets
\mathscr{U}_{n,1}
and
\mathscr{U}_{n,1}^{+}
are important as they have the structure of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-modules (indeed, if
u\in\mathscr{U}_{n,1}
or
\mathscr{U}_{n,1}^{+}
and
a\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, then
u^{a}=\sum_{k\geq 0}{a\choose k}(u-1)^{k}
converges). By contrast, the full local units
\mathscr{U}_{n}
and
\mathscr{U}_{n}^{+}
are only
{\mathbf{Z}}
-modules.

In general, our notation satisfies the following logic: if
X_{n}
is any subgroup of
\mathscr{U}_{n}
, then we let
X_{n}^{+}=X_{n}\cap\mathscr{U}_{n}^{+}
,
X_{n,1}=X_{n}\cap\mathscr{U}_{n,1}
and
X_{n,1}^{+}=X_{n}^{+}\cap\mathscr{U}_{n,1}^{+}
. Observe that, since
\mathscr{V}_{n}\subseteq\mathscr{U}_{n}
, the same applies for any subgroup
X_{n}
of
\mathscr{V}_{n}
.

It will be essential for our constructions and methods to consider these modules at all levels simultaneously. We define

(9.3)

\mathscr{U}_{\infty}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\varprojlim_{n}\mathscr{U}_{n},\;\;\;\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\varprojlim_{n}\mathscr{U}_{n,1};

\mathscr{U}_{\infty}^{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\varprojlim_{n}\mathscr{U}_{n}^{+},\;\;\;\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}^{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\varprojlim_{n}\mathscr{U}_{n,1}^{+},

where all limits are taken with respect to the norm maps. All of these infinite level modules are compact
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-modules (since they are inverse limits of compact
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-modules) and moreover they are all endowed with natural continuous actions of
\Gamma=\mathrm{Gal}(F_{\infty}/{\mathbf{Q}})
or
\Gamma^{+}=\mathrm{Gal}(F_{\infty}^{+}/{\mathbf{Q}})
. Accordingly, they are endowed with continuous actions of the Iwasawa algebras
\Lambda(\Gamma)
or
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
(which is the primary reason for passing to infinite level objects).

We fix once and for all a compatible system of roots of unity
(\xi_{p^{n}})_{n\in{\mathbf{N}}}
, that is, a sequence where
\xi_{p^{n}}
is a primitive
p^{n}
th root of unity such that
\xi_{p^{n+1}}^{p}=\xi_{p^{n}}
for all
n\in{\mathbf{N}}
. We let
\pi_{n}=\xi_{p^{n}}-1
, which is a uniformiser of
K_{n}
.

10
The Coleman map

In this section we prove a theorem of Coleman relating local units to power series over
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. Using this result, we construct in §
10
the
Coleman map
, a device for constructing
p
-adic
L
-functions from the data of a compatible system of units. We will explain how the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function can be constructed from towers of cyclotomic units using the Coleman map. This map thus provides an important bridge between analytic objects (
p
-adic
L
-functions) and arithmetic structures (cyclotomic units), and will serve as the key step in our formulation of the Main Conjecture.

In §
10.5
, we discuss a program started by Perrin-Riou to generalise Coleman’s work. Given a
p
-adic Galois representation, Perrin-Riou’s big logarithm maps construct a
p
-adic
L
-function from certain compatible systems of cohomology classes. Specialising to the representation
\mathbf{Q}_{p}(1)
, her map recovers the Coleman map. The results of this section are thus a prototype for studying
p
-adic
L
-functions in a larger, more conceptual framework.

10.1
Notation and Coleman’s theorem

Recall that
K_{n}=\mathbf{Q}_{p}(\mu_{p^{n}})
and
K_{\infty}=\mathbf{Q}_{p}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})
are the local versions of
F_{n}={\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p^{n}})
and
F_{\infty}={\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})
. We also defined

\mathscr{U}_{n}=\mathscr{O}_{K_{n}}^{\times}

to be the module of local units at level
n
, took a compatible system (
\xi_{p^{n}}
) of primitive
p^{n}
th roots of unity, and defined
\pi_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\xi_{p^{n}}-1
, a uniformiser of
K_{n}
. Recall that we defined

\mathscr{U}_{\infty}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\varprojlim_{n}\mathscr{U}_{n},

where the projective limit is taken with respect to the norm maps
N_{n,n-1}:K_{n}\rightarrow K_{n-1}
.

Let us first motivate Coleman’s theorem. The elements
\pi_{n}
give a sequence of elements in the open unit ball

B(0,1)=\{z\in{\mathbf{C}}_{p}:|z|<1\},

that approaches the boundary
\{|z|=1\}
as
n\to\infty
. Now, recall from Remark
3.39
that an element
f\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
can be viewed as a bounded rigid analytic function on
B(0,1)
; so any such
f
gives a sequence
f(\pi_{n})
of elements of
\mathscr{O}_{K_{n}}
. As we shall see below, the Weierstrass preparation theorem implies that
f
is uniquely determined by this sequence.

In the spirit of earlier chapters, where we sought analytic objects interpolating collections of specific values, it is natural to ask which sequences arise in this way. Precisely, given a sequence
\{u_{n}\in\mathscr{O}_{K_{n}}\}
, can it be interpolated by a power series
f
, in the sense that
f(\pi_{n})=u_{n}
for all
n
? Coleman’s theorem gives a positive answer to this question for norm-compatible sequences of units
(u_{n})_{n\in{\mathbf{N}}}\in\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
.

We begin with a simple observation, for a single fixed
n
.

Lemma 10.1
.

Let
u\in\mathscr{U}_{n}
be a local unit at level
n
. There exists a power series
f\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}
such that
f(\pi_{n})=u.

Proof.

This is essentially immediate from the fact that
\pi_{n}
is a uniformiser. Indeed,
K_{n}
is totally ramified, so one can choose some
a_{0}\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}
such that

a_{0}\equiv u\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0pt\pi_{n}),

and then
a_{1}\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}
such that

a_{1}\equiv\frac{u-a_{0}}{\pi_{n}}\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0pt\pi_{n}),

and so on, defining
f(T)=\sum_{n}a_{n}T^{n}\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!].
By construction,
f(\pi_{n})=u
. As
u
is a unit, we have
a_{0}\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. It’s then an exercise to see
f\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}
is invertible.
∎

The problem with this proposition is that such a power series
f
is far from being unique, since we had an abundance of choices for each coefficient. In the usual spirit of Iwasawa theory, Coleman realised that it was possible to solve this problem by passing to the infinite tower
K_{\infty}
, considering all
n
simultaneously.

Theorem 10.2

(Coleman)
.

There exists a unique injective homomorphism

\displaystyle\mathscr{U}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\longmapsto f_{u}

of multiplicative groups such that
f_{u}(\pi_{n})=u_{n}
for all
u\in\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
and
n\geq 1
.

Coleman actually proved something stronger. He described a precise subspace of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
in which the associated interpolating power series
f_{u}
lives; it is invariant under a certain norm operator
\mathcal{N}
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
. In particular, norm-compatibility on the right-hand side translates into norm-invariance on the left-hand side. We will prove all of this in §
10.3
below.

First, though, we study an important application, explaining how this theorem is related to the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function.

10.2
Example: cyclotomic units

Let
a\in{\mathbf{Z}}
prime to
p
, and define

c_{n}(a)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\xi_{p^{n}}^{a}-1}{\xi_{p^{n}}-1}\in\mathscr{U}_{n}.

This is indeed a unit, as both
\xi_{p^{n}}^{a}-1
and
\xi_{p^{n}}-1
are uniformisers in
K_{n}
.

Lemma 10.3
.

We have
c(a)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(c_{n}(a))_{n}\in\mathscr{U}_{\infty}.

Proof.

This is equivalent to proving that
N_{n,n-1}(c_{n}(a))=c_{n-1}(a).
Since the minimal polynomial of
\xi_{p^{n}}
over
K_{n-1}
is
X^{p}-\xi_{p^{n-1}}
, for any
b
prime to
p
we see that

N_{n,n-1}(\xi_{p^{n}}^{b}-1)=\prod_{\eta\in\mu_{p}}(\xi_{p^{n}}^{b}\eta-1)=\xi_{p^{n}}^{bp}-1=\xi_{p^{n-1}}^{b}-1,

where in the penultimate equality we have used the identity
X^{p}-1=\prod_{\eta\in\mu_{p}}(X\eta-1)
. Applying this with
b=a
shows the numerator of
c(a)
is norm-compatible, and with
b=1
the denominator. We conclude as norm is multiplicative.
∎

It is possible to write down
f_{c(a)}\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}
directly by inspection. Indeed, we see that

f_{c(a)}(T)=\frac{(1+T)^{a}-1}{T}

satisfies the required property (and
f_{c(a)}
is even a polynomial). We now connect this to the construction studied in §
4
. Recall the operator
\partial=(1+T)\tfrac{d}{dT}
from Lemma
3.29
.

Proposition 10.4
.

We have

\partial\log f_{c(a)}(T)=a-1-F_{a}(T),

where
F_{a}(T)
is the power series defined in Lemma
4.3
.

Proof.

We compute directly that

\displaystyle\partial\log f_{c(a)}

\displaystyle=\partial\log\big((1+T)^{a}-1\big)-\partial\log(T)

\displaystyle=\frac{a(1+T)^{a}}{(1+T)^{a}-1}-\frac{T+1}{T}

\displaystyle=a-1-\frac{1}{T}+\frac{a}{(1+T)^{a}-1}

\displaystyle=a-1-F_{a}(T).\qed

Lemma 10.5
.

We have

\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}(\mu_{\partial\log f_{c(a)}})=-\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}(\mu_{a}),

where
\mu_{a}
is the measure of Definition
4.5
.

Proof.

In terms of power series, the restriction to
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
corresponds to applying the operator
(1-\varphi\circ\psi)
. As
1-\varphi\circ\psi
kills the term
a-1
, we find that, as required,

(1-\varphi\circ\psi)\partial\log f_{c(a)}=-(1-\varphi\circ\psi)F_{a}.\qed

Remark 10.6
.

The measure
\mu_{a}
was used in the construction of
\zeta_{p}
. Later in §
10
we will use Theorem
10.2
to give a new construction of
\zeta_{p}
via the cyclotomic units. We will see more about the units
c_{n}(a)
, and in particular the module they generate in
\mathscr{U}_{n}
, in §
11
.

10.3
Proof of Coleman’s theorem

First we see that there is at most one power series
f_{u}
attached to a system of units
u
.

Lemma 10.7
.

Suppose
u=(u_{n})\in\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
and
f,g\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}
both satisfy

f(\pi_{n})=g(\pi_{n})=u_{n}

for all
n\geq 1
. Then
f=g
.

Proof.

The Weierstrass preparation theorem says that we can write any non-zero
h(T)\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
in the form
p^{m}u(T)r(T),
where
u(T)
is a unit and
r(T)
is a polynomial. Any such
h(T)
converges to a function on the maximal ideal in the ring of integers of
\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}
, and since
u(T)
cannot have zeros, we deduce that
h(T)
has a finite number of zeros in this maximal ideal. Now
(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is an infinite sequence of elements in this maximal ideal, so the fact that
(f-g)(\pi_{n})=0
for all
n\geq 1
implies that
f=g
, as required.
∎

We now move to showing the existence of such a series
f_{u}
. The key idea in the proof is to identify the subspace of
f\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}
such that
(f(\pi_{n}))_{n}\in\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
; that is, identify the
image
in Theorem
10.2
. For this, we want norm-compatibility of
f(\pi_{n})
. Lemma
10.8
and Proposition
10.10
below will show the existence of a norm operator on power series, and then translate the norm compatibility condition of units into norm invariance of power series; Lemma
10.11
will show certain continuity properties of this norm operator, which will allow us to prove Coleman’s theorem by a standard diagonal argument.

Recall that the action of
\varphi
on
f(T)\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
is defined by
\varphi(f)(T)=f((1+T)^{p}-1)
(see (
3.7
)), and that this action is injective. Importantly, we also have

(10.1)

\varphi(f)(\pi_{n+1})=f((\pi_{n+1}+1)^{p}-1)=f(\xi_{p^{n+1}}^{p}-1)=f(\xi_{p^{n}}-1)=f(\pi_{n}).

From our work with measures (cf. §
3.5.5
), we have also seen the existence of an additive operator
\psi
with the property that

(\varphi\circ\psi)(f)(T)=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{\eta\in\mu_{p}}f(\eta(1+T)-1).

We henceforth call
\psi
the
trace
operator (this terminology will become clear after Lemma
10.8
). We now define a multiplicative version of this operator.

Lemma 10.8
.

There exists a unique multiplicative operator
\mathcal{N}
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
, the
norm operator
, such that

(\varphi\circ\mathcal{N})(f)(T)=\prod_{\eta\in\mu_{p}}f(\eta(1+T)-1).

Proof.

The ring
B=\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
is an extension of
A=\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![\varphi(T)]\!]=\varphi(\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!])
of degree
p
, the former being obtained by adjoining a
p
th root of
(1+T)^{p}
to the latter. Each automorphism of
B
over
A
is given by
T\mapsto(1+T)\eta-1
for some
\eta\in\mu_{p}
. There is a norm map

\displaystyle N_{B/A}:\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\varphi(\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!])

\displaystyle f(T)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\prod_{\eta\in\mu_{p}}f((1+T)\eta-1).

The norm operator
\mathcal{N}
is then defined to be
\varphi^{-1}\circ N_{B/A}
, recalling that
\varphi
is injective.
∎

We similarly have
\psi=p^{-1}\varphi^{-1}\circ\mathrm{Tr}_{B/A}
, where
\mathrm{Tr}_{B/A}
is the trace operator for the extension
B/A
in the proof of Lemma
10.8
. Note that as
\mathcal{N}
is multiplicative, it preserves
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}
. Moreover, it is closely related to the norm operator
N_{n+1,n}:\mathscr{U}_{n+1}\to\mathscr{U}_{n}
used to defined
\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
, via the following lemma.

Lemma 10.9
.

The following diagram commutes:

(10.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}_{n}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \ f\mapsto f(\pi_{n+1})\ \ \ }
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{n+1,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \ f\mapsto f(\pi_{n})\ \ \ }

Proof.

If
f\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}
, then
f(\pi_{n})\in\mathscr{U}_{n}
for all
n
, as
f(\pi_{n})^{-1}=f^{-1}(\pi_{n})
is also integral. In particular, the horizontal maps are well-defined.

Observe now that, as the minimal polynomial of
\xi_{p^{n+1}}
over
K_{n}
is
X^{p}-\xi_{p^{n}}=0
, we can write the right-hand norm as

\displaystyle N_{n+1,n}\big(f(\pi_{n+1})\big)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\eta\in\mu_{p}}f(\eta\xi_{p^{n+1}}-1)

\displaystyle=(\varphi\circ\mathcal{N})(f)(\pi_{n+1})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{N}f)(\pi_{n}),

giving exactly the claimed commutativity. In the final step we have used (
10.1
).
∎

In particular, we get the following.

Proposition 10.10
.

There is an injective map

\displaystyle R:(\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times})^{\mathcal{N}=\mathrm{id}}

\displaystyle\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mathscr{U}_{\infty},

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\longmapsto(f(\pi_{n}))_{n}.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathcal{N}(f)=f
. By Lemma
10.9
, we deduce that

(10.3)

N_{n+1,n}\big(f(\pi_{n+1})\big)=f(\pi_{n}),

so
(f(\pi_{n}))_{n}\in\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
.
∎

To prove Theorem
10.2
it suffices to prove that the map
R
is surjective. We need the following lemma on the behaviour of
\mathcal{N}
modulo powers of
p
.

Lemma 10.11
.

Let
f(T)\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
. Then:

(i)

If
\varphi(f)(T)\equiv 1\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp^{k})
for some
k\geq 0
, then
f(T)\equiv 1\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp^{k}).

(ii)

We have

\mathcal{N}(f)\equiv f\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp).

Now suppose
f\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}
. Then:

(iii)

If
f\equiv 1\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp^{k})
with
k\geq 1
, then

\mathcal{N}(f)\equiv 1\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp^{k+1}).

(iv)

If
k_{2}\geq k_{1}\geq 0
, then

\mathcal{N}^{k_{2}}(f)\equiv\mathcal{N}^{k_{1}}(f)\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp^{k_{1}+1}).

Proof.

We leave parts (i) and (ii) as an exercise (cf.
[
CS06
, Lem. 2.3.1]
). To see part (iii), suppose that
f\equiv 1\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp^{k})
with
k\geq 1
, and recall that
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
is the maximal ideal of the ring of integers of
K_{1}=\mathbf{Q}_{p}(\mu_{p})
. For each
\eta\in\mu_{p}
, as
(\eta-1)(1+T)\in\mathfrak{p}_{1}\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
, we have

\eta(1+T)-1\equiv T\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0pt\mathfrak{p}_{1}\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]),

so that

f\big(\eta(1+T)-1\big)\equiv f(T)\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0pt\mathfrak{p}_{1}p^{k}\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!])

by considering each term separately. It follows that

\displaystyle\varphi\circ\mathcal{N}(f)(T)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\eta\in\mu_{p}}f\big(\eta(1+T)-1\big)

\displaystyle\equiv f(T)^{p}\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0pt\mathfrak{p}_{1}p^{k}\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]).

Since both
\varphi\circ\mathcal{N}(f)
and
f(T)^{p}
are elements of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
, this is actually an equivalence modulo
\mathfrak{p}_{1}p^{k}\cap\mathbf{Z}_{p}=p^{k+1}.
If
f(T)\equiv 1\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp^{k})
, then
f(T)^{p}\equiv 1\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp^{k+1})
, and then the proof follows from part (i).

To see part (iv), from part (ii) we see that

\frac{\mathcal{N}^{k_{2}-k_{1}}f}{f}\equiv 1\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp).

Then iterating
\mathcal{N}
and using part (iii)
k_{1}
times, we obtain the result.
∎

Proposition 10.12
.

The map
R:(\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times})^{\mathcal{N}=\mathrm{id}}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
is surjective.

Proof.

Let
u=(u_{n})_{n\geq 1}\in\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
. For each
n
, choose
f_{n}\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}
such that

f_{n}(\pi_{n})=u_{n}.

We claim that
\mathcal{N}f_{n+1}(\pi_{n})=u_{n}
. Indeed, using Lemma
10.9
we have

\mathcal{N}f_{n+1}(\pi_{n})=N_{n+1,n}(f_{n+1}(\pi_{n+1}))=N_{n+1,n}(u_{n+1})=u_{n}.

Iterating, for any
k\geq 0
we have

(10.4)

(\mathcal{N}^{k}f_{n+k})(\pi_{n})=u_{n}.

In view of Lemma
10.11
(iv), we define

g_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{N}^{n}f_{2n}\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}.

Then, for any
m\geq n
, we have

\displaystyle u_{n}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}^{2m-n}f_{2m}(\pi_{n})

\displaystyle\equiv\mathcal{N}^{m}f_{2m}(\pi_{n})=g_{m}(\pi_{n})\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp^{m+1}),

where the first equality is (
10.4
) and the congruence is Lemma
10.11
(iv), taking
k_{2}=2m-n
and
k_{1}=m
. Hence as
m\to\infty
, we have
g_{m}(\pi_{n})\to u_{n}
for all
n
. It thus suffices to find a convergent subsequence of
(g_{m})
; but such a subsequence exists, as
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}
is compact. Letting
f_{u}\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}
denote the limit of this subsequence, we have
f_{u}(\pi_{n})=u_{n}
for all
n
.

It remains to show that
\mathcal{N}(f_{u})=f_{u}
. Indeed, as the sequence
(u_{n})
is norm compatible, we have, using Lemma
10.9
,

\mathcal{N}(f_{u})(\pi_{n})=N_{n+1,n}f_{u}(\pi_{n+1})=N_{n+1,n}(u_{n+1})=u_{n}=f_{u}(\pi_{n}).

As
\mathcal{N}(f_{u})
and
f_{u}
are both Coleman power series for
u
, by Lemma
10.7
they are equal.
∎

With this in hand, we have proved the following more precise version of Theorem
10.2
.

Theorem 10.13
.

There exists a unique isomorphism of groups

\displaystyle\mathscr{U}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\to\big(\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}\big)^{\mathcal{N}=\mathrm{id}}

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\mapsto f_{u}

such that
f_{u}(\pi_{n})=u_{n}
for all
u\in\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
and
n\geq 1
.

Proof.

By Propositions
10.10
and
10.12
, we have a bijection
R:(\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times})^{\mathcal{N}=\mathrm{id}}\xrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
. This is an isomorphism, and
R^{-1}
gives the required map. We have
f_{u}(\pi_{n})=u_{n}
by construction of
R
and uniqueness follows from Lemma
10.7
.
∎

10.4
Definition of the Coleman map

The Coleman map is motivated by the example of §
10.2
, where we saw that a distinguished family of local units – the cyclotomic units – are strongly linked to the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function. In particular, given the construction of
\zeta_{p}
in §
4
and Lemma
10.5
,
\zeta_{p}
can be defined by the following procedure:

(1)

Consider the tower
c(a)
of cyclotomic units.

(2)

Take its Coleman power series
f_{c(a)}
.

(3)

Apply
\partial\log
.

(4)

Apply
(1-\varphi\circ\psi)
.

(5)

Apply
\partial^{-1}
.

(6)

Pass to the corresponding measure on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
by inverting the Mahler transform.

(7)

Finally, divide by
\theta_{a}
.

Recall that in terms of measures, step (4) corresponds to restriction to
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, and (5) to multiplication by
x^{-1}
. We are therefore led to consider the following construction.

Definition 10.14
.

Let

\mathrm{Col}:\mathscr{U}_{\infty}\xrightarrow{u\mapsto f_{u}(T)}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times})^{\mathcal{N}=\mathrm{id}}\xrightarrow{\partial\log}\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]\xrightarrow{1-\varphi\circ\psi}\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=0}\xrightarrow{\partial^{-1}}\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=0}\xrightarrow{\mathscr{A}^{-1}}\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}),

where the first map is Coleman’s isomorphism, the second is the logarithmic derivative appearing in §
10.2
, the third is the measure-theoretic restriction from
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
to
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, the fourth is multiplication by
x^{-1}
, and the last is the Mahler correspondence (§
3.4
and Corollary
3.32
).

Via §
10.2
, we have the following description of the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function.

Theorem 10.15
.

For any topological generator
a
of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, we have an equality of pseudo-measures

\zeta_{p}=\frac{\mathrm{Col}(c(a))}{\theta_{a}}\in Q(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}).

10.5
Generalisations: The Kummer sequence, Euler systems and
p
-adic
L
-functions

We conclude this section with a digression on the generalisation of the Coleman map that leads to a conjectural construction, under the assumption of the existence of certain global cohomological elements, of
p
-adic
L
-functions of more general motives. This section is included as additional context and may be skipped on a first reading. Throughout, if
F
is a number field, we let
\mathscr{G}_{F}
denote its absolute Galois group.

Consider, for
m\geq 1
, the Kummer exact sequence

(10.5)

0\to\mu_{p^{m}}\to\mathbf{G}_{\rm m}\xrightarrow{x\mapsto x^{p^{m}}}\mathbf{G}_{\rm m}\to 0.

Evaluating at
\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}
and taking fixed points by
\mathscr{G}_{F}
, this short exact sequence induces, for any number field
F
, a long exact sequence on cohomology

(10.6)

0\to\mu_{p^{m}}(F)\to F^{\times}\xrightarrow{x\mapsto x^{p^{m}}}F^{\times}\longrightarrow H^{1}(F,\mu_{p^{m}})\to H^{1}(F,\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}^{\times}).

Here, for any topological
\mathscr{G}_{F}
-module
A
, we write
H^{1}(F,A)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=H^{1}(\mathscr{G}_{F},A)
for the Galois cohomology, i.e. the continuous group cohomology of
\mathscr{G}_{F}
. By Hilbert 90, we have
H^{1}(F,\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}^{\times})=0
. Taking inverse limits over
m\geq 1
, which is exact, we obtain an isomorphism called the Kummer map

(10.7)

\delta:F^{\times}\otimes\mathbf{Z}_{p}\xrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}H^{1}(F,\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)).

Explicitly, at each finite level, the isomorphism

F^{\times}\otimes{\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}}=F^{\times}/(F^{\times})^{p^{n}}\xrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}H^{1}(\mathscr{G}_{F},\mu_{p^{n}})

is given as follows. Take
a\in F^{\times}
and take any
b\in\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}^{\times}
such that
b^{p^{n}}=a
. Then
c_{a}:\sigma\mapsto\frac{\sigma(b)}{b}
defines a
1
-coycle on
\mathscr{G}_{F}
and it is a coboundary if and only if
a
is a
p^{n}
th power in
F^{\times}
, which shows that the map sending the class of
a
to the class of
c_{a}
is well defined.

Let
m=Dp^{n}
,
n\geq 1
, and define

\mathbf{c}_{m}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\xi_{m}^{-1}-1}{\xi_{m}-1}\in\mathscr{O}_{{\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{m})}^{\times},

a generalisation of the cyclotomic units
c_{n}(-1)
(where
D=1
) from Example
10.2
, where
(\xi_{m})_{m}
denote a compatible system of
m
th roots of unity. One can show that these elements satisfy the following relations with respect to the norm maps:

\mathrm{N}_{{\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{m\ell})/{\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{m})}(\mathbf{c}_{m\ell})=\begin{cases}\mathbf{c}_{m}&\quad\text{if }\ell\mid m\\
(1-\ell^{-1})\mathbf{c}_{m}&\quad\text{if }\ell\nmid m.\end{cases}

Using the Kummer map from Equation (
10.7
), we get elements
\mathbf{z}_{m}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\delta(\mathbf{c}_{m})\in H^{1}({\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{m}),\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1))
satisfying

\mathrm{cores}_{{\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{m\ell})/{\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{m})}(\mathbf{z}_{m\ell})=\begin{cases}\mathbf{z}_{m}&\quad\text{if }\ell\mid m\\
(1-\mathrm{Frob}_{\ell}^{-1})\mathbf{z}_{m}&\quad\text{if }\ell\nmid m,\end{cases}

where we have used that
\mathrm{Frob}_{\ell}
acts on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)
simply by multiplication by
\ell
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)
. Observe also that
(1-\ell^{-1})
is the Euler factor at
\ell
of the Riemann zeta function (evaluated at
s=1
). This admits the following huge generalisation, as described comprehensively in
[
Rub00
]
.

Definition 10.16
.

Let
\Sigma
be a finite set of primes containing
p
, let
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{L}\mathscr{G}_{\mathbf{Q}}
be a global
p
-adic Galois representation which is unramified outside
\Sigma
, and let
T\subseteq V
be an
\mathscr{O}_{L}
-lattice stable under
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbf{Q}}
. An
Euler system
for
(V,T,\Sigma)
is a collection of classes

\mathbf{z}_{m}\in H^{1}({\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{m}),T),

where
m
is of the form
m=p^{n}m^{\prime}
with
n\geq 0
, and where
m^{\prime}
is a square-free product of prime noumbers not belonging to
\Sigma
, satisfying

\mathrm{cores}_{{\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{m\ell})/{\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{m})}(\mathbf{z}_{m\ell})=\begin{cases}\mathbf{z}_{m}&\quad\text{if }\ell=p\\
P_{\ell}(V^{*}(1),\sigma_{\ell}^{-1})\mathbf{z}_{m}&\quad\text{if }\ell\neq p,\end{cases}

where
P_{\ell}(V^{*}(1),X)=\det(1-\mathrm{Frob}_{\ell}^{-1}X|{V^{*}(1)}^{I_{\ell}})
is the Euler factor at
\ell
of the
L
-function associated to
V^{*}(1)
and
\sigma_{\ell}
denotes the image of
\mathrm{Frob}_{\ell}
in
\mathrm{Gal}({\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{m})/{\mathbf{Q}})
.

The cyclotomic units form an Euler system for the representation
\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)
, and lie at the heart of Rubin’s proof of the Main Conjecture. In general, constructing Euler systems for a Galois representation is a very difficult task, and few examples exist at the moment.

We now describe a rephrasing of Coleman’s map that more easily generalises. Above, we showed that evaluating Kummer’s exact sequence (
10.5
) at
\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}
, taking the long exact sequence in Galois cohomology for
F
, and taking an inverse limit, we get an isomorphism
F^{\times}\otimes\mathbf{Z}_{p}\cong H^{1}(F,\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1))
. In exactly the same way, replacing
\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}
by
\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}
, and
F
by the finite extension
K_{n}
of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
for
n\geq 1
, we obtain an isomorphism

K_{n}^{\times}\otimes\mathbf{Z}_{p}\cong H^{1}(K_{n},\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)).

These isomorphisms intertwine the norm maps on the left hand side with the corestriction maps in cohomology on the right hand side, and hence, considering the inverse limit over all
n
, we see that there is an isomorphism

(10.8)

\varprojlim_{n\geq 1}K_{n}^{\times}\otimes\mathbf{Z}_{p}\cong\varprojlim_{n\geq 1}H^{1}(K_{n},\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)).

We define the
Iwasawa cohomology
to be

H^{1}_{\rm Iw}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},\mathbf{Q}_{p}(1))\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\varprojlim_{n\geq 1}H^{1}(K_{n},\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1))\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathbf{Q}_{p}.

Such groups can be attached to general Galois representations (see below), and they are a natural generalisation of the local units.

To make this precise, note that the inclusion
\mathscr{U}_{n}=\mathscr{O}_{K_{n}}^{\times}\subset K_{n}^{\times}
induces a natural map
\mathscr{U}_{n}\to K_{n}^{\times}\otimes\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, yielding a map
\mathscr{U}_{\infty}\to\varprojlim K_{n}^{\times}\otimes\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. Composing this with (
10.8
), we obtain a map

\kappa:\mathscr{U}_{\infty}\longrightarrow\varprojlim_{n\geq 1}H^{1}(K_{n},\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)).

One can then show that there exists a map

\mathrm{Col^{\prime}}:H^{1}_{\rm Iw}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},\mathbf{Q}_{p}(1))\to\mathscr{M}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times},\mathbf{Q}_{p}),

where we recall that
\mathscr{M}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times},\mathbf{Q}_{p})=\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathbf{Q}_{p}
is the space of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
-valued measures on
\Gamma
, making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\rm Iw}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},\mathbf{Q}_{p}(1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}({{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}},\mathbf{Q}_{p})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Col}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Col}^{\prime}}

commute.

By localising, the Euler system of cyclotomic units give rise to an element of the Iwasawa cohomology. By combining the above with Proposition
10.4
, we see that the
p
-adic zeta function can be obtained by evaluating
\mathrm{Col}^{\prime}
at this Iwasawa cohomology class (and dividing through by the measure
\theta_{a}
to make it independent of
a
, which introduces a pole).

The advantage of this reformulation is that Iwasawa cohomology generalises well, as we now explain. Let
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{L}\mathscr{G}_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
be any
p
-adic representation of
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
, i.e a finite dimensional
L
-vector space
V
equipped with a continuous linear action of
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
. As before, we define its Iwasawa cohomology groups as

H^{1}_{\rm Iw}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},V)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\varprojlim_{n\geq 1}H^{1}(K_{n},T)\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{L}}L,

where
T\subseteq V
denotes any
\mathscr{O}_{L}
-lattice of
V
stable under the action of the Galois group
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
, and where as before the inverse limit is taken with respect to the corestriction maps in cohomology. Morally, Iwasawa cohomology groups are the groups where the local part at
p
of an Euler system of a global
p
-adic representation lives. Assuming that the representation is crystalline
11
11

11

Loosely, a
p
-adic representation of
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
being
crystalline
is a condition from
p
-adic Hodge theory that is the
p
-adic equivalent to an
\ell
-adic representation of
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
(with
\ell\neq p
) being unramified. For the Galois representation attached to an elliptic curve
E
defined over
{\mathbf{Q}}
, this amounts to asking that
E
has good reduction at
p
. An extension of these results in the case of bad reduction can be found in
[
RJ18
]
.
, the Coleman map has been generalised by Perrin-Riou
[
PR95a
]
. Under some choices, she constructed
big logarithm maps

\mathrm{Log}_{V}:H^{1}_{\rm Iw}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},V)\to\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{la}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times},L),

where
\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{la}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times},L)
denotes the space of
L
-valued locally analytic distributions on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
(in the sense of §
3.7
). The map
\mathrm{Log}_{V}
satisfies certain interpolation properties expressed in terms of Bloch-Kato’s exponential and dual exponential maps and, for
V=\mathbf{Q}_{p}(1)
, we recover
\mathrm{Col}^{\prime}
.

The general idea is that, given an Euler system for a global
p
-adic Galois representation, localising it at the place
p
and applying Perrin-Riou’s map, one can construct a
p
-adic
L
-function for
V
. In a diagram:

\big\{\text{Euler systems}\big\}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{loc}_{p}}H^{1}_{\rm Iw}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},V)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Log}_{V}}\big\{p\text{-adic $L$-functions}\big\}.

This splits the problem of constructing
p
-adic
L
-functions for motives into a global problem (finding an Euler system) and a purely local problem (constructing the big logarithm maps). See
[
Col00
]
for further references on this subject.

11
Iwasawa’s theorem on the zeros of the
p
-adic zeta function

In the previous section, the Coleman map allowed us to give a construction of the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function
\zeta_{p}
using a specific tower of cyclotomic units. We now describe a theorem of Iwasawa (Theorem
11.9
) that puts this on a deeper footing. This theorem describes the zeros of
\zeta_{p}
– captured by a canonically attached ideal in the Iwasawa algebra – in terms of arithmetic data, via the
module
of cyclotomic units inside the local units. The Coleman map from §
10
will be the key step for connecting both worlds.

With the aim of moving all the analytic information to the Galois side, we will start by reformulating the definition of the
p
-adic zeta function as a pseudo-measure on the Galois group
\Gamma=\mathrm{Gal}(F_{\infty}/{\mathbf{Q}})\cong\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. We then introduce the global and local modules of cyclotomic units (which will be systematically studied later), stating the connection to class numbers, and state Iwasawa’s theorem.

11.1
Measures on Galois groups

Recall that
F_{\infty}=\cup_{n\geq 1}{\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p^{n}})
, that
\Gamma=\mathrm{Gal}(F_{\infty}/{\mathbf{Q}})
, and that the cyclotomic character gives an isomorphism
\chi:\Gamma\xrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}
. This isomorphism induces an identification between measures on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
and measures on the Galois group
\Gamma
. From now on, we will let
\Lambda(\Gamma)
be the space of measures on
\Gamma
, which we identify with
\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
via the cyclotomic character. We may thus naturally consider
\zeta_{p}
as a pseudo-measure on
\Gamma
.

Similarly, the Galois group
\Gamma^{+}=\mathrm{Gal}(F_{\infty}^{+}/{\mathbf{Q}})=\Gamma/\langle c\rangle
is identified through the cyclotomic character with
{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}/\{\pm 1\}
. Observe that
\zeta_{p}
, which ostensibly is an element of
Q(\Gamma)
, vanishes at the characters
\chi^{k}
, for any odd integer
k>1
. We will use this fact to show that
\zeta_{p}
actually descends to a pseudo-measure on
\Gamma^{+}
.

Lemma 11.1
.

Let
c\in\Gamma
denote complex conjugation. Let
R
be a ring in which
2
is invertible and
M
an
R
-module with a continuous action of
\Gamma
. Then
M
decomposes as

M\cong M^{+}\oplus M^{-},

where
c
acts as
+1
on
M^{+}
and as
-1
on
M^{-}
.

Proof.

This follows directly by using the idempotents
\frac{1+c}{2}
and
\frac{1-c}{2}
, which act as projectors to the corresponding
M^{+}
and
M^{-}
.
∎

We are assuming that
p
is odd, so
\Lambda(\Gamma)\cong\Lambda(\Gamma)^{+}\oplus\Lambda(\Gamma)^{-}
. In fact, the module
\Lambda(\Gamma)^{+}
admits a description solely in terms of the quotient
\Gamma^{+}
.

Lemma 11.2
.

There is a natural isomorphism

\Lambda(\Gamma)^{+}\cong\Lambda(\Gamma^{+}).

Proof.

We work at finite level. Let
\Gamma_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{Gal}(F_{n}/{\mathbf{Q}})
, and
\Gamma_{n}^{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{Gal}(F_{n}^{+}/{\mathbf{Q}})
. Then there is a natural surjection

\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n}]\to\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n}^{+}]

induced by the natural quotient map on Galois groups. Since this must necessarily map
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n}]^{-}
to 0, this induces a map
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n}]^{+}\rightarrow\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n}^{+}]
. The result now follows at finite level by a dimension count (as both are free
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-modules of rank
(p-1)p^{n-1}/2
, and one sees easily that the map sends a basis of the first module to a basis of the second). We obtain the required result by passing to the inverse limit.
∎

We henceforth freely identify
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
with the submodule
\Lambda(\Gamma)^{+}
of
\Lambda(\Gamma)
.

Lemma 11.3
.

Let
\mu\in\Lambda(\Gamma)
. Then
\mu\in\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
if and only if

\int_{\Gamma}\chi(x)^{k}\cdot\mu=0

for all odd
k\geq 1
.

Proof.

By Lemma
11.1
, we can write
\mu=\mu^{+}+\mu^{-}
, where
\mu^{\pm}=\frac{1\pm c}{2}\mu
. We want to show that
\mu^{-}=0
if and only if
\int_{\Gamma}\chi(x)^{k}\cdot\mu=0
for all odd
k\geq 1
. Since
\chi(c)=-1
, we have

\int_{\Gamma}\chi(x)^{k}\cdot\mu^{+}=\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\int_{\Gamma}\chi^{k}\cdot\mu+(-1)^{k}\int_{\Gamma}\chi^{k}\cdot\mu\bigg).

If
k
is odd, the above expression vanishes, showing that
\int_{\Gamma}\chi(x)^{k}\cdot\mu=\int_{\Gamma}\chi(k)\cdot\mu^{+}
for all odd
k
. On the other hand, the same argument shows that
\int_{\Gamma}\chi(x)^{k}\cdot\mu^{-}
vanishes for all
k
even. The result follows then by Lemma
3.36
.
∎

Corollary 11.4
.

The
p
-adic zeta function is a pseudo-measure on
\Gamma^{+}
.

Proof.

This follows from the interpolation property, as
\zeta(1-k)=0
for odd
k\geq 1
.
∎

11.2
The ideal generated by the
p
-adic zeta function

It is natural to ask about the zeros of the
p
-adic zeta function. Since the zeros are not modified if we multiply by a unit, studying the zeros of a measure on
\Gamma
is equivalent to studying the ideal in
\Lambda(\Gamma)
generated by the measure.

Even though Kubota–Leopoldt is only a pseudo-measure – hence not an element of
\Lambda(\Gamma)
– we now see that it still ‘generates’ a natural ideal in
\Lambda(\Gamma)
. By definition of pseudo-measures, the elements
([g]-[1])\zeta_{p}
belong to the Iwasawa algebra
\Lambda(\Gamma)
for any
g\in\Gamma
. Recall from Definition
3.37
that
I(\Gamma)
denotes the
augmentation ideal
of
\Lambda(\Gamma)
, that is, the ideal

I(\Gamma)=\ker(\Lambda(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{Z}_{p}),

where
\Lambda(\Gamma)\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{Z}_{p}
is the map induced by
[g]\mapsto 1
for any
\sigma\in\Gamma
. We define
I(\Gamma^{+})
similarly.

Proposition 11.5
.

The module
I(\Gamma)\zeta_{p}
is an ideal in
\Lambda(\Gamma)
. Similarly, the module
I(\Gamma^{+})\zeta_{p}
is an ideal in
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
.

Proof.

Since
\zeta_{p}
is a pseudo-measure, we know
([g]-[1])\zeta_{p}\in\Lambda(\Gamma)
for all
g\in\Gamma
. Hence the result follows as
I(\Gamma)
is the topological ideal generated by the elements
[g]-[1]
for
g\in\Gamma
. The same argument holds for
I(\Gamma^{+})\zeta_{p}
.
∎

11.3
Cyclotomic units and Iwasawa’s theorem

Iwasawa’s theorem describes the ideal
I(\Gamma)\zeta_{p}
in terms of the module of cyclotomic units. We now recall this module, and its classical connection to class numbers, and then state Iwasawa’s theorem.

Definition 11.6
.

For
n\geq 1
, we define the group
\mathscr{D}_{n}
of cyclotomic units of
F_{n}
to be the intersection of
\mathscr{O}_{F_{n}}^{\times}
and the multiplicative subgroup of
F_{n}^{\times}
generated by
\{\pm\xi_{p^{n}},\xi_{p^{n}}^{a}-1\,:\,1\leq a\leq p^{n}-1\}
. We set
\mathscr{D}_{n}^{+}=\mathscr{D}_{n}\cap F_{n}^{+}
.

We will study the structure of cyclotomic units more in detail in subsequent sections. The following result shows their connection to class numbers.

Theorem 11.7
.

Let
n\geq 1
. The group
\mathscr{D}_{n}
(resp.
\mathscr{D}_{n}^{+}
) is of finite index in the group of units
\mathscr{V}_{n}
(resp.
\mathscr{V}_{n}^{+}
) in
F_{n}
(resp.
F_{n}^{+}
), and we have

h_{n}^{+}=[\mathscr{V}_{n}:\mathscr{D}_{n}]=[\mathscr{V}_{n}^{+}:\mathscr{D}_{n}^{+}],

where
h_{n}^{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\#\mathrm{Cl}(F_{n}^{+})
is the class number of
F_{n}^{+}
.

Proof.

We will not prove this here; see
[
Was97
, Theorem 8.2]
. The proof goes by showing that the regulator of cyclotomic units is given in terms of special
L
-values at
s=1
of Dirichlet
L
-functions, and then using the class number formula.
∎

As we explained in §
10.4
, the construction of the
p
-adic zeta function via the Coleman map goes as follows. The cyclotomic units
c_{n}(a)
, introduced in §
10.2
, are naturally elements of
\mathscr{D}_{n}
, hence global. One then considers their image inside the space of local units, and then applies the Coleman map (Definition
10.14
), which is a purely local procedure. In this spirit it is natural to switch here from studying the global modules
\mathscr{D}_{n}
and
\mathscr{D}_{n}^{+}
to their closures in the space of local units. Recall
\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}^{+}
from the notational introduction to Part II; it is the group of norm-compatible local units congruent to
1\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp)
.

Definition 11.8
.

For any
n\geq 1
, define
\mathscr{C}_{n}
as the
p
-adic closure of
\mathscr{D}_{n}
inside the local units
\mathscr{U}_{n}
12
12

12

We will describe this closure more explicitly in Lemma
12.20
below.
, let
\mathscr{C}^{+}_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathscr{C}_{n}\cap\mathscr{U}_{n}^{+}
, and let

\mathscr{C}_{n,1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathscr{C}_{n}\cap\mathscr{U}_{n,1},\;\;\;\mathscr{C}^{+}_{n,1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathscr{C}^{+}_{n}\cap\mathscr{U}_{n,1};

\mathscr{C}_{\infty,1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\varprojlim_{n\geq 1}\mathscr{C}_{n,1},\;\;\;\mathscr{C}^{+}_{\infty,1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\varprojlim_{n\geq 1}\mathscr{C}^{+}_{n,1}.

We will see that
\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}^{+}
, and its quotient
\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}^{+}/\mathscr{C}_{\infty,1}^{+}
, naturally have
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
-module structures. Moreover, Iwasawa explicitly related this quotient to the
p
-adic zeta function. The following theorem, which we prove in §
12
, says that the cyclotomic units capture the zeros of
\zeta_{p}
and ultimately motivated Iwasawa to formulate his Main Conjecture.

Theorem 11.9
.

The Coleman map induces an isomorphism of
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
-modules

\mathscr{U}^{+}_{\infty,1}/\mathscr{C}^{+}_{\infty,1}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})/I(\Gamma^{+})\zeta_{p}.

The quotient
\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}^{+}/\mathscr{C}_{\infty,1}^{+}
is a local analogue, at infinite level, of the cyclotomic units inside the global units, whose indices compute class numbers in the cylotomic tower (Theorem
11.7
). They will in turn be related (cf. Corollary
13.14
) to the Galois modules appearing in the formulation of the Iwasawa Main Conjecture. This theorem is hence the first step into proving a remarkable and deep connection between class groups and the
p
-adic zeta function, which will be the main purpose of the Iwasawa Main Conjecture.

12
Proof of Iwasawa’s theorem

In this section, we prove Theorem
11.9
. First, we equip the local units with an action of
\Lambda(\Gamma)
, and prove that the Coleman map is equivariant with respect to this action. Then, in Theorem
12.17
, we compute the kernel and cokernel of the Coleman map. Finally we describe generators of the modules of cyclotomic units, and compute their image under the Coleman map. We combine all of this to prove Theorem
11.9
.

12.1
Equivariance properties of the Coleman map

Theorem
11.9
is a statement about
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
-modules. Here it is important that we work over the full Iwasawa algebra; the structure theorem for modules over
\Lambda(\Gamma)
and
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
– stated in Theorem
13.1
below – is crucial in studying the Iwasawa Main Conjecture. It is desirable, then, to equip
\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
with a
\Lambda(\Gamma)
-module structure. As
\Lambda(\Gamma)
is the completed group ring of
\Gamma
over
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, this amounts to equipping it with compatible actions of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
and
\Gamma
. For the latter, we use the natural Galois action on the local units. For the former, however, we are stuck: whilst there is a natural action of
{\mathbf{Z}}
on
\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
by
u\mapsto u^{a}
for an integer
a
, this does not extend to an action of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
.

12.1.1
The action of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}

To fix the absence of a
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-action on local units, we recall the definition of the subgroup
\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}\subset\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
introduced in (
9.3
). In particular, we showed there that the action of
{\mathbf{Z}}

does
extend to
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
on
\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}
. For convenience, we recall (cf. Definition
10.14
) that the Coleman map was defined as the following composition

\mathrm{Col}:\mathscr{U}_{\infty}\xrightarrow{u\mapsto f_{u}(T)}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times})^{\mathcal{N}=\mathrm{id}}\xrightarrow{\partial\log}\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]\xrightarrow{1-\varphi\circ\psi}\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=0}\xrightarrow{\partial^{-1}}\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=0}\xrightarrow{\mathscr{A}^{-1}}\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}).

Proposition 12.1
.

The map
\mathrm{Col}
restricts to a
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-equivariant map

\mathrm{Col}:\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}\longrightarrow\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}).

Proof.

It suffices to check
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-equivariance for each map in the composition in Definition
10.14
. The action of
a\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}
on
u\in\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}
is given by
u\mapsto u^{a}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{k\geq 0}{a\choose k}(u-1)^{k}
. Write
f_{u}=\sum_{k\geq 1}a_{k}(u)T^{k}
. We first claim that

(12.1)

a_{0}(u)\equiv 1\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp).

Indeed, by definition
f_{u}(\pi_{n})=u_{n}\equiv 1\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0pt\mathfrak{p}_{n})
for each
n
, and as
\pi_{n}
is a uniformiser for
K_{n}
, we see

f_{u}(\pi_{n})=a_{0}(u)+\sum_{k\geq 1}a_{k}(u)\pi_{n}^{k}\ \ \ \in a_{0}(u)+\mathfrak{p}_{n},

from which we see that
a_{0}(u)\equiv 1\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0pt\mathfrak{p}_{n})
. But
a_{0}(u)
lies in
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, giving (
12.1
).

Thus
f_{u}(T)-1\in(p,T)
. As
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
is complete in the
(p,T)
-adic topology,

f_{u}(T)^{a}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\begin{pmatrix}a\\
j\end{pmatrix}(f_{u}(T)-1)^{j}

converges to a power series
f_{u}^{a}(T)\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
. Since by construction
f_{u}(\pi_{n})^{a}=u_{n}^{a}
, by Lemma
10.7
we have,

f_{u}^{a}=f_{u^{a}}\in(\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times})^{\mathcal{N}=\mathrm{id}}.

As a result, we have equipped the image of
\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}
inside
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
under the map
u\mapsto f_{u}
with a
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-action such that the restriction of the Coleman isomorphism is
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-equivariant. We compute that
\partial\log(f_{u}^{a})=a\partial\log(f_{u})
, so
\partial\log
is equivariant for the natural
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-action on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
. Finally the maps
(1-\varphi\circ\psi)
,
\partial^{-1}
and
\mathscr{A}^{-1}
are
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-equivariant by definition.
∎

The next two lemmas show that we have not lost any information by restricting.

Lemma 12.2
.

We have
\mathscr{U}_{\infty}=\mu_{p-1}\times\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}.

Proof.

We start at finite level
n
. As
p
is totally ramified in
K_{n}
for all
n
, there is a unique prime
\mathfrak{p}_{n}
of
K_{n}
above
p
, and reduction modulo
\mathfrak{p}_{n}
gives a short exact sequence

1\to\mathscr{U}_{n,1}\to\mathscr{U}_{n}\to\mu_{p-1}\to 1,

which is split, so
\mathscr{U}_{n}=\mu_{p-1}\times\mathscr{U}_{n,1}
. The result follows in the inverse limit.
∎

Lemma 12.3
.

The subgroup
\mu_{p-1}
of
\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
is killed by
\mathrm{Col}
. In particular, no information is lost when restricting to
\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}
.

Proof.

Note
\mu_{p-1}\subset\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. The first map
u\mapsto f_{u}
is an isomorphism that sends
v=(v)_{n\in{\mathbf{N}}}\in\mu_{p-1}\subset\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
to the constant power series
f_{v}(T)=v
. But constant power series are killed by the second map
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]\xrightarrow{\partial\log}\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
, which involves differentiation. Thus
\mu_{p-1}
is mapped to zero under the composition, and hence under
\mathrm{Col}
.
∎

Remark 12.4
.

The kernel of
\partial\log
is comprised of constant power series. Moreover, if
f\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
is constant and invariant under
\mathcal{N}
, then this forces
f^{p}=f
. Thus the kernel of the composition of the first two maps is exactly
\mu_{p-1}
.

12.1.2
The Galois action

The Galois group
\Gamma=\mathrm{Gal}(F_{\infty}/{\mathbf{Q}})
is naturally isomorphic to
\mathrm{Gal}(K_{\infty}/\mathbf{Q}_{p})
, as
p
is totally ramified in
F_{\infty}
. Thus
\Gamma
acts on
\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
.

Notation
.

For
a\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, let
\sigma_{a}\in\Gamma
be the corresponding element of
\Gamma
with
\chi(\sigma_{a})=a
, recalling that
\chi:\Gamma\xrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
is the cyclotomic character from (
9.1
).

Proposition 12.5
.

The Coleman map
\mathrm{Col}:\mathscr{U}_{\infty}\to\Lambda(\Gamma)
is
\Gamma
-equivariant.

Proof.

We must show that if
a\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, and
u\in\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
, we have

\mathrm{Col}(\sigma_{a}(u))=\sigma_{a}(\mathrm{Col}(u)).

This is easy to check if we understand how
\Gamma
acts on each of the modules involved. If
u=(u_{n})_{n\geq 1}\in\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
, then

\sigma_{a}(u)=(\sigma_{a}(u_{n}))_{n\geq 1}\in\mathscr{U}_{\infty},

and if
f(T)\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
, then

\sigma_{a}(f)(T)=f\big((1+T)^{a}-1\big).

Then:

–

We have

\displaystyle(\sigma_{a}f_{u})(\pi_{n})

\displaystyle=f_{u}((1+\pi_{n})^{a}-1)

\displaystyle=f_{u}(\xi_{p^{n}}^{a}-1)

\displaystyle=f_{u}(\sigma_{a}(\xi_{p^{n}}-1))

\displaystyle=\sigma_{a}(f_{u}(\xi_{p^{n}}-1))=\sigma_{a}(u_{n}),

so that
u\mapsto f_{u}(T)
is
\Gamma
-equivariant.

–

If
f(T)\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}
, then an easy calculation on power series shows that

(12.2)

\partial\log(\sigma_{a}(f))=a\sigma_{a}(\partial\log(f)).

–

On measures, restriction to
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant since the action of
\sigma_{a}
is by multiplying the variable by
a\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, which obviously stabilises both
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
and
p\mathbf{Z}_{p}
.

–

As operations on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=0}
, we have

(12.3)

\partial^{-1}\circ\sigma_{a}=a^{-1}\sigma_{a}\circ\partial^{-1},

as is easily checked on measures. Indeed,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}f(x)\cdot\partial^{-1}\sigma_{a}\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\frac{f(x)}{x}\cdot\sigma_{a}\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\frac{f(ax)}{ax}\cdot\mu

\displaystyle=a^{-1}\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}f(ax)\cdot\partial^{-1}\mu

\displaystyle=a^{-1}\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}f(x)\cdot\sigma_{a}\partial^{-1}\mu.

–

By definition of the action, the inverse Mahler transform
\mathscr{A}^{-1}
is equivariant under
\sigma_{a}
.

Putting all that together, the result follows.
∎

Now, the
\Gamma
-action on
\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
fixes
1\in\mu_{p-1}
, so it stabilises the subspace
\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}
. This action commutes with the
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-action on
\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}
. We deduce that
\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}
is a
\Lambda(\Gamma)
-module. The results of §
12.1
can then be summarised as follows.

Corollary 12.6
.

The map
\mathrm{Col}
restricts to a map
\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}\to\Lambda(\Gamma)
of
\Lambda(\Gamma)
-modules.

Remark 12.7
.

In the construction of
\zeta_{p}
, we renormalised by ‘dividing by
x
’ (in §
4.3
). This appears here via
\partial^{-1}
. We see from (
12.3
) that
\partial^{-1}
really is essential for the Coleman map to be
\Gamma
-equivariant, motivating the appearance of
x^{-1}
in §
4.3
. Conceptually,
\zeta
and
\zeta_{p}
are the
L
-function and
p
-adic
L
-function of the trivial Galois representation
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
, whilst the cyclotomic units in
\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
form an Euler system for its twist
\mathbf{Q}_{p}(1)
; the
\partial^{-1}
bridges between these two Galois representations.

12.2
The fundamental exact sequence

Theorem
11.9
says that the Coleman map induces an isomorphism
\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}^{+}/\mathscr{C}_{\infty,1}^{+}\cong\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})/I(\Gamma^{+})\zeta_{p}
. To prove this, we must study the kernel and cokernel of the Coleman map. We do so here (in Theorem
12.17
) via a careful study of each of its constituent maps.

12.2.1
The logarithmic derivative

We will now show that the logarithmic derivative translates norm-invariance into trace-invariance (recalling the trace operator
\psi
). The key result is Theorem
12.9
. For convenience of notation, and consistency with
[
CS06
]
, we make the following definition.

Definition 12.8
.

For
f(T)\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}
, define its logarithmic derivative as

\displaystyle\Delta(f)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\partial\log f=\frac{\partial f(T)}{f(T)}=(1+T)\frac{f^{\prime}(T)}{f(T)}.

The main result of this section is the following.

Theorem 12.9
.

The logarithmic derivative induces a short exact sequence

0\to\mu_{p-1}\to\big(\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}\big)^{\mathcal{N}=\mathrm{id}}\xrightarrow{\ \ \Delta\ \ }\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=\mathrm{id}}\to 0.

We described the kernel of
\Delta
in Remark
12.4
above, so it suffices to deal with its image. We first prove that this image is contained in
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=\mathrm{id}}
(Lemma
12.10
). We then reduce the proof of surjectivity, via Lemma
12.11
, to surjectivity modulo
p
. Finally, in Lemma
12.12
and Lemma
12.13
we calculate the reduction modulo
p
of both spaces.

For convenience, let
\mathscr{W}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\big(\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}\big)^{\mathcal{N}=\mathrm{id}}
.

Lemma 12.10
.

We have
\Delta(\mathscr{W})\subseteq\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=\mathrm{id}}
.

Proof.

If
f\in\mathscr{W}
, then

\varphi(f)=(\varphi\circ\mathcal{N})(f)=\prod_{\eta\in\mu_{p}}f((1+T)\eta-1).

Applying
\Delta
to the above equality and using the fact that
\Delta\circ\varphi=p\,\varphi\circ\Delta
(which is easy to see on power series from the definitions), we obtain

(\varphi\circ\Delta)(f)=p^{-1}\sum_{\eta\in\mu_{p}}\Delta(f)((1+T)\eta-1)=(\varphi\circ\psi)(\Delta(f)).

By injectivity of
\varphi
, we deduce
\psi(\Delta(f))=\Delta(f)
.
∎

We move now to the proof of surjectivity. In the following, let

A=\overline{\Delta(\mathscr{W})}\subseteq\mathbf{F}_{p}[\![T]\!];\;\;\;B=\overline{\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=\mathrm{id}}}\subseteq\mathbf{F}_{p}[\![T]\!]

be the reduction modulo
p
of the modules we need to compare.

Lemma 12.11
.

If
A=B
, then
\Delta(\mathscr{W})=\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=\mathrm{id}}
.

Proof.

Let
f_{0}\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=\mathrm{id}}
. By hypothesis, there exists a
g_{1}\in\mathscr{W}
such that
\Delta(g_{1})-f_{0}=pf_{1}
for some
f_{1}\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
. Since
\Delta(\mathscr{W})\subseteq\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=\mathrm{id}}
by Lemma
12.10
, we see that
\psi
fixes both
\Delta(g_{1})
and
f_{0}
and hence, by additivity,
\psi
fixes
f_{1}
; so again by hypothesis, there exists some
g_{2}\in\mathscr{W}
such that
\Delta(g_{2})-f_{1}=pf_{2}
for some
f_{2}\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
. By induction, we induce the existence of
g_{i}\in\mathscr{W}
and
f_{i}\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=\mathrm{id}}
,
i\geq 1
, such that

\Delta(g_{i})-f_{i-1}=pf_{i}.

Now let

h_{n}=\prod_{k=1}^{n}g_{k}^{(-1)^{k-1}p^{k-1}}\in\mathscr{W},

i.e.

h_{1}=g_{1},\ \ \ \ h_{2}=\frac{g_{1}}{g_{2}^{p}},\ \ \ \ h_{3}=\frac{g_{1}\cdot g_{3}^{p^{2}}}{g_{2}^{p}},\ \ \ \ h_{4}=\frac{g_{1}\cdot g_{3}^{p^{2}}}{g_{2}^{p}\cdot g_{4}^{p^{3}}},

etc. As
\Delta
transforms multiplication into addition, we have

\displaystyle\Delta(h_{n})

\displaystyle=\Delta(g_{1})-p\Delta(g_{2})+\cdots+(-1)^{n-1}p^{n-1}\Delta(g_{n})

\displaystyle=(f_{0}+pf_{1})-(pf_{1}+p^{2}f_{2})+\cdots+(-1)^{n-1}(p^{n-1}f_{n-1}+p^{n}f_{n})

\displaystyle=f_{0}+(-1)^{n-1}p^{n}f_{n}.

By compactness, the sequence
(h_{n})_{n\geq 1}
admits a convergent subsequence converging to an element
h\in\mathscr{W}
satisfying
\Delta(h)=f_{0}
, which shows the result.
∎

Lemma 12.12
.

We have
\overline{\mathscr{W}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathscr{W}\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp)=\mathbf{F}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}
.

Proof.

The inclusion
\subset
is obvious. Conversely, for any element
f\in\mathbf{F}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}
, lift it to an element
\tilde{f}_{0}\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}
. By points (ii) and (iv) of Lemma
10.11
, the sequence
\mathcal{N}^{k}(\tilde{f}_{0})
converges to an element
\tilde{f}
that is invariant under
\mathcal{N}
and whose reduction modulo
p
is
f
.
∎

The most delicate and technical part of the proof of Theorem
12.9
is contained in the following two lemmas describing the reduction of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=\mathrm{id}}
modulo
p
.

Lemma 12.13
.

We have
B=\Delta(\mathbf{F}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times})
.

Proof.

We have
\Delta(\mathscr{W})\subseteq\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=\mathrm{id}}
by Lemma
12.10
, so the inclusion
\Delta(\mathbf{F}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times})\subset B
is clear using Lemma
12.12
. For the other inclusion, take any
f\in B
and use Lemma
12.14
below to write

f=\Delta(a)+b

for some
a\in\mathbf{F}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}
and
b=\sum_{m=1}^{+\infty}d_{m}\frac{T+1}{T}T^{pm}
. Since
\psi(f)=f
and
\psi(\Delta(a))=\Delta(a)
(by a slight abuse of notation, as
f
and
\Delta(a)
are actually the reduction modulo
p
of elements fixed by
\psi
), we deduce that
\psi(b)=b
. But we can explicitly calculate the action of
\psi
on
b
. Using the identity
13
13

13

Again, this can be easily checked on measures.

\psi(g\cdot\varphi(f))=\psi(g)f
, the identity
T^{pm}=\varphi(T^{m})
in
\mathbf{F}_{p}[\![T]\!]
and the fact that
\psi
fixes
\frac{T+1}{T}
(cf. the proof of Lemma
4.7
), we deduce that

\psi(b)=\sum_{m=1}^{+\infty}d_{m}\frac{T+1}{T}T^{m},

which immediately implies
b=0
and concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 12.14
.

We have

\mathbf{F}_{p}[\![T]\!]=\Delta\Big(\mathbf{F}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times}\Big)+\frac{T+1}{T}C,

where
C=\big\{\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}a_{n}T^{pn}\big\}\subseteq\mathbf{F}_{p}[\![T]\!]
.

Proof.

One inclusion is clear. Take
g\in\mathbf{F}_{p}[\![T]\!]
and write
\frac{T}{T+1}g=\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}a_{n}T^{n}
. Define

h=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m=1\\
(m,p)=1\end{subarray}}^{+\infty}a_{m}\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}T^{mp^{k}}.

Clearly
\frac{T}{T+1}g-h\in C
, so it suffices to show that
\frac{T+1}{T}h\in\Delta(\mathbf{F}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times})
.
Indeed, we will show by induction that, for every
m\geq 1
, there exists
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbf{F}_{p}
for
1\leq i<m
such that

h_{m}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{T+1}{T}h-\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\Delta(1-\alpha_{i}T^{i})\bigg)\in T^{m-1}\mathbf{F}_{p}[\![T]\!].

The case
m=1
is empty. Suppose that the claim is true for
m
and that
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m-1}
have been chosen. Observe first that

\Delta(1-\alpha_{i}T^{i})=-\frac{T+1}{T}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}i\alpha_{i}^{k}T^{ik},

so we can write

h_{m}=\frac{T+1}{T}\sum_{k=m}^{+\infty}d_{k}T^{k}.

Observe that, by construction of
h
and
h_{m}
, we have
d_{n}=d_{np}
for all
n
. If
d_{m}=0
then we set
\alpha_{m}=0
. If
d_{m}\neq 0
then, by what we have just remarked,
m
must be prime to
p
, hence invertible in
\mathbf{F}_{p}
, and we set
\alpha_{m}=-\frac{d_{m}}{m}
. One can then check that

g=\prod_{n=1}^{+\infty}(1-\alpha_{n}T^{n})\in\mathbf{F}_{p}[\![T]\!]

satisfies
\Delta(g)=\frac{T+1}{T}h
, which concludes the proof.
∎

We can now complete the proof of Theorem
12.9
.

Proof of Theorem
12.9
.

By Lemma
12.10
, the map
\Delta
is well-defined, and its kernel is
\mu_{p}
by Remark
12.4
. It remains to prove surjectivity. By Lemma
12.11
, it suffices to prove that
A=B
, which follows directly from Lemma
12.12
and Lemma
12.13
.
∎

12.2.2
The fundamental exact sequence

Finally, we study the fundamental exact sequence describing the kernel and cokernel of the Coleman map. The only remaining map to study is
1-\varphi\circ\psi
. By Theorem
12.9
, it suffices to study this on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=\mathrm{id}}
.

Lemma 12.15
.

There is an exact sequence

0\to\mathbf{Z}_{p}\to\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=\mathrm{id}}\xrightarrow{\ 1-\varphi\ }\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=0}\to\mathbf{Z}_{p}\to 0,

where the first map is the natural inclusion and the last map is evaluation at
T=0
.

Proof.

Injectivity of the first map is trivial. To see surjectivity of the last map, note that
\psi(1+T)=0
, since

(\varphi\circ\psi)(1+T)=p^{-1}\sum_{\eta\in\mu_{p}}\eta(1+T)=0.

Thus
1+T\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=0}
and is mapped to 1 under the last map.

Let
f(T)\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=0}
be in the kernel of the last map, that is be such that
f(0)=0
. Then
\varphi^{n}(f)
goes to zero (in the weak/
(p,T)
-adic topology) and hence
\sum_{n\geq 0}\varphi^{n}(f)
converges to an element
g(T)
whose image under
(1-\varphi)
is
f(T)
. Since
\psi\circ\varphi=\mathrm{id}
, we also have

\psi(g)=\sum_{n\geq 0}\psi\circ\varphi^{n}(f)=\psi(f)+\sum_{n\geq 1}\varphi^{n-1}(f)=g,

as
\psi(f)=0
, which shows that

f\in(1-\varphi)\left(\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=\mathrm{id}}\right)

and hence that the sequence is exact at
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=0}.
Finally, if
f(T)\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
is not constant, then
f(T)=a_{0}+a_{r}T^{r}+\ldots
for some
a_{r}\neq 0
and
\varphi(f)(T)=a_{0}+pa_{r}T^{r}+\ldots\neq f(T)
, which shows that
\ker(1-\varphi)=\mathbf{Z}_{p}
and finishes the proof.
∎

Definition 12.16
.

Let
\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\varprojlim\mu_{p^{n}}
, the module
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
with an action of
\Gamma
by
\sigma\cdot x=\chi(\sigma)x,
recalling
\chi
is the cyclotomic character. This is an integral version of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}(1)
.

Theorem 12.17
.

The Coleman map induces an exact sequence of
\Gamma
-modules

0\to\mu_{p-1}\times\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)\longrightarrow\mathscr{U}_{\infty}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Col}}\Lambda(\Gamma)\longrightarrow\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)\to 0,

where the last map sends
\mu\in\Lambda(\Gamma)
to
\int_{\Gamma}\chi\cdot\mu
. In particular, it induces an exact sequence

0\to\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)\longrightarrow\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Col}}\Lambda(\Gamma)\longrightarrow\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)\to 0

of
\Lambda(\Gamma)
-modules.

Proof.

We first compute the kernel of the Coleman map, which we recall from Definition
10.14
is the composition

\displaystyle\mathrm{Col}:\mathscr{U}_{\infty}\xrightarrow{\ u\mapsto f_{u}(T)\ }(\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\times})^{\mathcal{N}=\mathrm{id}}\xrightarrow{\ \ \Delta\ \ }

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=\mathrm{id}}\xrightarrow{\ 1-\varphi\circ\psi\ }\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=0}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\ \ \partial^{-1}\ \ }\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=0}\xrightarrow{\ \ \mathscr{A}^{-1}\ \ }\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}).

In the third term, we have used Theorem
12.9
to replace
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
with
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]^{\psi=\mathrm{id}}
.

The first map is an isomorphism by Theorem
10.13
. By Theorem
12.9
, the second map surjects with kernel
\mu_{p-1}
. By Lemma
12.15
the third map has kernel
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
; this is the image of
\{(1+T)^{a}:a\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}\}
under
\Delta
. This is the power series interpolating the sequence
(\xi_{p^{n}}^{a})_{n\geq 1}
. Accordingly, when we pull this back to
\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
, we get the factor

\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)=\{(\xi_{p^{n}}^{a})_{n}:a\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}\}\subset\mathscr{U}_{\infty}.

Finally, the fourth and fifth maps are isomorphisms, so ultimately the kernel of
\mathrm{Col}
is as claimed.

We now compute the cokernel. The first two and last two maps in
\mathrm{Col}
are surjective, and the third map has cokernel
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
by Lemma
12.15
, showing the exactness of the sequence.

Finally, we turn to the
\Gamma
-equivariance. The subspace
\mu_{p-1}\times\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)\subset\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
is preserved by
\Gamma
, so the first map is
\Gamma
-equivariant. That
\mathrm{Col}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant was Corollary
12.6
. The last map is
\Gamma
-equivariant since

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\chi(x)\cdot\sigma\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma}\chi(\sigma x)\cdot\mu(x)=\chi(\sigma)\int_{\Gamma}\chi\cdot\mu,

and
\Gamma
acts on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)
through the cyclotomic character
\chi
.
∎

12.3
Generators for the global cyclotomic units

Recall the (global) module of cyclotomic units
\mathscr{D}_{n}
is the intersection of
\mathscr{O}_{F_{n}}^{\times}
with the multiplicative subgroup of
F_{n}^{\times}
generated by
\pm\xi_{p^{n}}
and
\xi_{p^{n}}^{a}-1
with
1\leq a<p^{n}
, and
\mathscr{D}_{n}^{+}=\mathscr{D}_{n}\cap F_{n}^{+}
. We now show this module is cyclic over the group ring
{\mathbf{Z}}[\Gamma_{n}^{+}]
. This is essential preparation for treating the local analogue
\mathscr{C}_{n,1}^{+}
in the next subsection.

Recall that we defined

c_{n}(a)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\xi_{p^{n}}^{a}-1}{\xi_{p^{n}}-1}\in\mathscr{D}_{n},

and note that

\gamma_{n,a}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\xi_{p^{n}}^{(1-a)/2}c_{n}(a)=\frac{\xi_{p^{n}}^{a/2}-\xi_{p^{n}}^{-a/2}}{\xi_{p^{n}}^{1/2}-\xi_{p^{n}}^{-1/2}}

is fixed by conjugation
c\in\Gamma
, hence gives an element of
\mathscr{D}_{n}^{+}
. In fact:

Lemma 12.18
.

Let
n\geq 1
. Then

(i)

The group
\mathscr{D}_{n}^{+}
is generated by
-1
and

\bigg\{\gamma_{n,a}\;:\;1<a<\frac{p^{n}}{2},(a,p)=1\bigg\}.

(ii)

The group
\mathscr{D}_{n}
is generated by
\xi_{p^{n}}
and
\mathscr{D}_{n}^{+}
.

Proof.

We first show that we need only consider those elements
\xi_{p^{n}}^{a}-1
with
a
prime to
p
. Indeed, this follows from the identity

\xi_{p^{n}}^{bp^{m}}-1=\prod_{j=0}^{p^{m}-1}(\xi_{p^{n}}^{b+jp^{n-m}}-1),

where
(b,p)=1
and
1\leq m<n
, noting that
b+jp^{n-m}
is prime to
p
. Also, since
\xi_{p^{n}}^{a}-1=-\xi_{p^{n}}^{a}(\xi_{p^{n}}^{-a}-1)
, we can restrict to considering
1\leq a<\frac{1}{2}p^{n}
(recall here that
p
is odd).

Suppose that

\gamma=\pm\xi_{p^{n}}^{d}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq a<\frac{1}{2}p^{n}\\
(a,p)=1\end{subarray}}(\xi_{p^{n}}^{a}-1)^{e_{a}}\in\mathscr{D}_{n},

for some integers
d
and
e_{a}
. Since
v_{p}(\xi_{p^{n}}^{d})=0
and all the
p
-adic valuations of
\xi_{p^{n}}^{a}-1
coincide (namely,
v_{p}(\xi_{p^{n}}^{a}-1)=\frac{1}{(p-1)p^{n-1}}
), we deduce that
\sum_{a}e_{a}=0
. Therefore we can write

\gamma=\pm\xi_{p^{n}}^{d}\prod_{a}\bigg(\frac{\xi_{p^{n}}^{a}-1}{\xi_{p^{n}}-1}\bigg)^{e_{a}}=\pm\xi_{p^{n}}^{e}\prod_{a}\gamma_{n,a}^{e_{a}},

where
e=d+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{a}e_{a}(a-1)
. This shows the second point; the first point follows by observing that every term
\gamma_{n,a}^{e_{a}}
of the product is real, so
\gamma\in\mathscr{D}_{n}^{+}
if and only if
e=0
.
∎

Corollary 12.19
.

If
a
generates
({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}
, then
\gamma_{n,a}
generates
\mathscr{D}^{+}_{n}
as a
{\mathbf{Z}}[\Gamma_{n}^{+}]
-module.

Proof.

If
1\leq b<p^{n}
is prime to
p
, then
b\equiv a^{r}
(mod
p^{n}
) for some
r\geq 0
, and hence

\gamma_{n,b}=\frac{\xi_{p^{n}}^{a^{r}}-1}{\xi_{p^{n}}-1}=\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}\frac{\xi_{p^{n}}^{a^{i+1}}-1}{\xi_{p^{n}}^{a^{i}}-1}=\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}(\gamma_{n,a})^{\sigma_{a}^{i}}.\qed

12.4
Generators for the local cyclotomic units

We now move to analogous results for the local cyclotomic units, that is, the
p
-adic closure of the global cyclotomic units.

A natural guess might be that ‘as
\mathscr{D}_{n}^{+}
is generated by
\gamma_{n,a}
as a
{\mathbf{Z}}[\Gamma_{n}^{+}]
-module, then
\mathscr{C}_{n}^{+}
is generated by
\gamma_{n,a}
as a
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n}^{+}]
-module’. However, this is nonsense, as
\mathscr{C}_{n}^{+}
is not a
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-module; to parse this, we must pass to the principal cyclotomic units
\mathscr{C}_{n,1}^{+}
. The following simple lemma then describes precisely the
p
-adic closure.

Lemma 12.20
.

Let
g_{1},\dots,g_{r}\in\mathscr{U}_{n,1}
, and let
X=\langle g_{1},\dots,g_{r}\rangle\subset\mathscr{U}_{n,1}
be the
{\mathbf{Z}}
-module they generate (multiplicatively
14
14

14

By which we mean: the
{\mathbf{Z}}
-module structure is given by exponentiation, i.e.
(a,g)\mapsto g^{a}
for
a\in{\mathbf{Z}}
(or
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
). As is standard, but perhaps confusingly, we sometimes use additive notation
ag
for this module structure.
). Then the
p
-adic closure
\overline{X}
of
X
in
\mathscr{U}_{n,1}
is the
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-submodule of
\mathscr{U}_{n,1}
generated by
g_{1},\dots,g_{r}
.

Proof.

Let
a\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, and
a_{j}
be any sequence of integers tending to
a\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. Then

(12.4)

g_{i}^{a_{j}}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\begin{pmatrix}a_{j}\\
k\end{pmatrix}(g_{i}-1)^{k}\longrightarrow\sum_{k\geq 0}\begin{pmatrix}a\\
k\end{pmatrix}(g_{i}-1)^{k}=g_{i}^{a},

since
g_{i}-1\equiv 0\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0pt\mathfrak{p}_{n})
(as
g_{i}\in\mathscr{U}_{n,1}
). Thus
g_{i}^{a}
lies in the
p
-adic closure. An identical argument shows more generally that the
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-span of the
g_{i}
’s is a subset of the
p
-adic closure.

To see the converse, let
g\in\overline{X}
. Then by definition there exists a sequence
(g_{1}^{a_{1,j}}\cdots g_{r}^{a_{r,j}})_{j}\subset X
tending to
g
, with each
a_{i,j}\in{\mathbf{Z}}
. This gives a sequence
(a_{1,j},\dots,a_{r,j})_{j}\subset{\mathbf{Z}}^{r}\subset\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{r}
. By compactness of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{r}
, there exists a convergent subsequence

(b_{1,k},\dots,b_{r,k})_{k}\to(b_{1},\dots,b_{r})\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{r}.

Then
(g_{1}^{b_{1,k}}\cdots g_{r}^{b_{r,k}})_{k}
converges to
g_{1}^{b_{1}}\cdots g_{r}^{b_{r}}
by (
12.4
); but by construction it also converges to
g
. Thus
g=g_{1}^{b_{1}}\cdots g_{r}^{b_{r}}
, showing that
g
lies in the
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-span of the
g_{i}
’s, as required.
∎

We now put Corollary
12.19
into a form useful for Lemma
12.20
.

Lemma 12.21
.

Let
a\in{\mathbf{Z}}
be a topological generator of
{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}
, and
w\in\mu_{p-1}\subset\mathscr{U}_{n}
be such that
aw\equiv 1\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0pt\mathfrak{p}_{n})
. Then:

(i)

w\gamma_{n,a}\in\mathscr{U}_{n,1}
,

(ii)

(w\gamma_{n,a})^{p-1}=\gamma_{n,a}^{p-1}
lies in
\mathscr{U}_{n,1}^{+}
, and generates the cyclic
{\mathbf{Z}}[\Gamma_{n}^{+}]
-module

{\mathbf{Z}}[\Gamma_{n}^{+}]\cdot(w\gamma_{n,a})^{p-1}=(p-1)\mathscr{D}_{n}^{+}=\{\gamma^{p-1}:\gamma\in\mathscr{D}_{n}^{+}\}\subset\mathscr{U}_{n,1}^{+}.

Proof.

(i) We first claim that
\gamma_{n,a}\equiv a\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0pt\mathfrak{p}_{n})
. Indeed, by definition
\gamma_{n,a}=\xi_{p^{n}}^{a/2}c_{n}(a)
. Recall
\pi_{n}=\xi_{p^{n}}-1
is a uniformiser in
\mathfrak{p}_{n}
, so
\xi_{p^{n}}^{a/2}\equiv 1\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0pt\mathfrak{p}_{n})
. It thus suffices to show

c_{n}(a)\equiv a\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0pt\mathfrak{p}_{n}).

For this, recall that by construction of the Coleman power series attached to any unit
u=(u_{n})\in\mathscr{U}_{\infty}
, we have

u_{n}=f_{u}(\pi_{n})\equiv f_{u}(0)\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0pt\mathfrak{p}_{n}).

For
u=c(a)
, recall we have
f_{c(a)}=((1+T)^{a}-1)/T
; so
c_{n}(a)\equiv f_{c(a)}(0)=a\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0pt\mathfrak{p}_{n})
, proving the claim. Thus
w
is the unique element of
\mu_{p-1}
such that
w\gamma_{n,a}\equiv 1\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0pt\mathfrak{p}_{n})
, and hence
w\gamma_{n,a}\in\mathscr{U}_{n,1}
, as required.

(ii) By Corollary
12.19
, we know
\gamma_{n,a}
generates
\mathscr{D}_{n}^{+}
, and deduce
\gamma_{n,a}^{p-1}
generates
(p-1)\mathscr{D}_{n}^{+}
. In particular
\gamma_{n,a}^{p-1}
lies in
\mathscr{U}_{n,1}^{+}
. As
w^{p-1}=1
, we have
\gamma_{n,a}^{p-1}=(w\gamma_{n,a})^{p-1}
, giving (ii).
∎

Lemma 12.22
.

Let
a\in{\mathbf{Z}}
be a topological generator of
{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}
, and
w\in\mu_{p-1}\subset\mathscr{U}_{n}
be such that
aw\equiv 1\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0pt\mathfrak{p}_{n})
. Then:

(i)

The module
\mathscr{C}_{n,1}^{+}
is a cyclic
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\Gamma^{+}_{n}]
-module generated by
w\gamma_{n,a}
.

(ii)

The module
\mathscr{C}^{+}_{\infty,1}
is a cyclic
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
-module generated by
(w\gamma_{n,a})_{n\geq 1}
.

Proof.

(i) By Lemma
12.21
(ii),

(p-1)\mathscr{D}_{n}^{+}=(p-1)\mathscr{D}_{n,1}^{+}\subset\mathscr{U}_{n,1}^{+}

is generated as a
{\mathbf{Z}}[\Gamma^{+}_{n}]
-module by
(w\gamma_{n,a})^{p-1}
. By Lemma
12.20
the
p
-adic closure
(p-1)\mathscr{C}_{n,1}^{+}
of
(p-1)\mathscr{D}_{n,1}^{+}
is generated as a
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\Gamma^{+}_{n}]
-module by
(w\gamma_{n,a})^{p-1}
. As
p-1
is invertible in
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, we conclude
(p-1)\mathscr{C}_{n,1}^{+}=\mathscr{C}_{n,1}^{+}
is generated by
w\gamma_{n,a}
. We use here that
w\gamma_{n,a}\equiv 1\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0pt\mathfrak{p}_{n})
, so that
w\gamma_{n,a}
is the unique
(p-1)
-th root of
(w\gamma_{n,a})^{p-1}
lying in
\mathscr{C}_{n,1}^{+}
.

(ii) Observe that

\mathscr{C}_{\infty,1}^{+}\cong\varprojlim\mathscr{C}_{n,1}^{+}=\varprojlim\Big(\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n}^{+}]\cdot w\gamma_{n,a}\Big)\cong\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})\cdot(w\gamma_{n,a})_{n},

as required, with all maps as
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
-modules and where the middle equality is (i).
∎

12.5
End of the proof

Finally we can prove Iwasawa’s Theorem
11.9
.

Theorem 12.23
.

The Coleman map induces:

(i)

A short exact sequence of
\Lambda(\Gamma)
-modules

0\to\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}/\mathscr{C}_{\infty,1}\to\Lambda(\Gamma)/I(\Gamma)\zeta_{p}\to\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)\to 0.

(ii)

An isomorphism of
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
-modules

\mathscr{U}^{+}_{\infty,1}/\mathscr{C}^{+}_{\infty,1}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})/I(\Gamma^{+})\zeta_{p}.

Proof.

Theorem
12.17
gave an exact sequence of
\Lambda(\Gamma)
-modules

0\to\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)\longrightarrow\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Col}}\Lambda(\Gamma)\longrightarrow\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)\to 0.

The theorem will follow by calculating the image of the modules
\mathscr{C}_{\infty,1}
and
\mathscr{C}^{+}_{\infty,1}
under the Coleman map. By Lemma
12.22
, it suffices to calculate the image under
\mathrm{Col}
of an element
(\xi_{p^{n}}^{b}\gamma_{n,a})_{n\geq 1}\in\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}
, for
a,b\in{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}
. But this has already been done: by Theorem
10.15
, and the fact that
\xi_{p^{n}}^{b}
lies in the kernel of the Coleman map, we know that

\mathrm{Col}\big((\xi_{p^{n}}^{b}\gamma_{n,a})_{n\geq 1}\big)=\mathrm{Col}\left(\xi_{p^{n}}^{-(1-a)/2}(\gamma_{n,a})_{n\geq 1}\right)=\mathrm{Col}\big(c(a)\big)=([\sigma_{a}]-[1])\zeta_{p},

where as usual
\sigma_{a}
denotes an element of
\Gamma
such that
\chi(\sigma_{a})=a.
Since
a\in{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}
was arbitrary, we conclude that the image of
\mathscr{C}_{\infty,1}
(resp.
\mathscr{C}^{+}_{\infty,1}
) under
\mathrm{Col}
is
I(\Gamma)\zeta_{p}
(resp.
I(\Gamma^{+})\zeta_{p}
). We deduce an exact sequence

0\to\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}/\mathscr{C}_{\infty,1}\longrightarrow\Lambda(\Gamma)/I(\Gamma)\zeta_{p}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)\to 0.

This shows (i). Since
p
is odd, taking invariants under the group
\langle c\rangle\subset\Gamma
of order two generated by complex conjugation is exact. As
c
acts on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)
by
-1
, we see that
\mathbf{Z}_{p}(1)^{\langle c\rangle}=0
, which shows (ii) and concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

13
The Iwasawa Main Conjecture

We now move from arithmetic to algebra. To state the Iwasawa Main Conjecture, we use the structure theory of
\Lambda
-modules. We first summarise this theory. We then define modules from the Galois theory of abelian extensions that will be needed. These modules carry an action of the Galois group
\Gamma=\mathrm{Gal}(F_{\infty}/{\mathbf{Q}})\cong\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, and hence obtain the structure of
\Lambda(\Gamma)
-modules. The Iwasawa Main Conjecture describes the characteristic ideal of one of these Galois modules in terms of the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function.

In the interests of space, where necessary, we state without proof relevant auxiliary results.

13.1
Structure theory for
\Lambda
-modules

There is a rich structure theory of modules over Iwasawa algebras, which looks similar to that of modules over PIDs. Here we state (without proof) some basic yet fundamental results.

Let
L
be a finite extension of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
,
\mathscr{O}_{L}
its ring of integers and let
\Lambda\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p})=\varprojlim\mathscr{O}_{L}[\mathbf{Z}_{p}/p^{n}\mathbf{Z}_{p}]\cong\mathscr{O}_{L}[\![T]\!]
be the Iwasawa algebra of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
over
\mathscr{O}_{L}
. Let
M,M^{\prime}
be two
\Lambda
-modules. We say that
M
is pseudo-isomorphic to
M^{\prime}
, and we write
M\sim M^{\prime}
, if there exists a homomorphism
M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
with finite kernel and cokernel, i.e, if there is an exact sequence

0\to A\to M\to M^{\prime}\to B\to 0,

with
A
and
B
finite
\Lambda
-modules (just in case:
A
and
B
have finite cardinality!). We remark that
\sim
is
not
an equivalence relation (see
[
Was97
, Warning, §13.2]
) but it
is
an equivalence relation between
finitely generated, torsion

\Lambda
-modules. The following is the main result concerning the structure theory of finitely generated
\Lambda
-modules.

Theorem 13.1
.

[
Was97
, Thm. 13.12]
. Let
M
be a finitely generated
\Lambda
-module. Then

M\sim\Lambda^{r}\oplus\bigg(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}\Lambda/(p^{n_{i}})\bigg)\oplus\bigg(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{t}\Lambda/(f_{j}(T)^{m_{j}})\bigg),

for some
r,s,t\geq 0
,
n_{i},m_{j}\geq 1
and irreducible distinguished polynomials
f_{j}(T)\in\mathscr{O}[T]
.

Here we call a polynomial
P(T)\in\mathscr{O}_{L}[T]

distinguished
if
P(T)=a_{0}+a_{1}T+\ldots+a_{n-1}T^{n-1}+T^{n}
with
a_{i}\in\mathfrak{p}
for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
.

Remark 13.2
.

We do
not
have a similar result for the finite level group algebras
\mathscr{O}_{L}[\mathbf{Z}_{p}/p^{n}\mathbf{Z}_{p}]
, only for the projective limit. This is another major example of the fundamental concept of Iwasawa theory, where it is profitable to study a whole tower of objects all in one go, rather than individually at finite level.

Definition 13.3
.

Suppose
M
is a finitely generated torsion
\Lambda
-module. Then
r=0
in the structure theorem. We define the
characteristic ideal
of
M
to be the ideal

\mathrm{Ch}_{\Lambda}(M)=\left(p^{n}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{t}\left(f_{j}^{m_{j}}\right)\subset\Lambda,\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where }n=\sum_{i=1}^{s}n_{i}.

We will apply this theory more generally. Suppose
\Gamma=H\times\Gamma^{\prime}
, where
H
is a finite commutative group of order prime to
p
and
\Gamma^{\prime}\cong\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. A key example to have in mind is
\Gamma={{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}
,
H=\mu_{p-1}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}=1+p\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. Then we have a decomposition

\Lambda(\Gamma)\cong\mathscr{O}_{L}[H]\otimes\Lambda.

Let
M
be a finitely generated torsion
\Lambda(\Gamma)
-module. Let
H^{\wedge}
denote the group of characters of
H
and define, for any
\omega\in H^{\wedge}
, the projector to the isotypic component

e_{\omega}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{|H|}\sum_{a\in H}\omega^{-1}(a)[a]\in\mathscr{O}_{L}[H],

possibly after extending
L
by adjoining the values of
\omega
. As the order of
H
is prime to
p
, one can easily show the following result.

Lemma 13.4

(
[
CS06
, A.1]
)
.

The group
H
acts on
M^{(\omega)}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=e_{\omega}M
via multiplication by
\omega
and we have a decomposition of
\Lambda(\Gamma)
-modules

M=\oplus_{\omega\in H^{\wedge}}M^{(\omega)}.

Moreover, each
M^{(\omega)}
is a finitely generated torsion
\Lambda
-module.

Definition 13.5
.

Let
\Gamma=H\times\mathbf{Z}_{p}
be as above and let
M
be a finitely generated torsion
\Lambda(\Gamma)
-module. We define the
characteristic ideal
of
M
to be the ideal
