\mathrm{Ch}_{\Lambda(\Gamma)}(M)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\oplus_{\omega\in H^{\wedge}}\mathrm{Ch}_{\Lambda}(M^{(\omega)})\subseteq\Lambda(\Gamma).

Lemma 13.6

(
[
CS06
, A.1 Prop. 1]
)
.

The characteristic ideal is multiplicative in exact sequences.

13.2
The
\Lambda
-modules arising from Galois theory

The following
\Lambda
-modules will be the protagonists of the Galois side of the Main Conjecture; we urge the reader to refer back to the following definitions as these objects appear in the text. Recall
F_{n}={\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p^{n}})
, that
\mathfrak{p}_{n}
is the unique prime above
p
in
F_{n}
, and other similar notation from §
9
. Then define

\mathscr{M}_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\text{maximal abelian $p$-extension of $F_{n}$ unramified outside $\mathfrak{p}_{n}$},

\mathscr{M}^{+}_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\text{maximal abelian $p$-extension of $F^{+}_{n}$ unramified outside $\mathfrak{p}_{n}^{+}$},

\mathscr{L}_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\text{maximal unramified abelian $p$-extension of $F_{n}$},

\mathscr{L}^{+}_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\text{maximal unramified abelian $p$-extension of $F^{+}_{n}$},

and set

\mathscr{M}_{\infty}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\cup_{n\geq 1}\mathscr{M}_{n}=\text{maximal abelian pro-$p$-extension of $F_{\infty}$ unramified outside $\mathfrak{p}$},

\mathscr{M}^{+}_{\infty}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\cup_{n\geq 1}\mathscr{M}^{+}_{n}=\text{maximal abelian pro-$p$-extension of $F^{+}_{\infty}$ unramified outside $\mathfrak{p}^{+}$},

\mathscr{L}_{\infty}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\cup_{n\geq 1}\mathscr{L}_{n}=\text{maximal unramified abelian pro-$p$-extension of $F_{\infty}$},

\mathscr{L}^{+}_{\infty}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\cup_{n\geq 1}\mathscr{L}^{+}_{n}=\text{maximal unramified abelian pro-$p$-extension of $F^{+}_{\infty}$}.

Finally, define

\mathscr{X}_{\infty}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{Gal}(\mathscr{M}_{\infty}/F_{\infty}),\;\;\;\mathscr{X}^{+}_{\infty}=\mathrm{Gal}(\mathscr{M}^{+}_{\infty}/F^{+}_{\infty}),

\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{Gal}(\mathscr{L}_{\infty}/F_{\infty}),\;\;\;\mathscr{Y}^{+}_{\infty}=\mathrm{Gal}(\mathscr{L}^{+}_{\infty}/F^{+}_{\infty}).

These modules fit into the following diagram of field extensions:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{M}_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{M}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}

There is an identical diagram for the totally real objects, with superscripts
+
everywhere.

Remark 13.7
.

In the limit, the module
\mathscr{X}_{\infty}
is a
\Lambda(\Gamma)
-module, and thus can be studied via Theorem
13.1
. To describe this, let
x\in\mathscr{X}_{\infty}
and
\sigma\in\Gamma
, and choose any lifting
\tilde{\sigma}\in\mathrm{Gal}(\mathscr{M}_{\infty}/{\mathbf{Q}})
of
\sigma
; then

\sigma\cdot x\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\tilde{\sigma}x\tilde{\sigma}^{-1}

gives a well defined action of
\Gamma
on
\mathscr{X}_{\infty}
. As
\mathscr{O}_{L}[\Gamma]
is dense in
\Lambda(\Gamma)
, and the latter is Hausdorff, this action extends by linearity and continuity to an action of
\Lambda(\Gamma)
on
\mathscr{X}_{\infty}
. In exactly the same way we define actions of
\Lambda(\Gamma)
on
\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}
and of
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
on
\mathscr{X}^{+}_{\infty}
and
\mathscr{Y}^{+}_{\infty}
.

13.3
The Main Conjecture

Recall the ideal
I(\Gamma^{+})\zeta_{p}\subset\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
, and that this encodes the zeros of
\zeta_{p}
. We already gave an arithmetic description of this ideal in Theorem
11.9
in terms of cyclotomic units. The Iwasawa Main Conjecture upgrades this to the following:

Theorem 13.8

(Iwasawa Main Conjecture)
.

\mathscr{X}^{+}_{\infty}
is a finitely generated torsion
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
-module, and

\mathrm{ch}_{\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})}(\mathscr{X}^{+}_{\infty})=I(\Gamma^{+})\zeta_{p}.

Remark 13.9
.

It is usual in the literature to formulate the Iwasawa Main Conjecture in terms of an even Dirichlet character of
\mathrm{Gal}({\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p})/{\mathbf{Q}})
. As one can already observe from the behaviour of the Bernoulli numbers, there exists a certain dichotomy involving the parity of this character which makes the formulation of the Main Conjecture different in the even and odd cases. The above formulation takes into account every such even Dirichlet character. For a formulation of the Main Conjecture for odd Dirichlet characters, see
[
HK03
]
.

13.4
The Iwasawa Main Conjecture for Vandiver primes

Definition 13.10
.

Let
h_{n}^{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\#\mathrm{Cl}(F_{n}^{+})
be the class number of
F_{n}^{+}
. We say
p
is a
Vandiver prime
if
p\nmid h_{1}^{+}
.

The rest of these notes are dedicated to the following theorem of Iwasawa.

Theorem 13.11
.

If
p
is a Vandiver prime, we have an isomorphism of
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
-modules

\mathscr{X}^{+}_{\infty}\cong\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})/I(\Gamma^{+})\zeta_{p}.

In particular, Iwasawa’s Main Conjecture holds.

The arguments of this section form the origins of Iwasawa’s formulation of the Main Conjecture, and give further motivation for it. As our main goal is the study of
p
-adic
L
-functions, we omit the proofs of some more classical auxiliary results. Our approach follows that of
[
CS06
, §4.5]
, which we suggest the reader consults for a more detailed exposition.

We first use class field theory to reinterpret Theorem
12.23
in terms of some modules arising from Galois theory.

Definition 13.12
.

For any
n\geq 1
, define
\mathscr{E}_{n}
as the
p
-adic closure of the global units
\mathscr{V}_{n}=\mathscr{O}_{F_{n}}^{\times}
inside the local units
\mathscr{U}_{n}
, let
\mathscr{E}^{+}_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathscr{E}_{n}\cap\mathscr{U}_{n}^{+}
, and let

\mathscr{E}_{n,1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathscr{E}_{n}\cap\mathscr{U}_{n,1},\;\;\;\mathscr{E}^{+}_{n,1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathscr{E}^{+}_{n}\cap\mathscr{U}_{n,1};

\mathscr{E}_{\infty,1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\varprojlim_{n\geq 1}\mathscr{E}_{n,1},\;\;\;\mathscr{E}^{+}_{\infty,1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\varprojlim_{n\geq 1}\mathscr{E}^{+}_{n,1}.

Leopoldt’s conjecture (which is known in this case by a theorem of Brumer) states that for any
n\geq 1
, the group
\mathscr{E}_{n}
is a finite
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-module of rank
r_{1}+r_{2}-1=p^{n-1}(p-1)/2
. Here as usual
r_{1}
(resp.
r_{2}
) denotes the number of real (resp. half the number of complex) embeddings of
F_{n}
into
{\mathbf{C}}
. In light of Lemma
12.20
, the conjecture says that if global units are (multiplicatively) independent over
{\mathbf{Z}}
, then they are independent over
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
.

Proposition 13.13
.

There is an exact sequence of
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
-modules

0\to\mathscr{E}^{+}_{\infty,1}\to\mathscr{U}^{+}_{\infty,1}\to\mathrm{Gal}(\mathscr{M}^{+}_{\infty}/\mathscr{L}^{+}_{\infty})\to 0.

Proof.

Global class field theory (see
[
Was97
, Cor. 13.6]
) gives a short exact sequence

(13.1)

0\to\mathscr{E}^{+}_{n,1}\to\mathscr{U}^{+}_{n,1}\to\mathrm{Gal}(\mathscr{M}^{+}_{n}/\mathscr{L}^{+}_{n})\to 0.

Taking the inverse limit over
n
gives the result. This is exact as all modules in the sequence above are finitely generated
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-modules (so satisfy the Mittag-Leffler condition).
∎

We now rewrite the terms in this sequence, moving it closer to Theorem
13.11
. Motivated by Theorem
11.9
, we also introduce
\mathscr{C}_{\infty,1}^{+}
into the picture. Then:

Corollary 13.14
.

We have an exact sequence of
\Lambda(G)
-modules

0\to\mathscr{E}^{+}_{\infty,1}/\mathscr{C}^{+}_{\infty,1}\to\mathscr{U}^{+}_{\infty,1}/\mathscr{C}^{+}_{\infty,1}\to\mathscr{X}^{+}_{\infty}\to\mathscr{Y}^{+}_{\infty}\to 0.

Proof.

The fundamental theorem of Galois theory yields a short exact sequence

0\to\mathrm{Gal}(\mathscr{M}_{\infty}^{+}/\mathscr{L}_{\infty}^{+})\to\mathscr{X}_{\infty}^{+}\to\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}^{+}\to 0.

The result now follows from Proposition
13.13
, as

\mathrm{Gal}(\mathscr{M}_{\infty}^{+}/\mathscr{L}_{\infty}^{+})\cong\mathscr{E}_{\infty,1}^{+}/\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}^{+}\cong\big(\mathscr{E}_{\infty,1}^{+}/\mathscr{C}_{\infty,1}^{+}\big)/\big(\mathscr{U}_{\infty,1}^{+}/\mathscr{C}_{\infty,1}^{+}\big),

the last identification being the third isomorphism theorem.
∎

Key to the proof of Theorem
13.11
is the following result from classical Iwasawa theory. For the sake of completeness we will give in Appendix
A
an introduction to this topic, including in particular a proof of the following result. Let

(13.2)

\mathscr{Y}_{n}^{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{Gal}(\mathscr{L}_{n}^{+}/F_{n}^{+})\cong\mathrm{Cl}(F_{n}^{+})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}}\mathbf{Z}_{p}.

Proposition 13.15

(
[
Was97
, Prop. 13.22]
)
.

For all
n\geq 0
, we have

(\mathscr{Y}^{+}_{\infty})_{\Gamma^{+}_{n}}=\mathscr{Y}_{n}^{+},

where
\Gamma^{+}_{n}=\mathrm{Gal}(F^{+}_{\infty}/F^{+}_{n})
and the left-hand side is the module of coinvariants.

Proof.

See Proposition
A.6
.
∎

Corollary 13.16
.

If
p
is a Vandiver prime, then:

(i)

\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}^{+}=0
;

(ii)

p\nmid h_{n}^{+}
for any
n\geq 1
;

(iii)

\mathscr{E}^{+}_{\infty,1}/\mathscr{C}^{+}_{\infty,1}=0
.

Proof.

By (
13.2
), we deduce that
p\nmid h_{n}^{+}
if and only if
\mathscr{Y}_{n}^{+}=0
.

(i) By Proposition
13.15
, if
p\nmid h_{1}^{+}
, then
0=\mathscr{Y}_{1}^{+}=(\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}^{+})_{\Gamma_{0}}=0
. By Nakayama’s lemma, this implies that
\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}^{+}=0
.

(ii) Combining (i) with Proposition
13.15
shows
\mathscr{Y}_{n}^{+}=0
, hence the result.

(iii) In Theorem
11.7
we saw that
[\mathscr{V}_{n}^{+}:\mathscr{D}_{n}^{+}]=h_{n}^{+}
, which is prime to
p
by (ii). We claim further that

(13.3)

[\mathscr{V}_{n,1}^{+}:\mathscr{D}_{n,1}^{+}]\text{ is prime to }p.

Indeed, note
\mathscr{D}_{n,1}^{+}=\mathscr{V}_{n,1}^{+}\cap\mathscr{D}_{n}^{+}
by definition; so applying the isomorphism theorem
S/(S\cap N)\cong SN/N\leq G/N
to the subgroups
S=\mathscr{V}_{n,1}^{+}
and
N=\mathscr{D}_{n}^{+}
of
G=\mathscr{V}_{n}^{+}
, we see that
\mathscr{V}_{n,1}^{+}/\mathscr{D}_{n,1}^{+}
is isomorphic to a subgroup of
\mathscr{V}_{n}^{+}/\mathscr{D}_{n}^{+}
, hence has order dividing
h_{n}^{+}
, which is prime to
p
. Hence there is an exact sequence

0\to\mathscr{D}^{+}_{n,1}\to\mathscr{V}^{+}_{n,1}\to W_{n}\to 0,

where
W_{n}
is a finite group of order prime to
p
. Applying
-\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}}\mathbf{Z}_{p}
to every term, we get

\mathscr{D}^{+}_{n,1}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{Z}_{p}\cong\mathscr{V}^{+}_{n,1}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{Z}_{p}.

Recall now that
\mathscr{E}^{+}_{n,1}
(resp.
\mathscr{C}^{+}_{n,1}
) is by definition the
p
-adic closure of
\mathscr{V}^{+}_{n,1}
(resp.
\mathscr{D}^{+}_{n,1}
) inside
\mathscr{U}^{+}_{n,1}
, and that
\mathscr{C}^{+}_{n,1}\subseteq\mathscr{E}^{+}_{n,1}
. By Lemma
12.20
, we have natural surjections
\mathscr{D}^{+}_{n,1}\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}}\mathbf{Z}_{p}\to\mathscr{C}^{+}_{n,1}
and
\mathscr{V}^{+}_{n,1}\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}}\mathbf{Z}_{p}\to\mathscr{E}^{+}_{n,1}
, making the diagram

\textstyle{\mathscr{D}_{n,1}^{+}\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}}\mathbf{Z}_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathscr{V}_{n,1}^{+}\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}}\mathbf{Z}_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}_{n,1}^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{E}_{n,1}^{+}}

commute. Thus the inclusion
\mathscr{C}^{+}_{n,1}\to\mathscr{E}^{+}_{n,1}
is surjective, so an isomorphism. Taking inverse limits yields
\mathscr{C}_{\infty,1}^{+}\cong\mathscr{E}^{+}_{\infty,1}
, which finishes the proof.
∎

We can now easily finish the proof of Iwasawa Main Conjecture for Vandiver primes.

Proof.

By Corollaries
13.14
and
13.16
(i,iii) (for the first isomorphism) and Theorem
11.9
(for the second), we have

\mathscr{X}^{+}_{\infty}\cong\mathscr{U}^{+}_{\infty,1}/\mathscr{C}^{+}_{\infty,1}\cong\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})/I(\Gamma^{+})\zeta_{p}.

In particular,

\mathrm{ch}_{\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})}(\mathscr{X}_{\infty}^{+})=\mathrm{ch}_{\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})}\big(\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})/I(\Gamma^{+})\zeta_{p}\big)=I(\Gamma^{+})\zeta_{p}.\qed

Remark 13.17
.

Conjecturally, every prime is a Vandiver prime, and under this conjecture we have proved the full Iwasawa Main Conjecture. The conditional proof above was due to Iwasawa himself. The first full proof of the Iwasawa Main Conjecture was given by Mazur–Wiles
[
MW84
]
. For a description of another proof, using Euler systems and due to Kolyvagin, Rubin, and Thaine, see
[
CS06
]
and
[
Lan90
]
.

13.5
Generalisations: Selmer groups,
p
-adic
L
-functions and Iwasawa–Greenberg Main Conjectures

Our focus throughout has been on Iwasawa’s original Main Conjecture. We conclude with a sketch of a formulation due to Greenberg
[
Gre89
]
of a Main Conjecture for more general Galois representations, which, for the trivial Galois representation, recovers Theorem
13.8
. In order to do this, we first need to introduce Selmer groups. These are fundamental objects in arithmetic, lying at the core of two very important conjectures concerning
L
-functions: Iwasawa Main Conjectures and Bloch–Kato conjectures. The reader interested in this beautiful theory can learn more from the references
[
Gre89
]
,
[
Bel09
]
,
[
Rub00
]
,
[
Kat04a
]
,
[
PR95b
]
, and
[
Ski18
]
.

13.5.1
Selmer groups over
F_{\infty}^{+}

Selmer groups are objects that generalise one of the sides of the Iwasawa Main Conjecture. Let us start with a general definition. For compatibility with our earlier study, we will work over the field
F_{\infty}^{+}={\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})^{+}
, which we denote
\mathcal{F}
(to ease notation). This differs from
[
Gre89
]
, who works over the cyclotomic
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension
{\mathbf{Q}}_{\infty}/{\mathbf{Q}}
(cf. Remark
13.9
). We continue to take coefficients in a fixed finite extension
L/\mathbf{Q}_{p}
.

Definition 13.18
.

(1)

Let
M
be a topological
\mathcal{O}_{L}
-module equipped with a continuous
\mathcal{O}_{L}
-linear action of
\mathscr{G}_{\mathcal{F}}
, which is unramified outside a finite set of places.

(2)

A
Selmer structure

\mathcal{L}=(\mathcal{L}_{v})_{v}
for
M
over
\mathcal{F}
is a choice of subspace
\mathcal{L}_{v}\subseteq H^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{v},M)
for every finite place
v
of
\mathcal{F}
such that, for almost all
v
, we have
\mathcal{L}_{v}=H^{1}_{\mathrm{ur}}(\mathcal{F}_{v},M)

15
15

15

Here, the
unramified cohomology groups
are defined as
H^{1}_{\mathrm{ur}}(\mathcal{F}_{v},M)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{ker}(H^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{v},M)\to H^{1}(I_{\mathcal{F}_{v}},M))
, where
I_{\mathcal{F}_{v}}
denotes the inertia group of
\mathcal{F}_{v}
and the map is restriction.
.

(3)

Given a Selmer structure
\mathcal{L}=(\mathcal{L}_{v})_{v}
for
M
, we define the
Selmer group
to be

H^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F},M)=\ker\left(H^{1}(\mathcal{F},M)\to\bigoplus_{v}H^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{v},M)/\mathcal{L}_{v}\right),

where
v
runs over every finite place of
\mathcal{F}
.

In what follows, we will only be interested in Selmer structures with unramified local conditions for all places
v
not dividing
p
. Let
T
be a finite free
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-module equipped with an action of
\mathscr{G}_{{\mathbf{Q}}}
. We let
V\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=T\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathbf{Q}_{p}
and
W\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=V/T
, which is a finite free
\mathbf{Q}_{p}/\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-representation of
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbf{Q}}
. We will assume that
V
is one of the representations that appeared in §
2.1
, i.e. a Galois representation attached to some arithmetic object, and take
M=W
.

Given the general definition of a Selmer structure and Selmer group, the next step is to give reasonable subspaces
\mathcal{L}_{v}
for
v\in\Sigma
. There are two main approaches to this.

Definition 13.19
.

(1)

(Greenberg’s approach) To define interesting Selmer structures at
p
, Greenberg’s approach assumes that the representation
V
is
p
-ordinary, in the sense that there exists a saturated, finite,
\mathscr{G}_{{\mathbf{Q}}}
-invariant filtration
\mathrm{Fil}^{i}
of
V
such that the inertia group
I_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
of
\mathscr{G}_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
acts on the
i
th graded piece
\mathrm{Fil}^{i}/\mathrm{Fil}^{i+1}
as the
i
th power of the cyclotomic character (from (
9.1
)). Let
\mathcal{F}={\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})^{+}
as before and let
v_{p}
be the unique place of
\mathcal{F}
above
p
. Then one defines

\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{Gr}}_{v_{p}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\ker\Big(H^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{v_{p}},W)\to H^{1}(I_{v_{p}},W)\to H^{1}(I_{v_{p}},W/\mathrm{Fil}^{1}W)\Big),

where
\mathrm{Fil}^{1}W
denotes the image of
\mathrm{Fil}^{1}V
under the natural map
V\to W
.

(2)

(Bloch–Kato’s approach) This approach uses technology coming from
p
-adic Hodge theory (see e.g.
[
Ber02
]
,
[
Ben22
]
), and we only mention it here for the sake of completeness. Letting
V
be as before, we define

H^{1}_{f}(\mathcal{F}_{v_{p}},V)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\ker\left(H^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{v_{p}},V)\to H^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{v_{p}},V\otimes\mathbf{B}_{\rm cris})\right),

where
\mathbf{B}_{\rm cris}
denotes Fontaine’s ring of crystalline periods. Then one defines

\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{BK}}_{v_{p}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{Im}\left(H^{1}_{f}(\mathcal{F}_{v_{p}},V)\to H^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{v_{p}},W)\right),

where the map is induced by the natural map
V\to W
.

Remark 13.20
.

Greenberg Selmer groups and Bloch–Kato Selmer groups coincide in some fundamental examples. For a general relation between them, we refer the reader to
[
Kat04b
]
and
[
PR95b
, §2.4.7]
.

13.5.2
The module
\mathscr{X}_{\infty}^{+}
via Selmer groups

We now reinterpret the space
\mathscr{X}_{\infty}^{+}
appearing in Theorem
13.8
in terms of a Greenberg Selmer group. We follow
[
Gre89
, §1]
. Recall
F_{\infty}={\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})
, and the cyclotomic character
\chi:\mathrm{Gal}(F_{\infty}/{\mathbf{Q}})\to\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. Note that
\mathrm{Gal}(F_{\infty}/{\mathbf{Q}})
is the quotient of
\mathscr{G}_{{\mathbf{Q}}}
by
\mathscr{G}_{F_{\infty}}
, so that we may consider
\chi
as a character of
\mathscr{G}_{{\mathbf{Q}}}
that is trivial on
\mathscr{G}_{F_{\infty}}
. For
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}
, we consider the representation
T_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{Z}_{p}(n)
, that is, the space
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
upon which
\mathscr{G}_{{\mathbf{Q}}}
acts by
\chi^{n}
(cf. Definition
12.16
). Let
V_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{Q}_{p}(n)
and
W_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=V_{n}/T_{n}=(\mathbf{Q}_{p}/\mathbf{Z}_{p})(n)
.

By the above remark,
\mathscr{G}_{F_{\infty}}
acts trivially on
W_{n}
, and we thus have

H^{1}(F_{\infty},W_{n})=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathscr{G}_{F_{\infty}},W_{n}).

As
\mathrm{Gal}(F_{\infty}/F_{\infty}^{+})=\{1,c\}\cong\{\pm 1\}
(with the non-trivial element given by complex conjugation) and
p
is odd, the inflation-restriction sequence gives

H^{1}(\mathcal{F},W_{n})=H^{1}(\mathscr{G}_{F_{\infty}},W_{n})^{\{\pm 1\}}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts},\{\pm 1\}}(\mathscr{G}_{F_{\infty}},W_{n}),

recalling
\mathcal{F}=F_{\infty}^{+}
and where the subscript
\{\pm 1\}
denotes homomorphisms equivariant for
\mathrm{Gal}(F_{\infty}/F_{\infty}^{+})
, acting on
\mathscr{G}_{F_{\infty}}
as in Remark
13.7
. We can thus describe the Greenberg Selmer group over
\mathcal{F}
in the simpler language of group homomorphisms, rather than Galois cohomology classes.

We can refine this via local/global conditions. To do so, let
\sigma\in H^{1}(\mathcal{F},W_{n})
. Then:

(1)

As
W_{n}
is abelian and
p
-power torsion, we find

H^{1}(\mathcal{F},W_{n})=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts},\{\pm 1\}}(\mathrm{Gal}(F_{\infty}^{\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{pro}-p}/F_{\infty}),W_{n}),

where
F_{\infty}^{\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{pro}-p}
is the maximal abelian pro-
p
extension of
F_{\infty}
.

(2)

(Finite primes away from
p
). Since the local condition at finite places
v\nmid p
is the unramified condition, we deduce that
\sigma
is unramified at all
v\nmid p
. Then
\sigma
descends to a homomorphism on
\mathscr{X}_{\infty}=\mathrm{Gal}(\mathscr{M}_{\infty}/F_{\infty})
, for
\mathscr{M}_{\infty}
the maximal abelian pro-
p
extension of
F_{\infty}
unramified outside
\mathfrak{p}
as in §
13.2
.

(3)

(The prime above
p
). At
p
, we have
\mathcal{F}_{v_{p}}=K_{\infty}^{+}=\mathbf{Q}_{p}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})^{+}
. The filtration required by Greenberg is given by

\mathrm{Fil}^{i}\mathbf{Q}_{p}(n)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbf{Q}_{p}(n)&\text{ if }i\leq n,\\
0&\text{ if }i>n.\end{array}\right.

From the definition, we see that

\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{Gr}}_{v_{p}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}H^{1}(K_{\infty}^{+},W_{n})&\text{ if }n\geq 1\\
H^{1}_{\mathrm{ur}}(K_{\infty}^{+},W_{n})&\text{ if }n\leq 0.\end{array}\right.

Thus if
n\geq 1
, the local condition at
p
is empty, while for
n\geq 0
the local condition at
p
means
\sigma
is also unramified at
p
, so it descends further to
\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}=\mathrm{Gal}(\mathscr{L}_{\infty}/F_{\infty})
(for notation as in §
13.2
). We conclude that

H^{1}_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{Gr}}}(F_{\infty},W_{n})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts},\{\pm 1\}}(\mathscr{X}_{\infty},W_{n})&\text{ if }n\geq 1\\
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts},\{\pm 1\}}(\mathscr{Y}_{\infty},W_{n})&\text{ if }n\leq 0.\end{array}\right.

(4)

(Equivariance for
\{\pm 1\}
). As
p
is odd, the action of
\mathrm{Gal}(F_{\infty}/F_{\infty}^{+})=\{1,c\}
yields a decomposition
\mathscr{X}_{\infty}=(\mathscr{X}_{\infty})^{c=1}\oplus(\mathscr{X}_{\infty})^{c=-1}
. There is a similar decomposition for
\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}
. Note also that
c
acts on
W_{n}
as
(-1)^{n}
. We see that if
n>0
, we have

H^{1}_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{Gr}}}(F_{\infty},W_{n})=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}((\mathscr{X}_{\infty})^{c=(-1)^{n}},W_{n}),

that is, we see the dual of
(\mathscr{X}_{\infty})^{c=1}
for even
n>0
, and of
(\mathscr{X}_{\infty})^{c=-1}
for odd
n>0
. Similarly for
n\leq 0
we have

H^{1}_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{Gr}}}(F_{\infty},W_{n})=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}((\mathscr{Y}_{\infty})^{c=(-1)^{n}},W_{n}).

As in
[
CS06
, p.6]
, the natural surjection
\mathscr{X}_{\infty}\to\mathscr{X}_{\infty}^{+}=\mathrm{Gal}(\mathscr{M}_{\infty}^{+}/F_{\infty}^{+})
induces an isomorphism
(\mathscr{X}_{\infty})^{c=1}\cong\mathscr{X}_{\infty}^{+}
(and similarly
(\mathscr{Y}_{\infty})^{c=1}\cong\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}^{+}
). Combining all of the above, we conclude that for even positive
n
, we have

H^{1}_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{Gr}}}(F_{\infty},W_{n})=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathscr{X}_{\infty}^{+},W_{n}),

(a twist of) the Pontryagin dual of the module
\mathscr{X}_{\infty}^{+}
appearing in Theorem
13.8
.

13.5.3
The Iwasawa–Greenberg Main Conjecture

Let
T,V
and
W
be representations as in §
13.5.1
. We also let
V^{\vee}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(V,\mathbf{Q}_{p}(1))
be the Tate dual of
V
. In particular
V
is ordinary at
p
. Inspired by the Iwasawa Main Conjecture and by an analogous conjecture of Mazur for ordinary elliptic curves, in
[
Gre89
]
, Greenberg described a Main Conjecture for
V
, which we now state.

Attached to
V
is an
L
-function
L(V,s)
, and an Euler factor at infinity
L_{\infty}(V,s)
(called the Gamma factor in
[
Gre89
]
). This involves a product of certain translates of the usual Gamma function
\Gamma(s)
and has no zeros. We let
r_{V}
denote the order of the pole of
L_{\infty}(V,s)
at
s=1
. For example, if
V=\mathbf{Q}_{p}(n)
, then
L(V,s)=\zeta(s-n)
and
L_{\infty}(V,s)=\pi^{-(s-n)/2}\Gamma((s-n)/2)
. The poles of the last function are
s=n,n-2,n-4,\ldots
.

Coates–Perrin-Riou conjectured in
[
CPR89
]
that there should exist a
p
-adic
L
-function for
V
, which in this context lies in the field of fractions of
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
. We comment more on this conjecture in Appendix
B.3
.

Conjecture 13.21

(Greenberg)
.

The following assertions hold.

(i)

H^{1}_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{Gr}}}(\mathcal{F},W)
has
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
-corank equal to
r_{V}
.

(ii)

If
r_{V}=r_{V^{\vee}}=0
, then the characteristic ideal of the Pontryagin dual of
H^{1}_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{Gr}}}(\mathcal{F},W)
(as a
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
-module) coincides with the ideal generated by the
p
-adic
L
-function of
V
.

Example 13.22
.

Suppose
n>0
is even; then above we showed
H^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F},W_{n})=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathscr{X}_{\infty}^{+},W_{n})
. This has Pontryagin dual
\mathscr{X}_{\infty}^{+}(-n)
, the space
\mathscr{X}_{\infty}^{+}
with
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
-action twisted by
\chi^{-n}
. In this case, the
p
-adic
L
-function in Greenberg’s conjecture is
\partial^{n}\zeta_{p}
, the
n
th twist of Kubota–Leopoldt, so we see that Greenberg’s conjecture is essentially a (twist of) Theorem
13.8
.

Remark 13.23
.

We described Theorem
13.8
as ‘the Main Conjecture for
V=\mathbf{Q}_{p}
’. Although they are equivalent, strictly speaking, the case
n=0
doesn’t fit into Greenberg’s picture. Indeed, the underlying assumption does not hold: here
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\vee}=\mathbf{Q}_{p}(1)
, so
L_{\infty}(\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\vee},s)=L_{\infty}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},1-s)
, hence
r_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{\vee}}=1
. In Greenberg’s terminology,
L(\mathbf{Q}_{p}(n),1)=\zeta(1-n)
and
L(\mathbf{Q}_{p}(n)^{\vee},1)=\zeta(n)
are both critical only when
n
is even and (strictly) positive, or
n
is odd and negative, so the case
n=0
is not covered.

Remark 13.24
.

There exist more general, even non-commutative, statements of the Main conjecture. We refer the interested reader to
[
Kat93
]
and
[
FK06
]
.

Appendix

Appendix A
Iwasawa’s
\mu
-invariant

We end these notes by giving a flavour of further topics in classical Iwasawa theory, introducing the
\mu
and
\lambda
-invariants of a
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension. In proving Iwasawa’s theorem on the
\mu
and
\lambda
-invariants, we develop techniques that can be used to show that the modules appearing in the exact sequence of Corollary
13.14
are finitely generated torsion modules over the Iwasawa algebra, a part of the general Iwasawa Main Conjecture (beyond the Vandiver case that we have already proved).

The following results will hold for an arbitrary
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension of number fields, although we will only prove them under some hypotheses that slightly simplify the proofs.

Definition A.1
.

Let
F
be a number field. A
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension
F_{\infty}
of
F
is a Galois extension such that
\mathrm{Gal}(F_{\infty}/F)\cong\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. If
F_{\infty}/F
is a
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension, we denote by
F_{n}
the subextension such that
\mathrm{Gal}(F_{n}/F)\cong{\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}}
.

Recall first that any number field has at least one
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension, the
cyclotomic
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension
. Indeed, by Galois theory
\mathrm{Gal}(F(\mu_{p^{\infty}})/F)
is an open subgroup of
\mathrm{Gal}({\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})/{\mathbf{Q}})\cong\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, and hence contains a maximal quotient isomorphic to
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
(specifically, the quotient by the finite torsion subgroup
\mu_{p-1}
). The corresponding field (under the fundamental theorem of Galois theory) is the cyclotomic
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension.

Example A.2
.

Let
F={\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p})
. Then
F_{\infty}={\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})
is the cyclotomic
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension of
F
, and

F_{n}={\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p^{n+1}}),

noting that earlier we denoted this field
F_{n+1}
. The cyclotomic
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension of
{\mathbf{Q}}
is the field
(F_{\infty})^{\mu_{p-1}}
in
F_{\infty}
fixed by the torsion subgroup
\mu_{p-1}\subset\mathrm{Gal}(F_{\infty}/{\mathbf{Q}})
.

Leopoldt’s conjecture
states that the number of independent
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extensions of a number field
F
is exactly
r_{2}+1
, where
r_{2}
is the number of complex embeddings of
F
. In particular, the conjecture predicts that any totally real number field possesses a unique
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension (the cyclotomic one). Whilst the conjecture remains open for general number fields, it is known in the case that
F
is an abelian extension of
{\mathbf{Q}}
or an abelian extension of an imaginary quadratic field (see
[
NSW99
, Theorem 10.3.16]
).

A.1
Iwasawa’s theorem

Let
F
be a number field,
F_{\infty}/F
a
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension,
\Gamma=\Gamma_{F}=\mathrm{Gal}(F_{\infty}/F)\cong\mathbf{Z}_{p}
and
\gamma_{0}
a topological generator of
\Gamma_{F}
. Using this choice of
\gamma_{0}
, we identify
\Lambda(\Gamma)
with
\Lambda\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
by sending
\gamma_{0}
to
T+1
(when
\gamma_{0}
is sent to
1
by the isomorphism
\Gamma\cong\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, this is simply the Mahler transform, but this identification holds for any
\gamma_{0}
). Let
\mathscr{L}_{n}
(resp.
\mathscr{L}_{\infty}
) be the maximal unramified abelian
p
-extension of
F_{n}
(resp. pro-
p
extension of
F_{\infty}
), and write

\mathscr{Y}_{F,n}=\mathscr{Y}_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{Gal}(\mathscr{L}_{n}/F_{n})=\mathrm{Cl}(F_{n})\otimes\mathbf{Z}_{p},

which is the
p
-Sylow subgroup of the ideal class group of
F_{n}
. Set

\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}=\mathscr{Y}_{F,\infty}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\varprojlim_{n}\mathscr{Y}_{F,n}.

Write
e_{n}=v_{p}(\#\mathscr{Y}_{n})
for the exponent of
p
in the class number of
F_{n}
. The following theorem is the main result we intend to show in this section.

Theorem A.3

(Iwasawa)
.

There exist integers
\lambda\geq 0
,
\mu\geq 0
,
\nu\geq 0
, and an integer
n_{0}
, such that, for all
n\geq n_{0}
, we have

e_{n}=\mu p^{n}+\lambda n+\nu.

Remark A.4
.

(1)

This is yet another typical example of the power of Iwasawa theory, in which we derive information at finite levels by considering all levels simultaneously.

There are two basic steps in the proof of Theorem
A.3
. We first show that the module
\mathscr{Y}_{F,\infty}
is a finitely generated torsion
\Lambda(\Gamma)
-module. Using the structure theorem of
\Lambda(\Gamma)
-modules (Theorem
13.1
), we study the situation at infinite level, and then we transfer the result back to finite level to get the result.

(2)

We will only describe the proof for the case where the extension
F_{\infty}/F
satisfies the following hypothesis: there is only one prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
F
above
p
, and it ramifies completely in
F_{\infty}
. The reduction of the general case to this case is not difficult, and is contained in
[
Was97
, §13]
. This assumption covers our cases of interest; in particular, it applies if
F={\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p^{m}})
or
F={\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p^{m}})^{+}
for some
m\geq 0
and
F_{\infty}/F
is the cyclotomic
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension.

A.1.1
First step

The first step of the proof of Theorem
A.3
consists in showing (Proposition
A.8
) that the module
\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}
is a finitely generated
\Lambda(\Gamma)
-module. Then Proposition
A.6
will allow us to recover each
\mathscr{Y}_{n}
from the whole tower
\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}
. We then use a variation of Nakayama’s lemma to conclude.

Since
\mathfrak{p}
is totally ramified in
F_{\infty}
, and
\mathscr{L}_{n}
is unramified over
F_{n}
, we deduce that
F_{n+1}\cap\mathscr{L}_{n}=F_{n}
and hence

\displaystyle\mathscr{Y}_{n}=\mathrm{Gal}(\mathscr{L}_{n}/F_{n})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Gal}(\mathscr{L}_{n}F_{n+1}/F_{n+1})

\displaystyle=\mathscr{Y}_{n+1}/\mathrm{Gal}(\mathscr{L}_{n+1}/\mathscr{L}_{n}F_{n+1}),

showing that
\mathscr{Y}_{n+1}
surjects onto
\mathscr{Y}_{n}
. The module
\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}
carries the natural Galois action of
\Lambda=\Lambda(\Gamma)
, and under the identification
\Lambda\cong\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
, the polynomial
1+T\in\Lambda
acts as
\gamma_{0}\in\Gamma
.

Let
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}
be a prime of
\mathscr{L}_{\infty}
above
\mathfrak{p}
, and write

I\subseteq G\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{Gal}(\mathscr{L}_{\infty}/F)

for its inertia group. Since
\mathscr{L}_{\infty}/F_{\infty}
is unramified, all of the inertia occurs in the subextension
F_{\infty}/F
. Accordingly
I\cap\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}=1
and since
F_{\infty}/F
is totally ramified at
\mathfrak{p}
, the inclusion
I\hookrightarrow G/\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}\cong\Gamma
is surjective, and hence bijective. We deduce that

G=I\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}=\Gamma\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}.

We’ve shown the following picture of extensions:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Y}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I\hskip 2.0pt\cong\hskip 2.0pt\Gamma\hskip 2.0pt\cong\hskip 2.0pt\mathbf{Z}_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G=I\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}}

Let
\sigma\in I
map to the topological generator
\gamma_{0}\in\Gamma
under the natural isomorphism
I\cong\Gamma
.

Proposition A.5
.

Let
G^{\prime}
be the closure of the commutator of
G
. Then

G^{\prime}=(\gamma_{0}-1)\cdot\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}=T\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}.

Proof.

Recall that we have a decomposition
G=\Gamma\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}
. Let
a=\alpha x,b=\beta y\in G
, where
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma
and
x,y\in\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}
. Using the definition of the
\Lambda(\Gamma)
structure of
\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}
, and the fact that
\Gamma
and
\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}
are abelian, we get that

\displaystyle aba^{-1}b^{-1}

\displaystyle=\alpha x\beta yx^{-1}\alpha^{-1}y^{-1}\beta^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\alpha x\alpha^{-1})(\alpha\beta y\beta^{-1}\alpha^{-1})(\alpha\beta x^{-1}\beta^{-1}\alpha^{-1})(\beta y^{-1}\beta^{-1})

\displaystyle=(x^{\alpha})^{1-\beta}(y^{\beta})^{\alpha-1}.

Setting
\beta=1
and
\alpha=\gamma_{0}
, we deduce that
(\gamma_{0}-1)\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}\subseteq G^{\prime}
. To see the other inclusion, write
\beta=\gamma_{0}^{c}
, where
c\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, so that
1-\beta=-\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}{c\choose n}(\gamma_{0}-1)^{n}=-\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}{c\choose n}T^{n}\in T\Lambda
and similarly for
\alpha-1
, which allows us to conclude.
∎

Recall that the
n
th power of the Frobenius operator on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
is given by
\varphi^{n}(T)=(1+T)^{p^{n}}-1
. Let
\varphi^{0}(T)=T
.

Proposition A.6
.

We have

\mathscr{Y}_{n}=\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}/\varphi^{n}(T).

Proof.

We treat first the case
n=0
. Since
\mathscr{L}_{0}
is the maximal unramified abelian
p
-extension of
F
and
\mathscr{L}_{\infty}/F
is a
p
-extension,
\mathscr{L}_{0}/F
is the maximal unramified abelian subextension of
\mathscr{L}_{\infty}
. In particular,
\mathscr{Y}_{0}=\mathrm{Gal}(\mathscr{L}_{0}/F)
is the quotient of
G
by the subgroup generated by the commutator
G^{\prime}
and by the inertia group
I
of
\mathfrak{p}
. By the above lemma and the decomposition
G=I\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathscr{Y}_{0}

\displaystyle=G/\langle G^{\prime},I\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}I/\langle(\gamma_{0}-1)\mathscr{Y}_{\infty},I\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}/(\gamma_{0}-1)\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}/T\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}.

For
n\geq 1
, we apply the arguments of the last paragraph, replacing
F
by
F_{n}
and
\gamma_{0}
by
\gamma_{0}^{p^{n}}
, so that
\sigma_{0}
becomes
\sigma_{0}^{p^{n}}
and
(\gamma_{0}-1)\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}
becomes

(\gamma_{0}^{p^{n}}-1)\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}=((1+T)^{p^{n}}-1)\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}=\varphi^{n}(T)\mathscr{Y}_{\infty},

which gives the result.
∎

We state next a variation of Nakayama’s lemma for testing when a
\Lambda
-module is finitely generated, whose standard proof is left as an exercise.

Lemma A.7

(Nakayama’s lemma;
[
Was97
, Lemma 13.16]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{Y}
be a compact
\Lambda
-module. Then
\mathscr{Y}
is finitely generated over
\Lambda
if and only if
\mathscr{Y}/(p,T)\mathscr{Y}
is finite. Moreover, if the image of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}
generates
\mathscr{Y}/(p,T)\mathscr{Y}
over
{\mathbf{Z}}
, then
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
generate
\mathscr{Y}
as a
\Lambda
-module. In particular, if
\mathscr{Y}/(p,T)\mathscr{Y}=0
, then
\mathscr{Y}=0
.

Applying this in our particular situation we obtain the following result.

Proposition A.8
.

\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}
is a finitely generated
\Lambda
-module.

Proof.

Since

\varphi(T)=(1+T)^{p}-1=\sum_{k=1}^{p}{p\choose k}T^{k}\in(p,T),

the module
\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}/(p,T)\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}
is a quotient of
\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}/\varphi(T)\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}=\mathscr{Y}_{1}=\mathrm{Cl}(F_{1})\otimes\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, the
p
-Sylow subgrop of
\mathrm{Cl}(F_{1}),
which is finite. Therefore, applying Lemma
A.7
, we conclude that
\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}
is a finitely generated
\Lambda
-module, as desired.
∎

A.1.2
Second step

Once we know that the module
\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}
is a finitely generated
\Lambda
-module, we can invoke the structure theorem for these modules (Theorem
13.1
) to get an exact sequence

0\to Q\to\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}\to\mathscr{A}\to R\to 0,

where
Q
and
R
are finite modules and where

\mathscr{A}=\Lambda^{r}\oplus\bigg(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}\Lambda/(p^{m_{i}})\bigg)\oplus\bigg(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{t}\Lambda/(f_{j}(T)^{k_{j}})\bigg).

for some integers
s,r,t\geq 0
,
m_{i},k_{j}\geq 1
and some distinguished polynomials
f_{j}(T)\in\Lambda
.

Recall that we want to calculate the size of
\mathscr{Y}_{n}=\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}/\varphi^{n}(T)
. The following lemma reduces the problem to calculating the size of
\mathscr{A}/\varphi^{n}(T)
.

Lemma A.9
.

There exists a constant
c
and an integer
n_{0}
such that, for all
n\geq n_{0}
,

|\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}/\varphi^{n}(T)|=p^{c}|\mathscr{A}/\varphi^{n}(T)|.

Proof.

The full proof of this lemma is given in
[
Was82
, Lem. 13.21]
; we give a sketch.

Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{n}(T)\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}/\varphi^{n}(T)\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{n}(T)\mathscr{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}/\varphi^{n}(T)\mathscr{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

By hypothesis, the kernel and cokernel of the middle vertical map are bounded. By elementary calculations and diagram chasing, one ends up showing that the kernel and the cokernel of the third vertical arrow stabilise for
n
large enough, which is what is needed to conclude the proof.
∎

We now proceed to calculate the size of the module
\mathscr{A}
.

Proposition A.10
.

Let

\mathscr{A}=\Lambda^{r}\oplus\bigg(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}\Lambda/(p^{m_{i}})\bigg)\oplus\bigg(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{t}\Lambda/(f_{j}(T)^{k_{j}})\bigg),

for some integers
s,r,t\geq 0
and
m_{i},k_{j}\geq 1
and some distinguished polynomials
f_{j}(T)\in\Lambda
, and write
m=\sum m_{i}
,
\ell=\sum k_{j}\,\mathrm{deg}(f_{j})
. Suppose
\mathscr{A}/\varphi^{n}(T)\mathscr{A}
is finite for all
n\geq 0
. Then
r=0
and there exist constants
n_{0}
and
c
such that, for all
n\geq n_{0}
,

|\mathscr{A}/\varphi^{n}(T)|=p^{mp^{n}+\ell n+c}.

Proof.

Step 1.
First we show
r=0
. Note that

\varphi^{n}(T)=(1+T)^{p^{n}}-1=T^{p^{n}}+\sum_{k=1}^{p^{n}-1}{p^{n}\choose k}T^{k}

is distinguished. We may thus apply the division algorithm from Weierstrass preparation (a
p
-adic analytic analogue of Euclid’s algorithm, see
[
BGR84
, §5.2.1]
). This implies that any
f\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]
can be written uniquely as
f(T)=q(T)\cdot((1+T)^{p^{n}}-1)+r(T)
, where
r(T)
is a polynomial of degree
\leq p^{n}-1
. We see

(A.1)

\Lambda/\varphi^{n}(T)=\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\![T]\!]/((1+T)^{p^{n}}-1)\cong\{r(T)\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[T]:\mathrm{deg}(r)\leq p^{n}-1\}

is infinite. Since
\mathscr{A}/\varphi^{n}(T)
is assumed to be finite, we deduce that
r=0
.

Step 2.
We now deal with the second summand. By Equation (
A.1
), for any
k\geq 0
we see
\Lambda/(p^{k},\varphi^{n}(T))
is the space of polynomials over
{\mathbf{Z}}/p^{k}{\mathbf{Z}}
of degree at most
p^{n}-1
, whence

|\Lambda/(p^{k},\varphi^{n}(T))|=p^{kp^{n}}.

We deduce from this that

\bigg|\bigg(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}\Lambda/(p^{m_{i}})\bigg)\Big/\varphi^{n}(T)\bigg|=p^{mp^{n}},

where
m=\sum_{i}m_{i}
.

Step 3.
Finally, we deal with the last (and most involved) summand. Let
g(T)\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}[T]
be a distinguished polynomial of degree
d
(that we don’t assume is irreducible, as we want to apply to
g=f_{j}^{k_{j}}
). Letting
V=\Lambda/(g(T))
, we want to compute the order of

V/\varphi^{n}(T)=\Lambda/(g,\varphi^{n}(T)).

We will show inductively that for sufficiently large
n
,
|V/\varphi^{n+1}(T)|=p^{d}|V/\varphi^{n}(T)|
.

As
g
is distinguished,
T^{d}\equiv p\cdot(\mathrm{poly})\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptg)
, where
(\mathrm{poly})
denotes some polynomial in
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[T]
. Further,
T^{k}\equiv p\cdot(\mathrm{poly})\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptg)
for all
k\geq d
. Let
n_{0}
be such that
p^{n_{0}}\geq d
. We will use the following lemma.

Lemma A.11
.

For any
n>n_{0}
, we have

\varphi^{n+1}(T)\cdot V=p\varphi^{n}(T)\cdot V.

Proof.

First take
k\geq n_{0}
, allowing
k=n_{0}
. As
\varphi^{k}(T)
is distinguished, we see that

\varphi^{k}(T)=T^{p^{k}}+p\,(\mathrm{poly})\equiv p\cdot(\mathrm{poly})\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptg),

as
p^{k}\geq d
. Write
\varphi^{k}(T)=pQ_{k}(T)\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptg)
, for
Q_{k}(T)\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[T]
.

We use the identity

(X^{p^{k+1}}-1)=(X^{p^{k}}-1)\cdot(X^{p^{k}(p-1)}+X^{p^{k}(p-2)}+\cdots+X^{p^{k}}+1).

Applying with
X=1+T
yields

\displaystyle\varphi^{k+1}(T)

\displaystyle=(1+T)^{p^{k+1}}-1

\displaystyle=((1+T)^{p^{k}}-1)\cdot\Big[(1+T)^{p^{k}(p-1)}+\cdots+(1+T)^{p^{k}}+1\Big]

\displaystyle=\varphi^{k}(T)\cdot\Big[(\varphi^{p^{k}}(T)+1)^{p-1}+\cdots+(\varphi^{k}(T)+1)+1\Big]

(A.2)

\displaystyle\equiv\varphi^{p^{k}}(T)\cdot\Big[\big(pQ_{k}(T)+1\big)^{p-1}+\cdots+\big(pQ_{k}(T)+1\big)+1\Big]\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptg).

Expanding the binomials, every term is divisible by
p
except the constant term 1 in each expression; but these sum to
p
. In particular, we deduce

\varphi^{k+1}(T)\equiv p\varphi^{k}(T)\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptg).

Applying this with
k=n_{0}
, we see that

pQ_{n_{0}+1}(T)\equiv\varphi^{n_{0}+1}(T)\equiv p\varphi^{n_{0}}(T)\equiv p^{2}Q_{n}(T)\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptg),

so
Q_{n_{0}+1}(T)\equiv 0\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp)
. Inductively we see
Q_{n}(T)\equiv 0\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp)
for all
n>n_{0}
.

Returning to the last line of (
A.2
), now take
k=n>n_{0}
. As
Q_{n}(T)=0\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptp)
, every term in each binomial expansion is now divisible by
p^{2}
, again except the constant terms, which sum to
p
. In particular, we deduce

\displaystyle\varphi^{n+1}(T)

\displaystyle\equiv\varphi^{n}(T)\cdot\big[p^{2}\cdot(\mathrm{poly})+p\big]

\displaystyle\equiv p\varphi^{n}(T)\cdot\big[p\cdot(\mathrm{poly})+1\big]\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptg).

The term
p\cdot(\mathrm{poly})+1
is a unit in
\Lambda
, so its reduction is a unit in
V=\Lambda/(g)
. We find

\varphi^{n+1}(T)\hskip 2.0pt(\mathrm{mod}\hskip 2.0ptg)\in p\varphi^{n}(T)\cdot V^{\times},

from which
\varphi^{n+1}(T)\cdot V=p\varphi^{n}(T)\cdot V
, completing the proof of the lemma.
∎

We now go back to the proof of the proposition. For any
n>n_{0}
, Lemma
A.11
implies that
\varphi^{n+1}(T)V\subset pV
, and

\displaystyle|V/\varphi^{n+1}(T)V|

\displaystyle=|V/pV|\cdot|pV/\varphi^{n+1}(T)V|

\displaystyle=|V/pV|\cdot|pV/p\varphi^{n}(T)V|.

Since
g(T)
is distinguished of degree
d
, we have

|V/pV|=|\Lambda/(p,g(T))|=|\Lambda/(p,T^{d})|=p^{d}.

Finally, we compute
|pV/p\varphi^{n+1}(T)V|
. Since
(g(T),p)=1
, multiplication by
p
is injective on
V
and hence

|pV/p\varphi^{n+1}(T)V|=|V/\varphi^{n+1}(T)V|.

Recall that
n_{0}
is fixed with
p^{n_{0}}\geq d
. Inducting on Lemma
A.11
, we find that, for any
n>n_{0}
, we have

\varphi^{n+1}(T)V=p^{n-n_{0}-1}\varphi^{n_{0}+1}(T)V.

Again by Weierstrass division, we know
V
is isomorphic to polynomials in
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[T]
of degree
\leq d-1
. This means

|V/\varphi^{n+1}(T)V|=p^{d(n-n_{0}-1)}|V/\varphi^{n_{0}+1}(T)V|.

Putting everything together, we deduce that

|V/\varphi^{n}(T)V|=p^{nd+c},

for some constant
c
and all
n>n_{0}
. Applying this to the third summand of
\mathscr{A}
, we get

\bigg|\bigg(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{t}\Lambda/(f_{j}(T)^{k_{j}})\bigg)\Big/\varphi^{n}(T)\bigg|=p^{\ell n+c},

for
n\geq n_{0}
, where
n_{0}
is such that
p^{n_{0}}\geq k_{j}\deg(f_{j})
for all
j
,
\ell=\sum_{j}k_{j}\deg(f_{j})
, and
c
is a constant. This finishes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Along the way, we have proven the following fact.

Corollary A.12
.

Let
\mathscr{Y}
be a finitely generated
\Lambda
-module. If
\mathscr{Y}/\varphi^{n}(T)\mathscr{Y}
is finite for all
n
, then
\mathscr{Y}
is torsion.

Proof.

If
\mathscr{A}
is as in the statement of Proposition
A.10
, then we showed that
r=0
in the structure theorem for
\mathscr{Y}
. This implies that
\mathscr{A}
is torsion; each element is annihilated by the characteristic ideal of
\mathscr{A}
. If
\mathscr{Y}
is any finitely generated
\Lambda
-module, then
\mathscr{Y}
is quasi-isomorphic to a module
\mathscr{A}
as before, and as
\mathscr{A}
is torsion, so is
\mathscr{Y}
.
∎

We can now complete the proof of Theorem
A.3
.

Proof of Theorem
A.3
.

Applying Lemma
A.9
and Proposition
A.10
, for
n\geq n_{0}
we get

|\mathscr{Y}_{n}|=|\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}/\varphi^{n}(T)\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}|=p^{c}|\mathscr{A}/(\varphi^{n}(T))|=p^{\mu p^{n}+\lambda n+\nu}.

This finishes the proof of the theorem.
∎

A.2
Some consequences of Iwasawa’s theorem

We have already seen one application of Iwasawa’s theorem (Corollary
13.16
) during the statement of the main conjecture. This stated that if one class number in a
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension is coprime to
p
, then so are all the others. We list here some further interesting applications.

Recall that if
A
is a finite abelian group, then

A[p]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{x\in A:px=0\}

denotes the subgroup of
p
-torsion elements and its
p
-rank
\mathrm{rk}_{p}(A)
is defined to be

\mathrm{rk}_{p}(A)=\dim_{\mathbf{F}_{p}}(A/pA)=\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbf{F}_{p}}(A[p]).

Equivalently, we can decompose
A
uniquely as a direct sum of cyclic groups of prime power order; then the rank at
p
is the number of direct summands of
p
-power order.

Corollary A.13
.

Let
F_{\infty}/F
be a
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension. Then
\mu=0
if and only if
\mathrm{rk}_{p}(\mathrm{Cl}(F_{n}))
is bounded independently of
n
.

Proof.

Recall that

\mathrm{Cl}(F_{n})\otimes\mathbf{Z}_{p}=\mathscr{Y}_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}/(\varphi^{n}(T)),

that
\mathscr{Y}_{\infty}=\varprojlim\mathscr{Y}_{n}
is quasi-isomorphic to a
\Lambda
-module
\mathscr{A}=\big(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}\Lambda/(p^{m_{i}})\big)\oplus\big(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{t}\Lambda/(g_{j}(T))\big)
for some integers
s,t\geq 0
,
m_{i}\geq 1
, and
g_{i}(T)\in\mathscr{O}_{L}[T]
distinguished polynomials, and that we have (cf. the proof of Proposition
A.10
) an exact sequence

0\to C_{n}\to\mathscr{Y}_{n}\to\mathscr{A}_{n}\to B_{n}\to 0,

where
\mathscr{A}_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathscr{A}/\varphi^{n}(T)
, with
|B_{n}|
and
|C_{n}|
bounded independently of
n
. It then suffices to show that
\mu=0
if and only if
\dim_{\mathbf{F}_{p}}(\mathscr{A}_{n}/p\mathscr{A}_{n})
is bounded independently of
n
.

We have

\mathscr{A}/p\mathscr{A}_{n}=\mathscr{A}/(p,\varphi^{n}(T))=\bigg(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}\Lambda/(p,\varphi^{n}(T))\bigg)\oplus\bigg(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{t}\Lambda/(p,g_{j}(T),\varphi^{n}(T))\bigg).

Take
n
large enough such that
p^{n}\geq\deg(g_{j})
for all
j
and recall that
g_{j}
and
\varphi^{n}(T)
are distinguished polynomials (in the sense that all but their leading coefficients are divisible by
p
). The above formula then equals

\bigg(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}\Lambda/(p,T^{p^{n}})\bigg)\oplus\bigg(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{t}\Lambda/(p,T^{\deg(g_{j})})\bigg)=({\mathbf{Z}}/p{\mathbf{Z}})^{sp^{n}+tg},

where
g=\sum\deg(g_{j})
. This shows that
\mathrm{rk}_{p}(\mathrm{Cl}(F_{n}))
is bounded independently of
n
if and only if
s=0
, i.e. if and only if
\mu=0
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Concerning Iwasawa’s invariants, we have the following results:

Theorem A.14

(Ferrero-Washington;
[
Was97
, §7.5]
)
.

If
F
is an abelian number field and
F_{\infty}/F
is the cyclotomic
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension of
F
, then
\mu=0
.

Finally, the following is an open conjecture of Greenberg (see
[
Gre76
]
).

Conjecture A.15

(Greenberg)
.

For any totally real field
F
, and any
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension
F_{\infty}/F
, we have
\mu=\lambda=0
. In other words, the values
\#\mathrm{Cl}(F_{n})
are bounded as
n\to+\infty
.

Appendix B
Iwasawa theory for modular forms

An interesting source of
L
-functions are those arising from automorphic forms, analytic functions on adelic groups that are symmetric for certain group actions. Dirichlet characters are algebraic automorphic forms for
\mathrm{GL}(1)
, so Parts I and II describe ‘Iwasawa theory for
\mathrm{GL}(1)
’. The next natural case, of
\mathrm{GL}(2)
, is that of
modular forms
(as explained in
[
Wei71
]
). It is natural to ask how much of the theory above has an analogue for modular forms. The short answer is
all of it
, though our understanding of this case is not fully complete.

B.1
Recapping
\mathrm{GL}(1)

In these notes we have described three different constructions of the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function
\zeta_{p}
. Recall
\Gamma^{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{Gal}({\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})^{+}/{\mathbf{Q}})\cong{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}}/\{\pm 1\}
, and that
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
is its Iwasawa algebra, with ring of fractions
Q(\Gamma^{+})
.

(1)

In Part I, we gave an
analytic
construction, a
p
-adic pseudo-measure
\zeta_{p}^{\mathrm{an}}\in Q(\Gamma^{+})
interpolating special values of the Riemann zeta function.

(2)

In §
10
, we gave an
arithmetic
construction, defining
\zeta_{p}^{\mathrm{arith}}
via the image under
\mathrm{Col}
of the family of cyclotomic units.

(3)

In §
13
we gave an
algebraic
construction. We described a torsion
\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
-module
\mathscr{X}_{\infty}^{+}
, with characteristic ideal
\zeta_{p}^{\mathrm{alg}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{Char}_{\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})}(\mathscr{X}_{\infty}^{+})\subset\Lambda(\Gamma^{+})
.

Theorem
10.15
says
\zeta_{p}^{\mathrm{an}}=\zeta_{p}^{\mathrm{arith}}
. The Iwasawa Main Conjecture says
\zeta_{p}^{\mathrm{alg}}=I(\Gamma^{+})\zeta_{p}^{\mathrm{an}}
.

B.2
Analogues for
\mathrm{GL}(2)

Ultimately, versions of all of the above theory are known for sufficiently nice modular forms. Let
f
be a cuspidal Hecke eigenform of weight
k+2
and level
\Gamma_{0}(N)
, with
p|N
, and let
L(f,s)
be its attached
L
-function. There are three ways of associating a
p
-adic
L
-function to
f
.

B.2.1
Analytic

In the
\mathrm{GL}(1)
story, the Kubota–Leopoldt
p
-adic
L
-function interpolated zeta values
L(\chi,-k)
for
k\geq 0
with
\chi(-1)(-1)^{k}=-1
. Such values are called ‘critical’. For a more general
L
-function
L(s)
, Deligne
[
Del79
, Def. 1.3]
gave an arithmetic characterisation
16
16

16

Precisely, Deligne asks that given a ‘motivic’
L
-function
L(M,s)
, then if
L_{\infty}(M,s)
denotes the ‘Euler factor at infinity’, then
s=j
is critical for
L(M,s)
if neither
L_{\infty}(M,s)
nor
L_{\infty}(M,1-s)
has a pole at
j
. For
\zeta(s)
, the factor at infinity is
\pi^{-s/2}\Gamma(s/2)
, and the
\Gamma
-function has poles at negative even integers. We deduce the critical values of
\zeta(s)
are exactly the negative odd integers (as seen by Kubota–Leopoldt) and positive even integers, which relate to the negative odd integers through the functional equation for
\zeta(s)
.
of which values
s
should be critical for
L(s)
. For the modular form
f
, his criterion says the critical values of
L(f,s)
are
L(f,\chi,j+1)
for
\chi
any Dirichlet character and
0\leq j\leq k
.

The analytic
p
-adic
L
-function is an element
L_{p}^{\mathrm{an}}(f)
in space of
p
-adic distributions
\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
which interpolates these critical values. In particular, we have the following.

Theorem B.1
.

Let
\alpha_{p}
denote the
U_{p}
eigenvalue of
f
. If
v_{p}(\alpha_{p})<k+1
, then there exists a unique locally analytic distribution
L_{p}^{\mathrm{an}}(f)\in\mathscr{D}^{\rm la}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
such that

–

L_{p}^{\mathrm{an}}(f)
has growth of order
v_{p}(\alpha_{p})
.

–

For all Dirichlet characters
\chi
of conductor
p^{n}
, and for all
0\leq j\leq k
, we have

\displaystyle L_{p}^{\mathrm{an}}(f,\overline{\chi},j+1)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}}\chi(x)x^{j}\cdot L_{p}^{\mathrm{an}}(f)

\displaystyle=-\alpha_{p}^{-n}\cdot\left(1-\chi(p)\frac{p^{j}}{\alpha_{p}}\right)\cdot\frac{G(\chi)\cdot j!\cdot p^{nj}}{(2\pi i)^{j+1}}\cdot\frac{L(f,\overline{\chi},j+1)}{\Omega_{f}^{\pm}}.

We see that
L_{p}^{\mathrm{an}}(f)
is, in this generality, only a locally analytic distribution (in the sense of §
3.7
). We note, however, that if
f
is
p
-ordinary (i.e. if
v_{p}(\alpha_{p})=0
) then the growth condition implies that
L_{p}^{\mathrm{an}}(f)
lies in the subspace of
p
-adic measures on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. This theorem was first proved in
[
MSD74
,
AV75
,
Vis76
]
.

If
\alpha_{p}\neq 0
, then we know that
v_{p}(\alpha_{p})\leq k+1
, but the above theorem does not handle the case
v_{p}(\alpha)=k+1
. Subsequent work of Pollack–Stevens
[
PS13
]
and Bellaïche
[
Bel12
]
means we have
p
-adic
L
-functions in this case too, though the statement is slightly different.

The case of
\alpha_{p}=0
is known as the
infinite slope
case. For certain particular cases, a construction of a
p
-adic
L
-function attached to elliptic curves of bad additive reduction (where
a_{p}=0
) can be found in
[
Del98
]
. For arbitrary modular forms, partial
p
-adic
L
-functions with good interpolation properties have been constructed in
[
RJ18
]
, using Kato’s Euler system and extending Perrin-Riou’s big logarithm maps (as discussed in §
10.5
).

B.2.2
Arithmetic

As we sketched in §
10.5
, the appropriate generalisation of the arithmetic construction goes through Galois representations and Euler systems. Attached to a modular form
f
, we have a Galois representation
V_{f}
, constructed by Deligne inside the étale cohomology of the modular curve, in which we can pick a Galois-stable integral lattice
T_{f}
. The arithmetic
p
-adic
L
-function is then given by the following deep theorem of Kato, proved in his magisterial paper
[
Kat04a
]
.

Theorem B.2

(Kato)
.

There exists an Euler system
\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{Kato}}(f)
attached to
T_{f}
.

In the yoga described in §
10.5
, we then consider the localisation of Kato’s Euler system at
p
, which we still denote by the same name, and obtain a class

\mathbf{z}_{\rm Kato}(f)\in H^{1}_{\rm Iw}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},V_{f}).

The arithmetic
p
-adic
L
-function is then the image of
\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{Kato}}(f)
under the Perrin-Riou big logarithm map :

\displaystyle\mathrm{Log}_{V_{f}}:\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbf{Q}_{p},V_{f})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathscr{D}^{\rm la}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{Kato}}(f)

\displaystyle\longmapsto L_{p}^{\mathrm{arith}}(f).

The second deep theorem of
[
Kat04a
]
is the following
explicit reciprocity law
.

Theorem B.3
.

There is an equality

L_{p}^{\mathrm{an}}(f)=L_{p}^{\mathrm{arith}}(f)\in\mathscr{D}^{\rm la}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}).

B.2.3
Algebraic

Recall that, in §
13.5
, we described how the Iwasawa Main Conjecture for
\mathrm{GL}(1)
(Theorem
13.8
) can be generalised via Selmer groups. If
f
is a
p
-
ordinary
modular form, that is, when the eigenvalue
\alpha_{p}
has
v_{p}(\alpha_{p})=0
, then its associated Galois representation
V_{f}
is
p
-ordinary in the sense of Definition
13.19
; analogously to that section, we obtain a Selmer group
H^{1}({\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p^{\infty}}),V_{f})
attached to
f
over the Iwasawa algebra
\Lambda(\Gamma)
of
\Gamma\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{Gal}({\mathbf{Q}}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})/{\mathbf{Q}})\cong\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. In
[
Kat04a
]
, Kato proved that this is a torsion
\Lambda
-module, and thus has a characteristic ideal

L_{p}^{\mathrm{alg}}(f)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{ch}_{\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})}(\mathscr{X}_{p^{\infty}}(V_{f})),

the
algebraic
p
-adic
L
-function of
f
.
When
f
is
p
-ordinary, the analytic/arithmetic
p
-adic
L
-function is actually a measure on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, and hence lives in the subspace
\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times})
.

Theorem B.4

(Iwasawa Main Conjecture for
f
)
.

Under some mild additional technical hypotheses, we have

L_{p}^{\mathrm{alg}}(f)=(L_{p}^{\mathrm{an}}(f))\subset\Lambda(\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}).

This is a theorem of Kato
[
Kat04a
]
and Skinner–Urban
[
SU14
]
.
There has since been much further work weakening the required hypotheses, including analogues for non-ordinary modular forms. For a description of up-to-date developments in this direction, see
[
FW
]
.

Remark B.5
.

The Iwasawa theory of modular forms has important applications to elliptic curves, and in particular to the BSD conjecture. An introduction to this is contained in Skinner’s 2018 Arizona Winter School lectures
[
Ski18
]
.

Remark B.6
.

The topics described above comprise the
cyclotomic
Iwasawa theory of modular forms. There is also a rich
anticyclotomic
Iwasawa theory, working over an auxiliary imaginary quadratic field, with similarly spectacular applications to BSD. This is described in
[
BD05
]
, and for a more recent overview, see
[
CGLS22
]
.

B.3
Further results

The three constructions above, and the equalities between them, are expected to go through in very wide generality, but there are very few cases in which the whole picture has been completed. We sketch this here. Suppose
V
is a Galois representation, arising from a motive
M
, and corresponding (at least conjecturally) under the Langlands correspondence to an automorphic representation
\pi
.

(1)

(Analytic)
. There should be a locally analytic
p
-adic distribution
L_{p}^{\mathrm{an}}(V)
which interpolates critical values of
L(V,s)
.
This analytic
p
-adic
L
-function is subject to precise conjectures of Coates–Perrin-Riou and Panchishkin
[
CPR89
,
Coa89
,
Pan94
]
.

All current techniques for proving such conjectures are automorphic; but this is already difficult, even assuming
p
-ordinarity. We illustrate this in the case of (regular algebraic, cuspidal,
p
-ordinary) automorphic representations of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbf{A}_{{\mathbf{Q}}})
.

–

The cases of
\mathrm{GL}(1)
and
\mathrm{GL}(2)
were described above.

–

The case of
\mathrm{GL}(3)
was only recently handled in
[
LW
]
. Constructions in the special case where
\pi
is a symmetric square lift were given (decades earlier) in
[
Sch88
,
Hid90
]
.

–

No general construction is known for any
n\geq 4
. The best known results are in further ‘degenerate’ cases where
\pi
really comes from a different group (e.g.
[
AG94
]
).

For other groups, there are also many results; e.g.
[
EHLS20
]
for unitary groups,
[
LPSZ21
,
Liu20
]
for Siegel modular forms, and
[
KMS00
,
Jan24
]
for
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n}
. The general picture remains, however, very fragmented.

We do not claim to give anything approaching a comprehensive list here. Indeed, we have only scratched the surface; there are also constructions for many other groups, with more general base fields, and without assuming ordinarity. We highlight mainly that there remain a vast number of open questions in the construction of analytic
p
-adic
L
-functions.

If one drops the cuspidality assumption, we know even less, with good results only for
\mathrm{GL}(2)
. Without the regular algebraic assumption, we know essentially nothing at all.

(2)

(Arithmetic)
. We also expect Euler systems, in the sense of
[
Rub00
]
, to exist in great generality, but known examples are scarcer still. Until relatively recently, Kato’s Euler system and the cyclotomic units were two of only three examples of Euler systems, the other being the system of
elliptic units
(though the system of
Heegner points
is closely related). The last decade, though, has seen an explosion of activity in the area. Recent important examples of Euler systems include Euler systems for products of two modular forms
[
LLZ14
]
, the
diagonal cycles
attached to triple products of modular forms
[
DR14
]
, and Euler systems for
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}

[
LSZ22
]
.

Where an Euler system exists, one can apply a Perrin-Riou logarithm map and extract an arithmetic
p
-adic
L
-function; but proving an explicit reciprocity law is harder still. Such reciprocity laws were studied in the Rankin–Selberg setting in
[
KLZ17
]
, for diagonal cycles in
[
BDR+22
]
, and for
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
in
[
LZ
]
. For a precise summary of the double- and triple-product settings, see
[
LR
, §B]
.

(3)

(Algebraic)
. There are Iwasawa Main Conjectures in wide generality, at least in ordinary settings, and there are many partial results towards these too. Whenever one has an Euler system with the equality
L_{p}^{\mathrm{an}}=L_{p}^{\mathrm{arith}}
, for example, one has that the corresponding Selmer group is torsion and the divisibility
L_{p}^{\mathrm{alg}}|(L_{p}^{\mathrm{an}})
.
